From Seiberg-Witten to Gromov: MCE and Singular Symplectic Forms
Yi-Jen Lee

Abstract
Motivated by various possible generalizations of Taubes’s S ​ W = G ​ r SW=Gr theorem [ T ] to Floer-theoretic setting, we prove certain variants of Taubes’s convergence theorem in [ T ] (the first part of his proof of S ​ W = G ​ r SW=Gr ). In place of the closed symplectic 4-manifold considered in [ T ] , this article considers non-compact manifolds with cylindrical ends, equipped with a self-dual harmonic 2-form with non-degenerate zeroes. This extends and simplifies some central technical ingredients of the author’s prior work in [ LT ] and [ KLT5 ] . Other expected applications include: extending the H ​ M = P ​ F ​ H HM=PFH theorem in [ LT ] and the H ​ M = H ​ F HM=HF theorem in [ KLT1 ] - [ KLT5 ] to TQFTs on both sides [ L1 ] ; definitions of large-perturbation Seiberg-Witten analogs of Heegaard Floer theory’s link Floer homologies and link cobordism invariants.



1
Introduction

In a series of ground breaking papers
[
T
]
, Taubes proved the equivalence of
the Seiberg-Witten invariant of closed symplectic 4-manifolds and a version
of Gromov invariant. The first article of the series, also technically
the most important of the four,
SW\Rightarrow Gr
, proves that by perturbing the Seiberg-Witten equations by a large multiple of the
symplectic form, the Seiberg-Witten solutions converge in a technical
sense to a union of
connected pseudo-holomorphic curves with weights.
As the first step to the ultimate goal of generalizing the equivalence
theorem to smooth 4-manifolds with
b^{2+}>0
, Taubes proved a
generalization of this convergence theorem in
[
Ts
]
. In the
language adopted in this paper, this is rephrased as follows.

Let
X
be a closed
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
4-manifold, and
{\mathbb{S}}_{X}={\mathbb{S}}={\mathbb{S}}^{+}\oplus{\mathbb{S}}^{-}
be its
associated spinor bundle. The Seiberg-Witten equations on
X
take
the following form:

\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2}F_{A}^{+}-\rho^{-1}(\Psi\Psi^{*})_{0}+\frac{i}{4}\mu^{+}=0,&\\
\mbox{\mbox{$\partial\mkern-7.5mu\mbox{/}\mkern 2.0mu$}}_{A}^{+}\Psi=0,&\end{cases}

(1.1)

where
A
is a connection on
\det{\mathbb{S}}^{+}
,
\Psi
is a section
on
{\mathbb{S}}^{+}
, and
\rho
,
\mbox{\mbox{$\partial\mkern-7.5mu\mbox{/}\mkern 2.0mu$}}_{A}^{+}\colon\thinspace{\mathbb{S}}^{+}\to{\mathbb{S}}^{-}
denote respectively the
Clifford multiplication and the Dirac operator. (We use the definition
given in Sections I.1.1-I.1.3 of
[
KM
]
). In the last term of the first
equation,
\mu
denotes a 2-form,
and
\mu^{+}
its self-dual part. This is often regarded as a
perturbation.

Theorem 1.1
.

[
Ts
]

Let
X
be a closed
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
4-manifold with
b^{2+}>0

as above, and let

\omega
be a self-dual harmonic 2-form on
X
vanishing
transversely along an embedded 1-submanifold. Take the perturbation
two form
\mu^{+}
in Equation (
1.1
) above to be

\mu^{+}_{r}=\frac{1}{2}r\omega+w^{+}_{r},

(1.2)

where
r>1
, and
w^{+}_{r}
is a smooth self-dual 2-form with

\|w_{r}^{+}\|_{C^{2}}
is bounded by an
r
-independent constant.
Let
\{(A_{r},\Psi_{r})\}_{r}
be a sequence of corresponding solutions, with
r\in\{r_{1},r_{2},\ldots,r_{n},\ldots\}
,
r_{n}\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
.
Then there exists a t-curve
\mathbf{C}
on
X-\omega^{-1}(0)
with
respect to the almost complex structure determined by
\omega
and
the metric on
X
, and a subsequence
of the gauge equivalence classes
\{[(A_{r},\Psi_{r})]\}_{r}
which t-converges to
\mathbf{C}
.

The terms ‘t-curves’ and ‘t-convergence’ above are respectively
catch-all phrases we coined for the kind of pseudo-holomorphic subvarieties
and the technical notion of convergence in Taubes’s
result
1
1

1

Admittedly, this is a poor choice of terminology. The
“t-convergence” is not “real” convergence in the usual sense:
The sequence
\{[(A_{r},\Psi_{r})]\}_{r}
does not converge in any
reasonable topological space.
. They will be defined in
§
2.1
and §
3.3
.

Remark 1.2
.

Only the
w_{r}^{+}=0
case was discussed in

[
Ts
]
. However, the arguments in
[
Ts
]
work to establish the
slightly more general statement in the preceding
theorem.

In the special case when
X=S^{1}\times Y
, where
Y
is a closed

\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
3-manifold with
b_{1}>0
, one may choose a harmonic
Morse-Novikov 1-form
\theta
on
Y
, and take
\omega=2(ds\wedge\theta)^{+}
on
X
. Here,
s
denotes the affine parameter of
S^{1}={\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}
. With
respect to the product metric,
\omega
is self-dual harmonic, and
the
S^{1}
-invariant Seiberg-Witten solutions on
X
correspond to
the Seiberg-Witten solutions on the 3-manifold
Y
. Let

\check{\theta}
denote the vector dual to
\theta
. The

S^{1}
-invariant t-curves are products of
S^{1}
with certain sets of
(weighted) trajectories of
\check{\theta}
on
Y
. We call the
latter ‘t-orbits’, with the precise definition given in §
2.1
. Thus, as a
corollary to the preceding theorem, an analogous statement for closed
3-manifolds also holds. (A more precise statement is given in
Proposition
3.15
below). For the purpose of this article however, we
need a slightly stronger notion of convergence, which we call “strong
t-convergence”. This will be defined in §
3.4
.

The 3-dimensional Seiberg-Witten
equation, arising from a dimensional reduction of the four dimensional
version, has the following general form:

{\mathfrak{F}}_{\mu}(B,\Phi):=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2}*F_{B}+\rho^{-1}(\Phi\Phi^{*})_{0}+\frac{i}{4}*\mu=0,&\\
\mbox{\mbox{$\partial\mkern-7.5mu\mbox{/}\mkern 2.0mu$}}_{B}\Phi=0,&\end{cases}

(1.3)

where
B

is a unitary connection on
\det\mathbb{S}
,
\mathbb{S}={\mathbb{S}}_{Y}

being the spinor-bundle associated to the given
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
structure,
and
\Phi
is a section of
\mathbb{S}
.
\rho
denotes the
Clifford action, and
\mbox{\mbox{$\partial\mkern-7.5mu\mbox{/}\mkern 2.0mu$}}_{B}
denotes the Dirac operator associated
to
B
.
(\Phi\Phi^{*})_{0}
denotes the traceless part of

\Phi\Phi^{*}
.
\mu
is a closed 2-form on
Y
. Two pairs
(B,\Phi)
,
(B^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime})
are said to be
gauge equivalent
if
there exists a
u\in C^{\infty}(Y,S^{1})
, such that
(B^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime})=(B-2u^{-1}du,u\cdot\Phi)
. Recall also that a closed 1-form
\nu

on a 3-manifold
Y
is said to be
Morse-Novikov
if
\nu
has
isolated, nondegenerate zeroes.

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
(Y,{\mathfrak{s}})
be a closed oriented
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
3-manifold with
b_{1}>0
, and

\theta
be a harmonic Morse-Novikov 1-form on
Y
.
Let
\nu=*\theta
and take
\mu=\mu_{r}=r\nu+w_{r}
, where

w_{r}
is a closed 2-form on
Y
, and
r>1
. Suppose that

\|w_{r}\|_{C^{2}}
is bounded by an
r
-independent constant. Let
\{(B_{r},\Phi_{r})\}_{r}
be a sequence of solutions to the
\mu=\mu_{r}
’s version of (
1.3
), with
r\in\{r_{1},r_{2},\ldots,r_{n},\ldots\}
,
r_{n}\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
.
Then there exists a t-orbit
\boldsymbol{\gamma}
on
Y-\theta^{-1}(0)
with
respect to the flow of
\check{\theta}
, the vector field dual to
\theta
, and a subsequence
of the gauge equivalence classes
\{[(B_{r},\Phi_{r})]\}_{r}
which
strongly t-converges to
\boldsymbol{\gamma}
.

Furthermore,

\int_{\tilde{\gamma}}\theta=\frac{1}{2}\big(c_{1}({\mathfrak{s}})\cdot[\theta]-\zeta_{\theta}\big)\geq 0,

(1.4)

where

\zeta_{\theta}
is a constant depending only on
\theta
(and not
the spin structure
{\mathfrak{s}}
).

An explicit formula for the constant

\zeta_{\theta}
in (
1.4
) is given in Proposition

3.15
.

See Section

3.4
for the proof of this theorem and terminologies in
its statement. The preceding theorem should be viewed as the first part of a (direct) proof of
a 3-dimensional variant of
SW=Gr
. In fact, the aforementioned
variant has a precise formulation as Conjecture 1.9 in
[
HL
]
. A
slightly weaker version of the latter conjecture is proved
(indirectly) by combining the results in
[
HL
,
MT
]
. A outline of
proof for Conjecture 1.9 in
[
HL
]
is given in Section 1.5 of
[
G
]
.

An explicit expression for the constant

\zeta_{\theta}
in (
1.4
) will be given in
Section
3.3
below.
Suffices to say for now that when
\theta=d\tilde{f}
for a
S^{1}
-valued Morse
function
\tilde{f}
,
\zeta_{\theta}
is the maximal Euler characteristic of the
regular fibers of
\tilde{f}\colon\thinspace Y\to S^{1}
.

1.1
Stating the main theorems

The goal of this article is to prove an analog of Taubes’s theorem (Theorem

1.1
above) for 4-manifolds with cylindrical
ends, equipped with a certain type of harmonic self-dual 2-form
\omega
.

Definition 1.4
.

A
manifold with cylindrical ends
(or “MCE” for short) is a
connected oriented
manifold
X
with a complete metric
g
, so that there is a
compact manifold-with-boundary
X_{c}\subset X
, and an isometry

{\mathfrak{i}}

from the closure of
X-X_{c}
to
\coprod_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}}{\mathbb{R}}^{\geq 0}\times Y_{i}
, where
Y_{i}

are closed connected manifolds and each half cylinder
{\mathbb{R}}^{+}\times Y_{i}

is endowed with the product metric. The index set,
{\mathfrak{Y}}={\mathfrak{Y}}_{X}
, is a non-empty
set of finitely many elements. In the rest of this article, we shall
sometimes implicitly identify subspaces in the closure of
X-X_{c}

and their image in
\coprod_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}}{\mathbb{R}}^{\geq 0}\times Y_{i}

under
{\mathfrak{i}}
.

We call the preimage (under
{\mathfrak{i}}
) of the half cylinder
{\mathbb{R}}^{\geq 0}\times Y_{i}
above an
end
of

X
, or the
Y_{i}
-end
when we wish to be specific. It will
be frequently denoted by
\hat{Y}_{i}
. Let

\text{{\small s}}_{i}\colon\thinspace{\mathbb{R}}^{\geq 0}\times Y_{i}\simeq\hat{Y}_{i}\subset X\,\to{\mathbb{R}}^{\geq 0}

denote the projection to the first factor. Denote

\text{{\small s}}_{i}^{-1}[l,\infty)\subset\hat{Y}_{i}
by

\hat{Y}_{i,l}
. Slightly abusing terminology, we sometimes also
call such
\hat{Y}_{i,l}
an end of
X
. We call each
Y_{i}
an
ending 3-manifold
of
X
. Unless specified otherwise, the ending
3-manifolds
Y_{i}
are oriented such that
{\mathfrak{i}}
is orientation preserving.

The analog of the self-dual
harmonic 2-form
\omega
has to satisfy appropriate asymptotic
conditions. Correspondingly, the Seiberg-Witten solutions
(A_{r},\Psi_{r})
need also satisfy certain asymptotic conditions.

Regular Morse-Novikov 1-forms are generic in an appropriate sense.

Definition 1.5
.

We call a Morse-Novikov closed 1-form
\theta
on a closed oriented
3-manifold
regular
if:

•

all
finite-length flow lines
\gamma
of
the vector field dual to
\theta
are
regular. Namely, either
\gamma
is a flow line from an index 2 zero

p\in\theta^{-1}(0)
to an index 1 zero
q\in\theta^{-1}(0)
, where the
descending manifold from
p
and ascending manifold of
q

intersect transversely at
\gamma
, or
\gamma
is a closed orbit with
nondegenerate linearized Poincaré return map;

•

for any fixed
l>0
, there are finitely many such flow lines

\gamma
with
\int_{\gamma}\theta\leq l
.

Definition 1.6
.

A smooth 2-form
\nu
on a MCE
X
is said to be
admissible
if it is a
nontrivial harmonic 2-form satisfying:

•

\nu
is asymptotic to a harmonic 2-form
v_{i}
on
Y_{i}

as
\text{{\small s}}_{i}\to\infty
on each end
\hat{Y_{i}}
.
By this we mean the following:
\tau_{-L}\nu
converges to

\pi_{2}^{*}\nu_{i}
on
[-1,1]\times Y_{i}
in the

C^{\infty}
-topology, where
\tau_{-L}
denotes translation by

-L\in{\mathbb{R}}
in the
{\mathbb{R}}
-factor of
\hat{Y}_{i}\simeq{\mathbb{R}}^{\geq 0}\times Y_{i}
, and
\pi_{2}
denotes projection to the

Y_{i}
-factor of
\hat{Y}_{i}\simeq{\mathbb{R}}^{\geq 0}\times Y_{i}
.
The
pair
(Y_{i},\nu_{i})
is called the
ending pair
associated to
\hat{Y}_{i}
.
The harmonic form
\theta_{i}:=*_{3}\nu_{i}
is either Morse-Novikov and regular, in which case the
corresponding end
\hat{Y}_{i}
is called a
Morse end
, or

\nu_{i}=0
, in which case the corresponding end is called a
vanishing end
. Let
{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}\subset{\mathfrak{Y}}
, and

{\mathfrak{Y}}_{v}:={\mathfrak{Y}}-{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}\subset{\mathfrak{Y}}
denote respectively the set of
Morse ends and the set of vanishing ends.

•

There are functions
{\bf l}_{v},{\bf l}_{v}^{+}\colon\thinspace{\mathfrak{Y}}_{v}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
,

{\bf l}_{v}(j)=:\textup{l}_{j}
,
{\bf l}^{+}_{v}(j)=:\textup{l}^{+}_{j}>\textup{l}_{j}+10
, so that
the restriction of
\nu
on

X^{\prime\prime}:=X-\bigcup_{j\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{v}}\hat{Y}_{j,\textup{l}^{+}_{j}}^{\circ}:\quad

vanishes transversely along a 1-submanifold
\nu^{-1}(0)
(possibly with
boundary).
(In the above,
\hat{Y}_{j,l}^{\circ}=\text{{\small s}}_{j}^{-1}(l,\infty)
denotes the
interior of
\hat{Y}_{j,l}
.)
Let
X^{\prime}:=X-\bigcup_{j\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{v}}\hat{Y}_{j,\textup{l}_{j}}^{\circ}
.
Without loss of generality, we take

\textup{l}_{j}^{+}=\textup{l}_{j}+10
for the rest of this article. We shall also encounter intermediate domains lying between
X^{\prime}
and

X^{\prime\prime}
. These are defined as follows: Given
a\in{\mathbb{R}}
, let

X^{{}^{\prime}a}:=X-\bigcup_{j\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{v}}\hat{Y}_{\textup{l}_{j}+a}^{\circ}.

In particular,
X^{\prime}\subset X^{{}^{\prime}a}\subset X^{\prime\prime}
when
0\leq a\leq 10
;
X^{\prime}=X^{{}^{\prime}0}
and

X^{\prime\prime}=X^{{}^{\prime}10}
. The value of
a
in
X^{{}^{\prime}a}
is left
unspecified in some statements in this article. In such case, the
statement holds for all value of
a
with
0\leq a\leq 10
, and
the precise value of
a
does not matter.

•

X
has at least one Morse end.

A
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
MCE
(X,{\mathfrak{s}})
together with an admissible form
\nu
on
it, denoted
(X,\nu)
, is called an
admissible pair
.

In the case when

X=\mathbb{R}\times Y
and
(X,\nu)
is invariant under the

\mathbb{R}
-action, the admissible pair is said to be
cylindrical
. In this case
X
has two ends,
[1,\infty)\times Y
and
(-\infty,-1]\times Y\simeq\mathbb{R}^{\geq 0}\times(-Y)
. We call the former the
+\infty
-end, and the latter the

-\infty
-end.

All pairs of MCE’s and 2-forms
\nu
,
(X,\nu)
are assumed to be
admissible in this article.

Remark 1.7
.

In Definition
1.4
’s terminology, the
ending 3-manifolds of a cylindrical
X={\mathbb{R}}\times Y
are
Y
and

-Y
.

Note that because the ending manifolds
Y_{i}
are compact,
\nu
decays
exponentially to
\nu_{i}
in the

C^{k}
-norm. (Cf. e.g. (
4.29
) for a more precise
statement.) Meanwhile, the second bullet in the
Definition
1.6
can be met with a generic choice of
(X,\nu)

and an appropriately chosen
{\bf l}_{v}
. Fix such a choice. The
condition also implies that over
X^{\prime\prime}

the restriction of
|\nabla\nu|
to
\nu^{-1}(0)
is bounded below
by a positive number.

The precise definition of
admissible
Seiberg-Witten solutions
associated to an admissible pair
(X,\nu)
and their
Y_{i}
-end
limits are given in §
3.1

below. It suffices to say for now that this is basically a finite
energy condition that prescribes the asymptotic behavior of the
solutions. For a Morse
end, the asymptotic conditions are specified by certain strongly t-convergent
Seiberg-Witten solutions associated to the ending pair.

On “vanishing ends”,
the asymptotic conditions are of the kind appearing in typical
Seiberg-Witten-Floer theories, as described in
[
KM
]
.

To ensure
transversality, an additional nonlocal perturbation term,

\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}
, is added to the left hand side of the Seiberg-Witten
equation in
[
KM
]
. For this purpose,
\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}
can be set
to be zero on the Morse ends, and may be taken to be arbitrarily small on the vanishing ends.
Let
{\mathbb{S}}={\mathbb{S}}_{X}={\mathbb{S}}^{+}\oplus{\mathbb{S}}^{-}
denote the spinor bundle associated to
the
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
structure on
X
. Given a
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
-connection on

{\mathbb{S}}
, let
A
denote the associated unitary connection on

\det{\mathbb{S}}^{+}
, and let
\mbox{\mbox{$\partial\mkern-7.5mu\mbox{/}\mkern 2.0mu$}}_{A}^{+}\colon\thinspace{\mathbb{S}}^{+}\to{\mathbb{S}}^{-}
denote the
associated Dirac operator. Let
\Psi\in\Gamma({\mathbb{S}}^{+})
.
The general Seiberg-Witten equation on
X
takes the form:

\mathfrak{S}_{\mu,\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}}\,(A,\Psi):=\Big(\frac{F_{A}^{+}}{2}-\rho^{-1}(\Psi\Psi^{*})_{0}+i\frac{\mu^{+}}{4},\mbox{\mbox{$\partial\mkern-7.5mu\mbox{/}\mkern 2.0mu$}}_{A}^{+}\Psi\Big)+\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}\,(A,\Psi),

(1.5)

where
\mu^{+}
denotes
the self-dual part of a 2-form
\mu
. (Cf. §
1.4
).
Note that
\mathfrak{S}_{\mu,\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}}\,(A,\Psi)
is invariant under
gauge transformations

(A,\Psi)\mapsto(A-2u^{-1}du,u\cdot\Psi)
, where
u
maps
X
to

S^{1}
. The gauge equivalence class of
(A,\Psi)
represented by

(A,\Psi)
is denoted by
[(A,\Psi)]
.

Theorem 1.8

(
local convergence).

Let
X
be a 4-dimensional
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
MCE and
\nu
be an
admissible 2-form on
X
.
Given
r\geq 1
, let
w_{r},\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}_{r}
be
respectively a closed 2-form and a nonlocal perturbation
of the type described in
[
KM
]
. Suppose they satisfy Assumption
3.4
below.
Let
\mu_{r}:=r\nu+w_{r}
, and write

\omega=2\nu^{+}
;
\mu_{r}^{+}=\frac{r}{2}\omega+w_{r}^{+}
.

Fix a
sequence of positive real numbers
\Gamma:=\{r_{n}\}_{n}
,
r_{n}\to\infty
as

n\to\infty
, and a corresponding sequence of admissible solutions

(A_{r},\Psi_{r})
to the Seiberg-Witten equation

\mathfrak{S}_{\mu_{r},\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}_{r}}(A_{r},\Psi_{r})=0
for

r\in\{r_{n}\}_{n}
. Suppose that the sequence
\{(A_{r},\Psi_{r})\}_{r\in\Gamma}
satisfies:

(0)

For each
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}
and
r
, The
Y_{i}
-end limit of

(A_{r},\Psi_{r})
, denoted
(B_{i,r},\Phi_{i,r})
, is
regular in the sense that the linearization of
{\mathfrak{F}}_{\mu_{i,r}}

from (
1.3
) is surjective. Here,
\mu_{i,r}
denotes the

Y_{i}
-end limit of
\mu_{r}
in the sense of
Section
1.4
(6). (Such a limit exists by the assumptions
on
\nu
and
w_{r}
.)

(1)

If
\hat{Y}_{i}
is a vanishing-end of
X
, then the
Y_{i}
-end limit
of
(A_{r},\Psi_{r})
, denoted
(B_{i},\Phi_{i})=(B_{i,r},\Phi_{i,r})
, is independent of
r
. If
\hat{Y}_{i}
is a
Morse-end of
X
, then the
Y_{i}
-end limit of
(A_{r},\Psi_{r})

strongly t-converges to a t-orbit
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{i}
as
r\to\infty
. The
Y_{i}
-end limits of
(A_{r},\Psi_{r})
are
regular in the sense that

(2)

\exists\,N\in{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
such that
\forall r>N
, the
relative homotopy classes of
[(A_{r},\Psi_{r})]
are identified
via the canonical isomorphisms in Lemma
3.19
.
Denote this class by
h
.

Then:

(a)

There is a
t-curve
\mathbf{C}
and an unbounded subsequence
\Gamma_{0}
of

\Gamma=\{r_{n}\}_{n}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
, such that
\{(A_{r},\Psi_{r})\}_{r\in\Gamma_{0}}

t-converges to
\mathbf{C}
over
X^{\prime}
.

(b)

Furthermore,

\mathbf{C}
has finite
\omega
-energy, which is bounded above by a positive constant determined by

h
(which depends on
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{i}
), the metric and
the
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
-structure of
X
,
\nu

and the constants
\varsigma_{w},\textsl{z}_{\mathfrak{p}}
in Assumption
3.4
.

Remark 1.9
.

(a) See §
3.5
for the definition of
relative homotopy classes of Seiberg-Witten solutions. The notion of

\omega
-energy of t-curves, as well as the
\nu
-energy
mentioned in the next theorem, are introduced in §
2.4
below. They are analogs of the notion of energies in symplectic field theory in §5.3 of

[
BEHWZ
]
.

(b) By Theorem
1.3
, the sequence
\{(A_{r},\Psi_{r})\}_{r\in\{r_{n}\}_{n}}
in the statement of the preceding theorem
exists only when
c_{1}({\mathfrak{s}}_{i})\cdot[*\nu_{i}]\geq-\zeta_{*\nu_{i}}
for
all Morse ends
\hat{Y}_{i}
.

For Floer-theoretic purposes, a stronger version of convergence result
is often desired. In heuristic terms, a gauge equivalence class
of Seiberg-Witten solutions on a 4-dimensional cobordism is regarded as
a (generalized) flow line. This type of results say that a
sequence of flow lines with the same end points and relative homotopy class
“weakly converge” to a “broken trajectory” that is a
concatenation of flow lines. (See Theorem 5.1.1 in
[
KM
]
for an
example.) The following theorem is intended
to play a similar role. The “broken trajectories” here in our
setting are the so-called “chains of t-curves”.

Theorem 1.10

(
global convergence).

Adopt the assumptions and notations in Theorem
1.8
; in
particular,
\{\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{i}\}_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}}
,
h
are as in
Conditions (1) and (2) in Theorem
1.8
respectively. Let

\mathfrak{s}={\mathfrak{s}}_{X}
denote the
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
structure of
X
. Then

(a)

There exists a chain of t-curves
\mathfrak{C}
in
X^{\prime}

with
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
structure
\mathfrak{s}
,

Y_{i}
-end limits
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{i}
for
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}
,
together with a
subsequence
\Gamma^{\prime}=\{r^{\prime}_{n}\}_{n}
of
\Gamma
,
r^{\prime}_{n}\to\infty
as

n\to\infty
, such that over
X^{\prime}
, the corresponding subsequence of Seiberg-Witten
solutions
\{(A_{r},\Psi_{r})\}_{r\in\Gamma^{\prime}}
weakly t-converges to

{\mathfrak{C}}
in the sense of Definition
2.12
.

(b)

{\mathfrak{C}}
has finite
\omega
-energy, which has
an upper bound determined by the same factors listed in Item (b) in the
statement of Theorem
1.8
.

(c)

Suppose in addition that:

b^{1}(Y_{i})=0\quad\forall i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{v},

(1.6)

and let
{\mathfrak{h}}
be the isomorphism introduced in Lemma

3.23
. (This is a map from the set of relative
homotopy classes of Seiberg-Witten gauge equivalence classes to the
set of relative homology classes of chains of t-curves.) Then

{\mathfrak{C}}\subset X^{\prime}
is of relative homology class
{\mathfrak{h}}\,(\text{{\scriptsize h}})
.

(d)

Assuming that
X
has no vanishing ends. Then the

\nu
-energy of
{\mathfrak{C}}
is determined by
\nu
, the

\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
-structure,
\{\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{i}\}_{i}
and
\operatorname{\text{{\scriptsize h}}}
via the explicit formula
(
7.36
).

See Definition
2.11
below for the definitions of chains of t-curves, as
well as their
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
structures and relative homology
classes.

Remark 1.11
.

As with typical Floer theories, constraints on the relative homotopy class (Condition (2) of
Theorem
1.8
in our setting; also assumed in Theorem

1.10
) may be replaced by the more general condition of
a uniform upper bound on a certain “topological energy”. Compare
e.g.
[
KM
]
’s Theorem 24.6.2 and Proposition 24.6.4. The
corresponding “energy-bound” condition in our setting takes the form
of:

r^{-1}{\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{\mu_{r},\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}_{r}}(X)(A_{r},\Psi_{r})\leq\textsc{e}\quad\text{for an $r$-independent constant $\textsc{e}$,}

(1.7)

where
{\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{\mu,\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}}
is a natural generalization of

[
KM
]
’s “perturbed topological energy”

{\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}}
.
(Cf. (
4.13
) below.) Fixing the relative homotopy class
h
as in Condition (2) of
Theorem
1.8
leads to a bound of the preceding form (with

\textsc{e}_{p}
depending on
h
); see Lemma
4.3

below. As explained in §
7.2
, such a
bound on the (Seiberg-Witten) topological energy is virtually
equivalent to a bound on the corresponding symplectic version of
energy, which is required in Gromov-type compactness theorems.
A special case of such a generalization is used in the proof of
HM=PFH
in
[
LT
]
.

1.2
Motivations and applications

This article is intended to provide some essential analytic foundations of
various speculated relations between Seiberg-Witten-Floer homologies
and
Gr
-type Floer homologies (such as Hutchings-Taubes’s
ECH
/
PFH

[
HT1
]
). More specifically, it began as a part of the author’s
program of proving the equivalence of Seiberg-Witten-Floer homology (
\mathring{HM}
)
and Heegaard Floer homology (
HF^{\circ}
)
[
L
]
; and the earliest incarnation of
the present paper dates back to 2006. Since then, several major progress
has been made
on the relation between the two types of Floer theories. In
particular, due to certain technical difficulties, the program in

[
L
]
has been since been modified to take advantage of Taubes’s
monumental work on the equivalence of
HM
and
ECH
in 2008

[
Te
]
. See
[
KLT1
]
,
[
KLT2
]
,
[
KLT3
]
,

[
KLT4
]
,
[
KLT5
]
. This renders the results stated here not immediately
relevant to our original goal (which partially accounts for its delay
in appearance). However, the aforementioned relation comes in many
forms, (some even lacking precise formulation), and is still very far
from being
completely understood. This article is expected to serve as technical
basis for possible results in this general direction. Here we briefly describe
some of them, as well as their background.

(a)
PFH
and TQFT

The definition of purported
Gr
analogs of
HM
is itself a difficult
subject. Certain variants, called Periodic Floer homology (
PFH
) and
Embedded Floer homology (
ECH
), are proposed by Hutchings, and shown to
be well-defined by Hutchings and Taubes
[
HT1
]
. The latter (
ECH
) is
associated to
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
3-manifolds equipped with a contact form. As
mentioned before, it is shown to be equivalent to the (unperturbed
version) of
HM
by Taubes. In fact, some of the expected properties of

ECH
are difficult to establish directly, and this relation with
the better-developed
HM
are used to justify them, resulting in
important consequences.
For example, the independence of
ECH
on
the contact form and the definition of cobordism
maps in
ECH
both hinge on their counterparts in
HM
, and these are
in turn the basis for the proofs of 3-dimensional Weinstein conjecture

[
Tw
]
, Arnolds’s conjecture
[
HT2
]
, and Hutchings’s

ECH
capacities
[
H
]
.

The closely related
PFH
pertains to a special case of the setting studied in this
paper. These are defined for 3-manifolds that are mapping tori, and
they are shown to be equivalent to (a perturbed version) of
HM
in
the author’s joint work with Taubes
[
LT
]
. Given Taubes’s prior
work on
HM=ECH
, the only new ingredient of this proof
consists of a special case of this article’s Theorem

1.8
, namely the case when
(X,\nu)=({\mathbb{R}}\times Y,\pi_{2}^{*}*_{3}\theta)
is cylindrical, and the harmonic 1-form
\theta
is nowhere
vanishing. One naturally expects that this equivalence extends to an
equivalence of the TQFT on both sides, and the theorems of this
article in that case when
X
is a symplectic cobordism would form
the main part of the proof. Unfortunately, like
ECH
, cobordism
maps for
PFH
is not at present (directly) defined. In
ECH
, it is defined
indirectly through its Seiberg-Witten counterparts by Taubes’s

HM=ECH
). A similar indirect definition of PFH
cobordism maps via the main theorems of this article is very likely
possible. Alternatively, for heuristic reasons one expects the
purported TQFT for
PFH
to be equivalent to Usher’s TQFT for what
he calls the “FCOB category”
[
U2
]
. based on
Donaldson’s result on the equivalence of (closed) symplectic
4-manifolds and Lefschetz pencils, Usher introduces in
[
U2
]
a
version of TQFT on what is called the “FCOB category” in

[
U2
]
. The latter
TQFT
is rigorously defined; and therefore one
has a precisely formulated conjecture:

Conjecture 1.12
.

[
U2
]

Usher’s TQFT is equivalent to the restriction of
(perturbed versions of)
Seiberg-Witten
TQFT
, restricted to the subcategory of
FCOB
.

The main results of this article are expected to play a key role in
a proof of this conjecture.

The aforementioned heuristic reasoning also leads to other variants of
this conjecture: In the context of closed symplectic manifolds, The
TQFTs of Usher’s and of
PFH
’s are respectively the counterparts of
Donaldson-Smith’s invariant
[
DS
]
for symplectic
Lefschetz pencils, and Taubes’s Gromov invariant for symplectic
4-manifolds. The equivalence of the two has been established by Usher

[
U1
]
. Hence by Taubes’s
SW_{4}=Gr

[
T
]
, the
Donaldson-Smith invariant is equivalent to the Seiberg-Witten
invariant for closed 4-manifolds. This is the counterpart of
Conjecture
1.12
above.

As
PFH
concerns the setting of Theorem

1.3
in the special case when the harmonic
1
-form has no
zeroes, in full generality Theorems

1.3
and
1.8
,
1.10
suggest that there
should be a generalized version of the
PFH
TQFT, which is defined for
a larger category containing
FCOB
as a subcategory, whose objects
consist of closed 3-manifolds with
b_{1}>0
(equipped with a
nondegenerate harmonic 1-form
\theta
), and whose morphisms are 4-dimensional
cobordisms with
b^{+}>0
, equipped with nondegenerate harmonic
self-dual 2-forms that appear as
\omega
in the setup of Theorems

1.8
,
1.10
. The closed 4-manifold counterpart of
this larger category consists of pairs
(X,\omega)
described in
the statement of Taubes’s Theorem
1.1
. Such manifolds are
called “near symplectic manifolds” by
Donaldson and some other authors. Donaldson’s description of symplectic manifolds
as Lefschetz pencils has been generalized to near-symplectic
4-manifolds
[
AKM
]
. This endows every near-symplectic manifold
with a “singular Lefschetz pencil”. Correspondingly, Perutz defined
a “Lagrangian-matching invariant” for singular Lefschetz fibrations

[
P
]
, which can be regarded as a generalization of
Donaldson-Smith’s invariant for symplectic Lefschetz invariants. A
TQFT version of this “Lagrangian-matching invariant” is briefly
outlined in Section 1 of
[
P
]
, and currently being developed by
Lekili and Perutz
[
LP
]
. This is supposed to be equivalent to the
putative “generalized
PFH
TQFT mentioned above, and Conjecture

1.12
has a corresponding generalization in terms of these
invariants.

A more modest project is to understand the relation between
HM

and
PFH
cobordism maps in special cases. Hutchings communicated to us that
PFH
cobordism maps
can be well-defined for some simple cobordisms equipped with Lefschetz
fibration structure. G.H. Chen is working on generalizing these and
establishing the equivalence of
HM
and
PFH
cobordism maps in
these special cases.
Also according Hutchings, one complication of directly
define
ECH
/
PFH
-cobordism maps is that, unlike the case of Floer
homologies, such maps would inevitably involve counting
(nontrivially-) broken trajectories (analogs of our “chains of
t-curves” consisting of more than one t-curves), even after
generic perturbation. We hope that by
exploring some explicit examples of Theorem
1.10
one may
gain some insight on how to correctly count holomorphic curves in
cobordisms for the definition of
ECH
/
PFH
-cobordism maps.

(b) Seiberg-Witten invariants and Heegaard Floer
invariants.

One common essential ingredient of both the proof of
HM=HF
in

[
KLT1
]
-
[
KLT5
]
and the original program
[
L
]
is a positivity result, exemplified by
Propositions 3.4 and 3.7 in
[
KLT5
]
. (A special case of this
result is also a major ingredient of
[
KLT4
]
). Their counterparts
in
[
L
]
are Claim (1) of its Section 6.4, and the last part of its
sketch of proof for Theorem 8.2. These type of positive results serve
the purpose of showing that the differential and cobordism maps
between Floer complexes preserve the filtration, ensuring that the
“filtered” Floer homologies and cobordism maps among them are
well-defined.

The following proposition is a by-product of the proof of Theorem

1.10
, given in Sections
7.3
,
7.4

below. The positivity results needed in
[
L
]
are special cases of
this proposition.

Proposition 1.13
.

Let
\{(A_{r},\Psi_{r})\}_{r\in\Gamma^{\prime}}
and
{\mathfrak{C}}
be respectively the sequence of
Seiberg-Witten solutions and the chain of t-curves from the conclusion of Theorem
1.10
.
Suppose that
P\subset\mathring{X}^{\prime}
is
a
pseudo-holomorphic submanifold with the following properties:

\hss\left\{\mkern-20.0mu\parbox{310.4979pt}{\begin{itemize}\par\itemize@item@$P$ is disjoint from $\nu^{-1}(0)$, and its distance to the
latter is bounded below by a positive number.
\par\end{itemize}}\right.

(1.8)

\hss\left\{\mkern-20.0mu\parbox{310.4979pt}{\begin{itemize}\par\itemize@item@On each Morse end $\hat{Y}_{i}$, $P$ is asymptotic to a union of
flow lines of the vector field dual to $*\nu_{i}$, denoted $\textsl{p}_{i}$,
in this sense of Definition \ref {def:asymp-cur}. ($\textsl{p}_{i}$ may be
empty.) For every $i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}$, $\textsl{p}_{i}$ is disjoint from all the rest
orbits of ${\mathfrak{C}}$.
\par\itemize@item@When $(X,\nu)$ is non-cylindrical: Write ${\mathfrak{C}}=\{{\bf C}_{0},\{{\mathfrak{C}}_{i}\}_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}}\}$. Then $P$ intersects $C_{0}$ transversely in a discrete set in the interior of $P$, and
for each ${\mathfrak{C}}_{i}$ with at least one component, ${\mathbb{R}}\times\textsl{p}_{i}$ intersects each component of ${\mathfrak{C}}_{i}$
transversely in a discrete subset in the interior of
${\mathbb{R}}\times\textsl{p}_{i}$. When $(X,\nu)$ is cylindrical: Let
$\textsl{p}_{\pm}$ denote the limit of $P$ in the $\pm\infty$-end. Then
every component of ${\mathfrak{C}}$ intersects all three of $P$,
${\mathbb{R}}\times\textsl{p}_{\pm}$ transversely in discrete points in the interior; and for every
rest orbit $\boldsymbol{\gamma}$ of ${\mathfrak{C}}$, $P$ intersects
${\mathbb{R}}\times\boldsymbol{\gamma}$ transversely in discrete points in the
interior.
\par\end{itemize}}\right.

(
1.8
)

Then

\lim_{r\to\infty}\int_{P}\frac{i}{2\pi}F_{A_{r}^{E}}
exists and is a
non-negative integer. (See §
3.14
for the definition of

A^{E}
). Moreover, if
{\mathfrak{Y}}_{v}=\emptyset
, this limit is determined
by the relative homotopy class
h
(which depends
implicitly on
\nu
and
\{\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{i}\}_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}}
) and
the (relative) homology class of
P
relative to
\{\textsl{p}_{i}\}_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}}
. In the above, two pseudo-holomorphic subvarieties
P,P^{\prime}

are said to be homologous relative to
\{\textsl{p}_{i}\}_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}}
if they
both satisfy (
1.8
) with the same
\{\textsl{p}_{i}\}_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}}
, and

P-P^{\prime}
bounds a chain with closed support (cf. the footnote in
Section
2.3
).

See Definition
2.11
for terminology and notation (e.g. rest
orbits, components) in the preceding statement.

In fact, with this proposition in place,
[
KLT5
]
can be
significantly shortened. This will be explained in more detail in the
next subsection. Its generality also renders the definition of
filtered Seiberg-Witten counterparts of some of the extensions of

HF^{\circ}
immediate. For example, the knot
Floer homology
HFK^{\circ}(Y,K)
in
[
OS
]
has a
HMT^{\circ}

counterpart
HMT^{\circ}(Y,K)
by a straightforward translation of Ozsvath-Szabo’s
construction to the large-perturbation Seiberg-Witten theory according
to the prescription of Section 6 of
[
L
]
, by introduction to a second
filtration to
CMT^{\circ}
associated to
\underline{\gamma}_{w}
, the
1-cycle in
\underline{Y}
corresponding to the second base point
w
in

[
OS
]
. Modifying
HMT^{\circ}(Y,K)
in the same way as

[
KLT1
]
-
[
KLT5
]
, one also has an extension of the latter’s

H^{\circ}
to knots. Theorem 1.1
of
[
KLT5
]
states that
H^{\circ}
is isomorphic to
HMT^{\circ}

tensored with copies of
H_{*}(S^{1};{\mathbb{Z}})
. The aforementioned
extension of
H^{\circ}
is related to
HMT^{\circ}(Y,K)
in the same
manner.

1.3
Relation to existing literature

The main theorems of this article may also be viewed as an extension
of a counterpart of

[
HT3
]
’s Proposition 5.2, which partially generalizes the
SW\Rightarrow Gr

part of Taubes’s
HM=ECH
proof in
[
Te
]
. In the latter,

{\mathbb{R}}
-invariant large-perturbation Seiberg-Witten equations on
{\mathbb{R}}\times Y
are
considered, with the role of
*_{3}\nu
here played by a contact form. This is
extended to the case of 4-dimensional exact symplectic cobordisms in

[
HT3
]
. The generalization is straightforward, as it does not
require new techniques.
Taubes’s equivalence theorem has a sister version in
[
LT
]
, which
asserts that (a different variant of)
HM
is isomorphic to

PFH
. Much of the proof for this theorem is similar to that in

[
Te
]
, except for the
SW\Rightarrow Gr
part. In both settings, a
crucial “energy bound” is required. In
[
Te
]
, this
hinges on certain spectral flow estimates, while in
[
LT
]
, a more
topological argument is used, basing on
harmonicity of
\nu
. The latter is also the strategy of this
article; see Section
4
. In fact, what was proved in
[
LT
]
is the special case of our
Theorem
1.8
when
X
is cylindrical and
\nu
is
nowhere-vanishing. Thus, one may expect that, like what happened in
the
ECH
case (Proposition 5.2 of
[
HT3
]
), the results of this article can
also be obtained by simple modifications of the work in
[
LT
]
. This
unfortunately turns out
not
to be the case.

In the case of

[
HT3
]
, the contact condition makes it possible to choose the
perturbation form on
X
so that it is identical with that in the
cylindrical case of
[
Te
]
. In the setting of this article, the
condition that
\nu
be harmonic is rather rigid, and the behavior of
the perturbation form on the ends of
X
is determined by that on
its compact piece,
X_{c}
. In general, it does not agree with
the cylindrical case, but only asymptotic to it. Surprisingly, this
small difference turns out to be a major obstacle, making the desired results
substantially more elusive. See remarks near the end of Section

4.1
and in the beginning of Section
4.4
.

Another complication stems from the existence of zero locus of

\nu
. In
[
Ts
]
, the harmonic form
\omega
is also allowed to vanish along a
1-dimensional submanifold of
X
. While part of
the ingredients in our proof can be obtained from
straightforward adaptation of their counterparts in
[
Ts
]
, the
same existence of zero locus causes additional
problems in our setting. In
[
Ts
]
, much of what was done in

[
T
]
easily carries over if one restricts attention to
complement of small tubular neighborhoods of
\omega^{-1}(0)
. This
is no longer possible in our setting,
since several important steps here rely on global estimates where
contribution from tubular neighborhood of
\nu^{-1}(0)
can not be
ignored. Lack of knowledge on the behavior of Seiberg-Witten
solutions on this region thus becomes an unavoidable problem.

The purpose of
[
KLT1
]
-
[
KLT5
]
’s modification to the original program in

[
L
]
is precisely to sidestep the aforementioned issues. In the
particular setting of
[
L
]
’s program, the zero locus in the
cylindrical
{\mathbb{R}}\times M
is explicitly known. Thus, one may
perform certain surgery operation along the zero locus of
*_{3}\nu
,
and “perturb”
\nu
near the surgered part so that
*_{3}\nu
approximates
certain standard contact 3-form. This makes
\nu
nowhere-vanishing,
and also allows one to take advantage Taubes’ proof of
HM=ECH
,
which was not available when
[
L
]
was written.

The major part of
[
KLT5
]
is dedicated to proving a
variant of Proposition
1.13
for the connecting sum
cobordisms. (Cf. Propositions 3.9-3.14 in
[
KLT5
]
). Here one also
encounter the first issue above, arising from the asymptotic
behavior of the perturbation form. Because of this problem, the
results there only applies to very special types of manifolds equipped
with rather stringent geometric conditions,
essentially only the specific cobordisms used in the article. For example, the perturbation form there is required to be

{\mathbb{R}}
-invariant on the ends (cf. e.g. (2.12) of
[
KLT5
]
), and
the pseudo-holomorphic submanifold
P
(in Proposition

1.13
’s notation) is a cylinder, near which the metric
is “standard”. As mentioned before, this does not
hold in most cases. Therefore the entire Section 9 therein (about 60
pages) is
dedicated to constructing such metrics and forms case by case for the specific
cobordisms needed in
[
KLT5
]
. Except for its Proposition 3.13,
where
X={\mathbb{R}}\times Y
and the perturbation form is one that
interpolates the harmonic version of
[
L
]
and the modified version
of
[
KLT1
]
-
[
KLT5
]
, all other positivity results in

[
KLT5
]
are special cases of Proposition
1.13

above, and its Proposition 3.13 says that as far as
[
KLT5
]
part
of the program is
concerned, it makes no difference to either work along the original
plan in
[
L
]
and or its modified form in
[
KLT1
]
-
[
KLT5
]
.

Alternatively, an
avatar (2006-2008) of the present article also assumed that the
perturbation form is constant on the ends, and this is made
permissible by only requiring the form
\nu
to be Lipshitz, not
necessarily smooth. (The assumptions of
[
KM
]
is sufficiently
general
to apply in this case). In this way, the proof therein was no more
difficult than that in
[
LT
]
. The trade off is that one would have
to deal with Lipshitz almost complex structures and Lipshitz
holomorphic curves.

One should also note that in
[
KM
]
, the perturbations used to
define the differentials in
HM
and the cobordism maps are also
assumed to be constant on the ends. Though this is in general not the
case when applying our theorem, we believe that the results in

[
KM
]
may be easily generalized to our setting, as our
perturbations decay to constant ones on the ends. One should be able
to do so by either going through the arguments in
[
KM
]
, or use a
direct limit argument.

1.4
Convention, notations, etc.

Here is a list of notations and conventions frequently
used throughout this article.

1.

W^{\circ}
or
\mathring{W}
denotes the interior of a
topological space
W
.

2.

p.d.
stands for “Poincaré dual”.

3.

Given an interval
I\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{\geq 0}
, we use
\hat{Y}_{i,I}
to denote the subset
\text{{\small s}}_{i}^{-1}I
in the end
\hat{Y}_{i}
,
and
\hat{Y}_{i,L}:=\hat{Y}_{i,[L,\infty)}
. The latter should
be contrasted with

Y_{i:L}:=\text{{\small s}}_{i}^{-1}(L).

We shall often omit
the index
i
when the statement refers to any of
the ends of
X
. The interval
I
above is allowed to be either
open or closed, or of the mixed type
[L,L^{\prime})
or
(L,L^{\prime}]
. We use
\hat{Y}_{i,[L,\infty]}\simeq[L,\infty]\times Y_{i}\supset[L,\infty)\times Y_{i}\simeq\hat{Y}_{i,L}
to denote the natural compactification of

\hat{Y}_{i,L}
by adding a
Y_{i:\infty}\simeq Y_{i}
at
infinity. Correspondingly,
\overline{X}=X_{c}\cup\bigcup_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}}\hat{Y}_{i,[L,\infty]}\supset X
denotes the compactification of
X
by adding

\bigcup_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}}Y_{i:\infty}
at infinity. Given a subspace
M\subset\overline{X}
,
\overline{M}
denotes its closure in

\overline{X}
.

4.

\pi_{2}\colon\thinspace I\times Y\to Y
denotes the projection to the
second factor of the product.

5.

Given a bundle
{\rm V}

over
M
,
C^{k}_{A}(M;{\rm V})
,
L^{p}_{k,A}(M;{\rm V})
respectively denote the spaces of

C^{k}
- and
L^{p}_{k}
-sections associated to a connection
A
on

{\rm V}
. Occasionally
M
,
V
, or the connection
A
is
omitted from the notation when it is clear from the
context. The bundles appearing in this article are typically
constructed out of
T^{*}M
and/or the spinor bundle over

M
. When the connection
A
is omitted from the notation, e.g.

L^{p}_{k}(M)
, the implicit connection
A
that induced from the Levi-Civita connection on

T^{*}M
, and/or the (previously specified) reference connection

A_{0}
on the spinor bundle.

6.

Given a bundle
{\rm V}
over
\overline{X}
with

{\rm V}_{i}:={\rm V}\big|_{Y_{i:\infty}}
, a section
q\in\Gamma({\rm V}\big|_{X})
is said to have
q_{i}\in\Gamma({\rm V}_{i})
as a
Y_{i}
-end limit
if

\|q-\pi_{2}^{*}q_{i}\|_{C^{2}(\hat{Y}_{l})}+\|q-\pi_{2}^{*}q_{i}\|_{L^{2}_{1}(\hat{Y}_{l})}\to 0\quad\text{as $l\to\infty$.}

7.

Given a function
\mathbf{L}\colon\thinspace{\mathfrak{Y}}\to[0,\infty]
with

{\bf L}(i)=L_{i}
, let
X_{\mathbf{L}}\subset\overline{X}
denote the subset

\{x\,|\,\text{{\small s}}_{i}(x)\leq L_{i}\,\forall i,\,\text{wherever
$\text{{\small s}}_{i}$ is defined}\}
. Given two functions
{\bf l},\mathbf{L}\colon\thinspace{\mathfrak{Y}}\to[0,\infty]
We write

\mathbf{l}<\mathbf{L}
if
X_{\mathbf{l}}\subset X_{\mathbf{L}}^{\circ}
, and we write
\mathbf{l}\leq\mathbf{L}
if
X_{\mathbf{l}}\subset X_{\mathbf{L}}
. Also, let
\hat{Y}_{[{\bf l},{\bf L}]}:=\bigcup_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}}\hat{Y}_{i,[l_{i},L_{i}]}
.

8.

The notation
X_{\bullet}
will be used to denote any of the

X_{\bf L}
,
\hat{Y}_{i,I}
above, or their interiors. Given
X_{\bullet}\subset\bar{X}
,
\overline{X_{\bullet}}
denotes its closure in

\bar{X}
. The
length
of

X_{\bullet}
, denoted by
|X_{\bullet}|
, is given by:

|X_{\bullet}|:=|\overline{X_{\bullet}}|\in[0,\infty]
and

|X_{\bullet}|:=\begin{cases}|I|=L-l&\text{when $X_{\bullet}=\hat{Y}_{I}$, and $I=[l,L]$.}\\
1+\sum_{i}L_{i}&\text{when $X_{\bullet}=X_{\bf L}$, ${\bf L}(i)=:L_{i}$.}\end{cases}

9.

Given an admissible pair
(X,\nu)
, let
X_{\delta}\subset X
denote the subspace of
X
consisting of
points whose distance to
\nu^{-1}(0)
is greater than
\delta
, and let
Z_{\delta}:=X-X_{\delta}\subset X
. Given

X_{\bullet}\subset\overline{X}
,
X_{\bullet,\delta}:=X_{\delta}\cap X_{\bullet}
and
Z_{\bullet,\delta}:=Z_{\delta}\cap X_{\bullet}
. For a
closed 3-manifold
Y
and a 1-form
\theta
over
Y
,

Y_{\delta}\subset Y
is similarly defined.

10.

\tau_{L}\colon\thinspace[l,\infty)\times Y\to[l+L,\infty)\times Y

denotes translation in the first factor. Slightly abusing the
notation, we also sometimes write
\tau_{-L}^{*}
as

\tau_{L}
. I.e., given a function or section
\xi(s,p)
on a half cylinder

\hat{Y}\simeq{\mathbb{R}}^{\geq 0}\times Y
and
L\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
(\tau_{L}\xi)(s,p):=\xi(s-L,p)
on
[L,\infty)\times Y
.

11.

Throughout this article, a “
k
-dimensional current”

\tilde{C}
on an

n
-manifold
X
refers to a linear functional on the space of
compactly-supported smooth
(n-k)
-forms on
X
, where the latter space
is endowed with the topology defined by the sup-norm. The

convergence
of currents refers to the convergence in weak
∗

topology. Given a smooth
(n-k)
-form
\nu
, we sometimes use

\int_{\tilde{C}}\nu
or
\langle\tilde{C},\nu\rangle
to denote the value of
\tilde{C}
on

\nu
.

12.

A (
k
-dimensional)
subvariety

C
of a manifold
X
is the image of an immersion

u_{0}\colon\thinspace C_{0}\to X
, where
C_{0}
is a (
k
-dimensional)
manifold (possibly disconnected or empty) containing a
countable set
\Lambda\subset C_{0}
such that

u_{0}\Big|_{C_{0}-\Lambda}
is an embedding.
The immersion
u_{0}
is said to be a
representing map
of

C
.
Given a
k
-form
\vartheta
on

X
,
\int_{C}\vartheta:=\int_{C_{0}}u_{0}^{*}\vartheta
. The
current associated to
C
, often
denoted
\tilde{C}
, refers to that given by
\vartheta\mapsto\int_{\tilde{C}}\vartheta:=\int_{C}\vartheta
. An
irreducible component
of
C
is the closure of a component of

C-u_{0}(\Lambda)
.
C
is
irreducible
if it has only one
irreducible component.

13.

A
weighted subvariety

{\bf C}
in a manifold
X
with

underlying subvariety

C
is a set of pairs
\{(C_{a},m_{a})\}_{a}
, where

C_{a}
are the irreducible components of
C
, and
m_{a}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}
is the
weight
associated to the irreducible component
C_{a}
. The
current
associated to
{\bf C}
, denoted

\tilde{C}
, is
\sum_{a}m_{a}\tilde{C}_{a}
. Since the underlying subvariety
C

and its associated current
\tilde{C}
together determine the
weighted subvariety
{\bf C}
, it is alternatively denoted as a pair

{\bf C}=[C,\tilde{C}]
.

14.

Let
{\bf C}=\{(C_{a},m_{a})\}_{a}
be an oriented weighted
subvariety, and
P
be an oriented submanifold in the
oriented manifold
X
. Let
u_{0}\colon\thinspace C_{0}\to X
be the immersion representing the
underlying subvariety
C=\bigcup_{a}C_{a}
of
{\bf C}
. Write

C_{0}=\coprod_{a}C^{\prime}_{a}
, where
C^{\prime}_{a}
is the connected
component which is the preimage of
C_{a}
under
u_{0}
. Then
P

intersects
{\bf C}

transversely
if
u_{0}
intersects
P\subset X
transversely. The
intersection number

\#\,(P\cap{\bf C}):=\sum_{a}m_{a}\,\chi\big((u_{0}|_{C^{\prime}_{a}})^{-1}P\big)
, where
\chi
denotes the Euler characteristic.

15.

The letters
\zeta
,
z
,
o
, or any of their decorated
forms (e.g.
\zeta_{i}
,
\zeta^{\prime}
)
customarily denote positive constants independent of
r
,
\delta
, and
(A,\Psi)
or
(B,\Phi)
, and
whose precise values are immaterial. Unless
otherwise specified, they can be taken so as to increase with each
appearance. Likewise,
r_{0}\geq 1
is a constant whose precise value may
change with appearances. It is taken so as to increase with each
appearance.

16.

Unless otherwise specified,
\chi\colon\thinspace{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}

denotes a smooth cutoff function supported on
(-\infty,2]
, and equal 1
on
(-\infty,1]
.

17.

B(x,R)
or
B_{x}(R)
denotes a geodesic ball centered at
x
with
radius
R
.

18.

*_{3}
and
*_{4}
respectively denote Hodge dual on
3-manifolds and 4-manifolds. Sometimes we also use
*_{Y}
to denote
the Hodge dual on the specific manifold
Y
.

19.

Given a
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
4-manifold
X
with spinor bundle

{\mathbb{S}}^{+}\oplus{\mathbb{S}}^{-}
, we use
\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits({\mathbb{S}}^{+})
,
\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits(\det{\mathbb{S}})
to denote respectively the space of
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
-connections
on
{\mathbb{S}}^{+}
, and the space of unitary connections on
\det{\mathbb{S}}^{+}
. Given a 3-manifold
Y
with spinor bundle
{\mathbb{S}}
, let

\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits({\mathbb{S}})
,
\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits(\det{\mathbb{S}})
be similarly defined. A

\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
connection induces a unitary connection on its
associated determinant line bundle, and therefore we have a map from

\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits({\mathbb{S}}^{+})
to
\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits(\det{\mathbb{S}}^{+})
(or
\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits({\mathbb{S}})

to
\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits(\det{\mathbb{S}})
in the case of 3-manifolds). This map is an
isomorphism when
H^{1}
of the 3- or 4-manifold in question has no
2-torsion; in particular,
\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits({\mathbb{S}})\simeq\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits(\det{\mathbb{S}})
in
the case of 3-manifolds.
Abusing notation, we often use the same notation (
A
in the case of
4-manifolds and
B
in the case of 3-manifolds) to denote either a
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}

connection or its induced connection on the associated determinant
bundle. It should be clear from the context which is
meant. E.g.
A-A_{0}
and
F_{A}
are

i{\mathbb{R}}
-valued differential forms; so the connection
A
in
these cases must refer to a connection on
\det{\mathbb{S}}^{+}
. The
A

appearing in covariant derivatives
\nabla_{A}
or Dirac operators

\mbox{\mbox{$\partial\mkern-7.5mu\mbox{/}\mkern 2.0mu$}}_{A}^{+}
on a spinor-bundle necessarily refer to a
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
-connection.
In the same token,
\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits(X)
(resp.
\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits(Y)
) is used to
denote either
\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits({\mathbb{S}}^{+})
or
\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits(\det{\mathbb{S}}^{+})
(resp.
\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits({\mathbb{S}})

or
\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits(\det{\mathbb{S}})
).

20.

We frequently use the shorthand
\partial_{s}
for

\frac{d}{ds}
.

21.

“LHS” and “RHS” respectively stand for “left hand side”
and “right hand side”.

22.

Given a function
f
,
(f)_{+}
denotes the function
\max\,(f,0)
. Given a 2-form
\mu
on a 4-manifold,
\mu^{+}
denotes
its self-dual part.

23.

(A_{r},\Psi_{r})
is used to denote a solution to the
r
-th
version of the Seiberg-Witten equations; i.e.
{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mu_{r},{\mathfrak{p}}}(A_{r},\Psi_{r})=0
. However, in the context of
a sequence
\{(A_{n},\Psi_{n})\}_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
,
(A_{n},\Psi_{n})

is often used as a short hand for
(A_{r_{n}},\Psi_{r_{n}})
, where

\{r_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
is a sequence of positive numbers
approaching
\infty
.

24.

The book
[
T
]
contains four previously published
articles. When referring to theorems or equations therein, we use
roman numerical to label the specific article we refer to.
For example: Theorem I.1.2 stands for
Theorem 1.2 of the first article in
[
T
]
.

This article frequently refers to formulas in various literature, which
unfortunately use different conventions. For the reader’s convenience,
we clarify some of their relations here. The Seiberg-Witten equations
in this article follow the convention of
[
KM
]
. In Taubes’ articles,
F_{A}/2
above is
replaced by
F_{A}
. This results in a difference of factor 2 in many
expressions below from their analogs in Taubes’ articles.
To sum up,

\begin{split}\Psi&=\Psi_{KM}=\Psi_{PFH}/\sqrt{2}=\Psi_{har}/\sqrt{2};\\
\frac{i\mu}{4}&=irw_{f}|_{PFH}=-2w|_{KM};\\
\frac{i\mu^{+}}{4}&=\frac{ir\omega}{8}|_{har},\end{split}

where the first expressions in all three lines are in the notation used in
this article, and the subscripts
KM
,
PFH
,
har
refer
respectively to their counterparts in
[
KM
]
,
[
LT
]
, and

[
Ts
]
.

The rest of this article is organized as follows: Sections 2 and 3
respectively contain prerequisites regarding
Gr
and
SW
sides of
the story in our context, in the sense of Taubes’
SW=Gr
in

[
T
]
. Section
3.6
enumerates some key steps of
Taubes’ arguments in the
SW\Rightarrow Gr
part of
[
T
]
, which basically all subsequent literature in
this direction also go through, including the present article. It also
indicates where each step is carried out in the remainder of the
article.
Section 4 is the crux of the proof, and contains a
preliminary version of the crucial “energy bound”. The latter is
iteratively improved in Sections 5 and 6 to serve our
purposes. Sections 5 and 6 describe how
some of Taubes’ original argument should be modified in our
context. The final section, Section 7, brings everything together to
complete the proofs of the main theorems.

Lastly, a word of caution is in order: For the sake of brevity, we
often quote minute details from
[
Ts
]
and
[
T
]
directly
without preparation.
The reader is thus strongly advised to keep copies of
[
Ts
]
and
[
T
]
at hand.

Acknowledgements.

The author’s debt to Taubes is
abundantly evident.
This work is supported in part by
Hong Kong Research Grant Counsel grants GRF-401913, 14316516, 143055419, and in earlier
stages, by U.S. NSF grants.

2
Preliminaries: the
Gr
side

Some basic notions and facts about the right hand side of our variant
of
SW\Rightarrow Gr
are gathered in this section.

2.1
t-orbits and t-curves

Recall (weighted) subvarieties and related notions and conventions from Section

1.4
; in particular, items (11)-(14). The notion of t-orbits and t-curves are special
cases of weighted subvarieties.

Let
Y
be a closed oriented riemannian 3-manifold and
\theta
be
a harmonic, regular Morse-Novikov 1-form on
Y
.

Definition 2.1
.

A
t-orbit
with respect to

(Y,\theta)
is a (possibly empty) finite set
\boldsymbol{\gamma}=\{(\gamma_{a},m_{a})\}_{a}
where:

•

Each
\gamma_{a}\subset Y-\theta^{-1}(0)
is
a finite-length flow line of the vector field dual to
\theta
, and

\gamma_{a}\neq\gamma_{b}
when
a\neq b
.
m_{a}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}
.

•

\partial\,(\bigcup_{a}\gamma_{a})=\theta^{-1}(0)
as oriented manifolds,

\gamma_{a}
is called a
constituent flow line
of

\boldsymbol{\gamma}
, and
m_{a}
its
weight
.

A
multi-orbit
of
(Y,\theta)
is a union

\cup_{a}\gamma_{a}\subset Y
, where
\gamma_{a}
are disjoint
finite-length flow lines of the vector field dual to
\theta
, and
the index set
\{a\}
has finite (possibly 0) elements.

Let
({\mathscr{X}},\omega)
be a (possibly noncompact)
symplectic 4-manifold, and
J
be a compatible almost
complex structure. A subvariety
C\subset{\mathscr{X}}
is
said to be a
pseudo-holomorphic (or
J
-holomorphic) subvariety
if the representing
map
u_{0}\colon\thinspace C_{0}\to{\mathscr{X}}
of
C
satisfies then following additional
conditions:

C_{0}
(possibly non-compact, non-connected or empty)
is
C^{\infty}
complex curve, and
u_{0}\colon\thinspace C_{0}\to{\mathscr{X}}
is a
proper
J
-holomorphic map. A
weighted pseudo-holomorphic (or

J
-holomorphic) subvariety
in
{\mathscr{X}}
is a weighted subvariety

\{(C_{a},m_{a})\}_{a}
such that
C_{a}
are irreducible
J
-holomorphic
subvarieties.

Let
X
be an oriented MCE, and
\nu
be an admissible form on

X
. Let
J
be the
almost complex structure on

X_{0}:=X-\nu^{-1}(0)

determined by
g
and
\omega=2\nu^{+}
.

Definition 2.2
.

A
t-curve
on
X_{\bullet}
is a weighted
J
-holomorphic subvariety

\mathbf{C}=[C,\tilde{C}]
in
X_{0}\cap X_{\bullet}
such that the intersection number of
\mathbf{C}
with each linking 2-sphere
of
\nu^{-1}(0)
is one.

Note that when
(X,\nu)=({\mathbb{R}}\times Y,\pi_{2}^{*}(*_{3}\theta))
is
cylindrical, a translation (by
\tau_{L}
) of a
t-curve/pseudo-holomorphic subvariety gives another
t-curve/pseudo-holomorphic subvariety. Moreover, every multi-orbit
\gamma
on
(Y,\theta)
corresponds
to a translation-invariant pseudo-holomorphic subvariety
{\mathbb{R}}\times\gamma
in
(X,\nu)
, and vice versa. Such a translation-invariant pseudo-holomorphic
subvariety is said to be
constant
. The notion
of
constant
t-curves in cylindrical admissible pairs is
similarly defined.

2.2
Topology on the space of t-curves

Following Taubes
[
Td
]
, we define

Definition 2.3
.

Given two subsets
S,S^{\prime}
in a riemannian manifold
{\mathscr{X}}
, the
geometric distance

between
S,S^{\prime}
in
{\mathscr{X}}
is

\mathop{\mathrm{dist}\,}\nolimits_{\mathscr{X}}(S,S^{\prime}):=\Big(\sup_{x\in S}\mathop{\mathrm{dist}\,}\nolimits(x,S^{\prime})+\sup_{x\in S^{\prime}}\mathop{\mathrm{dist}\,}\nolimits(x,S)\Big).

Let
({\mathscr{X}},\omega)
be a (possibly noncompact)
symplectic 4-manifold, and
J
be a compatible almost
complex structure. Let
C_{n}
,
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\geq 0}
be
pseudo-holomorphic subvarieties in
{\mathscr{X}}
with

\int_{C_{n}}\omega<\infty
. The sequence
\{C_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
is said to
converge geometrically
to
C_{0}
if the
following hold:

•

Regard each
C_{n}
and its irreducible components as
2-dimensional rectifiable currents. Then
\{C_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}

converges weakly as currents to a current of the form
\sum_{a}m_{a}C^{\prime}_{a}
,
where
m_{a}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}
, and
C^{\prime}_{a}
are irreducible components of

C_{0}
.

•

For any compact subset
K\subset{\mathscr{X}}
,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathop{\mathrm{dist}\,}\nolimits_{K}(C_{n}|_{K},C_{0}|_{K})=0
.

There is a version of Gromov compactness result for pseudo-holomorphic
subvarieties in 4-dimensional symplectic manifolds that hold
simultaneously for all genera:

Proposition 2.4
.

(
[
Td
]
Propositions 3.8) Let
({\mathscr{X}},\omega),J
be as in
Definition
2.3
. Suppose there is a countable exhaustion
\{{\mathscr{X}}_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
of
{\mathscr{X}}
by open subsets with compact closure,

{\mathscr{X}}_{1}\subset\cdots{\mathscr{X}}_{n}\subset{\mathscr{X}}_{n+1}\subset\cdots\subset{\mathscr{X}}
, and let
\{(\omega_{n},J_{n})\}_{n}

be a corresponding sequence whose typical element
(\omega_{n},J_{n})

consists of a symplectic form
\omega_{n}
and a compatible
almost complex structure
J_{n}
on
{\mathscr{X}}_{n}
. Suppose that
\{(\omega_{n},J_{n})\}_{n}
converges to
(\omega,J)
in the
C^{\infty}
topology.
Let
z_{E}>0
, and let
\{C_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
be a sequence of
J_{n}
-holomorphic
subvarieties in
({\mathscr{X}}_{n},\omega_{n})
such that
\int_{C_{n}}\omega<z_{E}

\forall n
. Then there is a
J
-holomorphic subvariety

C\subset{\mathscr{X}}
with
\int_{C}\omega<z_{E}
and a subsequence of

\{C_{n}\}_{n}
which converges geometrically to
C
.

Now let
X
be a 4-dimensional MCE and
\nu
be an admissible form
on
X
.

Definition 2.5
.

A sequence of t-curves
\{\mathbf{C}_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
in
X^{{}^{\prime}a}
is said to
(locally) converge
to a t-curve
\mathbf{C}_{0}
in
X^{{}^{\prime}a}
if
the underlying subvarieties of
\mathbf{C}_{n}

converge geometrically to the underlying subvariety of

\mathbf{C}_{0}
, and the associated currents of
\mathbf{C}_{n}

converge to the associated current of
\mathbf{C}_{0}
.

The notion of
convergence of t-orbits
is similarly defined.

Definition 2.6
.

Let
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}
. A t-curve

\mathbf{C}
on
X^{{}^{\prime}a}
is said to be
asymptotic
on the
Y_{i}
-end to some t-orbit

\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{i}
with respect to the pair
(Y_{i},\nu_{i})
if:

(i)

Let
\gamma_{i}
and
C
be respectively the underlying
subvarieties of
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{i}
and
\mathbf{C}
.
Given any sequence of positive numbers
\{L_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}
with

\lim_{n}L_{n}\to\infty
,

\{(\tau_{-L_{n}}C)|_{\hat{Y}_{i}}\}_{n}
geometrically converges to
[0,\infty)\times\gamma_{i}
in
\hat{Y}_{i}
. In fact,
for all sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
, there exists an

R(\varepsilon)>0
, such that

\mathop{\mathrm{dist}\,}\nolimits_{\hat{Y}_{i}}(\tau_{-L}C,[0,\infty)\times\gamma_{i})<\varepsilon
,
\forall L>R(\varepsilon)
.

(ii)

The sequence of currents
\{\tau_{-L_{n}}\tilde{C}\}_{n}
converges weakly to

\pi_{2}^{*}\tilde{\gamma}_{i}
, where
\pi_{2}\colon\thinspace\hat{Y}_{i}={\mathbb{R}}\times Y_{i}\to Y_{i}
is the projection.

The t-orbit
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{i}
above is said to be
the
limiting t-orbit
of
\mathbf{C}
on the

Y_{i}
-end. In the case when
X={\mathbb{R}}\times Y
is cylindrical, the
limiting t-orbit on
(+\infty)
-end is called the
+\infty
-limit
of
\mathbf{C}
, and the limiting t-orbit on
the
(-\infty)
-end, as a t-orbit in
Y
, is called the
-\infty
-limit
of
\mathbf{C}
. If
{\bf C}
has a limiting
t-orbit on each of its Morse ends,
{\bf C}
is said to be
asymptotically constant
.

Replace the t-curve
{\bf C}
and the limiting t-orbits
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{i}
above respectively by a pseudo-holomorphic subvariety
P
, and

\textsl{p}_{i}
, where
\textsl{p}_{i}
is a union of flow lines of the vector
field dual to
*\nu_{i}
. The notion of
P
being
asymptotic to

\textsl{p}_{i}
on the
Y_{i}
, is similarly defined.

2.3
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
structures

(a)
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
-structure of t-orbits

A t-orbit
\boldsymbol{\gamma}=\{(\gamma_{i},m_{i})\}_{i}
is assigned a
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}

structure as follows. Let
B_{\theta}
be a small tubular neighborhood
of
\theta^{-1}(0)\subset Y
. Let

[\boldsymbol{\gamma}]:=[\tilde{\gamma}]=\sum_{i}m_{i}[\gamma_{i}]\in H_{1}(Y,\theta^{-1}(0);\mathbb{Z})\simeq H_{1}(Y,B_{\theta};\mathbb{Z})

and let
E
be a complex line bundle on
Y-B_{\theta}
with

c_{1}(E)=p.d.[\boldsymbol{\gamma}]
. Let
K^{-1}
denote the subbundle

\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}\,}\nolimits\theta
of
TY|_{Y-B_{\theta}}
. This is an oriented plane bundle; choose a
complex structure on
K^{-1}
compatible with the orientation.
Then

\mathbb{S}=E\oplus E\otimes K^{-1}
is trivial over
\partial(Y-B_{\theta})
. Extend
\mathbb{S}
trivially over
B_{\theta}
to
get a complex rank 2 vector bundle on
Y
, also denoted by

\mathbb{S}
. Then the
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
-structure of
\boldsymbol{\gamma}
,
denoted
\mathfrak{s}(\boldsymbol{\gamma})
, is that with
\mathbb{S}
as its
spinor bundle, and with
E
,
E\otimes K^{-1}
being eigen-bundles
under the Clifford action of
*\theta/|\theta|
corresponding to
eigenvalues
-i
and
i
respectively.

Let
S_{Y,*\theta}\subset H_{1}(Y,\theta^{-1}(0);\mathbb{Z})
denote the subset that maps to the
fundamental class of
\theta^{-1}(0)
under the connecting map

\delta\colon\thinspace H_{1}(Y,\theta^{-1}(0);\mathbb{Z})\to H_{0}\,(\theta^{-1}(0);\mathbb{Z})
in the relative exact sequence of the pair
(Y,\theta^{-1}(0))
. Note that the relative homology class
[\boldsymbol{\gamma}]\in H_{1}(Y,\theta^{-1}(0);\mathbb{Z})
of any t-orbit falls in

S_{Y,*\theta}
, and in fact the
procedure described above defines an isomorphism from
S_{Y,*\theta}
to
the space of
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
structures on
Y
,

\varsigma_{(Y,*\theta)}\colon\thinspace S_{Y,*\theta}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}(Y),

as affines spaces over
H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z})\simeq H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z})
.

Note that the assumption that
\theta
is regular (cf. Definition

1.5
) implies that for any fixed
{\mathfrak{s}}\in\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits(Y)
, there
are finitely many t-orbits (with respect to
(Y,\theta)
),
\boldsymbol{\gamma}
, with

{\mathfrak{s}}(\boldsymbol{\gamma})=\boldsymbol{\gamma}
. Denote this set by
{\mathbb{P}}(Y,\theta;{\mathfrak{s}})
. (
Y
,
\theta
, or both are sometimes omitted
from the notation when they are clear from the context. For example,
{\mathbb{P}}(Y,\theta;{\mathfrak{s}})={\mathbb{P}}(Y,{\mathfrak{s}})={\mathbb{P}}({\mathfrak{s}})
.)

(b)
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
-structure of t-curves

Let
H_{*,closed}
denote the Borel-Moore homology
2
2

2

The
Borel-Moore homology is also
called the
“closed homology” or “homology with closed support”. See e.g.

[
BT
]
Remark 5.17, the Wiki article

https://en.m.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borel-Moore_homology#CITEREF1960

and references therein. It is dual to the cohomology of
compact support.
. It is useful to note that in our
setting,

\begin{split}H_{*,closed}\,(X;{\mathbb{K}})&\simeq H_{*,closed}\,(X^{{}^{\prime}a},\partial X^{{}^{\prime}a};{\mathbb{K}})\\
&\simeq H_{*}(X_{c},\partial X_{c};{\mathbb{K}});\\
H_{*,closed}\,\big(X^{{}^{\prime}a},\partial X^{{}^{\prime}a}\cup Z^{{}^{\prime}a}_{0};{\mathbb{K}}\big)&\simeq H_{*,closed}\,\big(\overline{X^{{}^{\prime}a}},\partial\overline{X^{{}^{\prime}a}}\cup Z_{0}^{{}^{\prime}a};{\mathbb{K}})\\
&\simeq H_{*}(\overline{X^{{}^{\prime}a}_{\delta}},\partial\overline{X^{{}^{\prime}a}_{\delta}};{\mathbb{K}}\big),\\
\end{split}

where
X^{{}^{\prime}a}_{\delta}
,
Z^{{}^{\prime}a}_{\delta}
are as defined in Section

1.4
(9).

A t-curve
\mathbf{C}=\{(C_{k},m_{k})\}_{k}
in
X^{{}^{\prime}a}
determines a relative homology
class through its associated current

\begin{split}[\mathbf{C}]&=[\tilde{C}]\in H_{2,closed}\,(X^{{}^{\prime}a},\partial X^{{}^{\prime}a}\cup Z^{{}^{\prime}a}_{\delta};\mathbb{Z})\\
&\quad\simeq H_{2,closed}\,(X^{{}^{\prime}a},\partial X^{{}^{\prime}a}\cup Z^{{}^{\prime}a}_{0};\mathbb{Z})\simeq H_{2}(\overline{X^{{}^{\prime}a}_{\delta}},\partial\overline{X^{{}^{\prime}a}_{\delta}};\mathbb{Z}).\end{split}

The last condition in Definition
2.2
implies that
[\mathbf{C}]

maps to the fundamental class
[Z_{0}^{{}^{\prime}a}]\in H_{1,closed}\,(Z_{0}^{{}^{\prime}a},\partial Z_{0}^{{}^{\prime}a};\mathbb{Z}^{{}^{\prime}a})
under the connecting map

\delta
in the relative exact sequence

\begin{split}\cdots 0\to&H_{2,closed}\,(X^{{}^{\prime}a},\partial X^{{}^{\prime}a};\mathbb{Z})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}H_{2,closed}\,(X^{{}^{\prime}a},\partial X^{{}^{\prime}a}\cup Z^{{}^{\prime}a}_{0};\mathbb{Z})\\
&\quad\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\delta}}{{\to}}H_{1,closed}\,\big(Z^{{}^{\prime}a}_{0},\partial Z^{{}^{\prime}a}_{0};\mathbb{Z}^{{}^{\prime}a}\big)\cdots.\end{split}

Let

S_{X,\nu}:=\delta^{-1}[Z_{0}^{{}^{\prime}a}]\subset H_{2,closed}\,(X^{{}^{\prime}a},\partial X^{{}^{\prime}a}\cup Z^{{}^{\prime}a}_{0};\mathbb{Z}).

In parallel to the case with t-orbits, there is an isomorphism

\varsigma_{X,\nu}\colon\thinspace S_{X,\nu}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}(X)\simeq\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}(X^{{}^{\prime}a})

as affine spaces
over the abelian group

\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}\,}\nolimits\delta=\mathop{\mathrm{Im}\,}\nolimits p\simeq H_{2,closed}\,(X^{{}^{\prime}a},\partial X^{{}^{\prime}a};\mathbb{Z})\simeq H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}).

(In this article, we do not
distinguish between the set
of
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
-structures and
X
and the set of

\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
-structures on
X^{{}^{\prime}a}
, as they are canonically isomorphic.)
The inverse of
\varsigma_{X,\nu}
can be described more concretely
as follow: Given
a
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
structure
\mathfrak{s}
on
X
, take

{\mathbb{S}}_{X}=\mathbb{S}=\mathbb{S}^{+}\oplus\mathbb{S}^{-}
to be the associated spinor
bundle. Over
X^{{}^{\prime}a}_{0}
, the Clifford action of
\nu^{+}/|\nu^{+}|
splits
\mathbb{S}^{+}|_{X_{0}}
as a
direct sum
E\oplus E\otimes K^{-1}
of eigenbundles, where
E

is a complex line bundle
corresponds to eigenvalue
-i
. Then
\varsigma_{X,\nu}^{-1}(\mathfrak{s})\in H_{2,closed}\,(X^{{}^{\prime}a},\partial X^{{}^{\prime}a}\cup Z_{0};\mathbb{Z})
is the Poincaré dual of
c_{1}(E)\in H^{2}(X^{{}^{\prime}a}-\nu^{-1}(0);{\mathbb{Z}})
.

Notice that the definition of
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
structures of t-orbits
and t-curves only depend on their associated currents. So are the
various notions of energy defined in the upcoming subsection.
Also, when
(X,\nu)
is cylindrical, two t-curves related by
translation have the same
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
structure.

2.4
Energy of t-curves and chains of t-curves

Let
X
be an MCE and
\nu
an admissible form on
X
.

Definition 2.7
.

Given an oriented 2-dimensional subvariety
C
in
X^{{}^{\prime}a}
and

X_{\bullet}\subset X^{{}^{\prime}a}
, the
\nu
-energy
of
C|_{X_{\bullet}}
is:

{\mathscr{F}}_{\nu}(C|_{X_{\bullet}}):=\int_{C|_{X_{\bullet}}}\nu.

When
X_{\bullet}
is compact, the following is called the

*\nu
-energy of
C|_{X_{\bullet}}
:

{\mathscr{F}}^{*}_{\nu}(C|_{X_{\bullet}}):=\int_{C|_{X_{\bullet}}}*\nu.

Finally, setting
\omega=2\nu^{+}
, the
\omega
-energy
of
C|_{X_{\bullet}}
is:

{\mathscr{F}}_{\omega}(C|_{X_{\bullet}}):={\mathscr{F}}_{\nu}(C|_{X_{\bullet}})+\sup_{X_{*}\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{1}}{\mathscr{F}}^{*}_{\nu}(C|_{X_{*}}),

where
{\mathfrak{X}}_{1}=\{X_{*}\,|\,X_{*}\subset X_{\bullet},|X_{*}|\leq 1\}
. (Cf. §
1.4
for the
definition of
|X_{\bullet}|
).

When
\omega^{-1}(0)=\nu^{-1}(0)=\emptyset
, the finite-energy condition above
agrees with that in
[
BEHWZ
]
. It also straightforwardly from the
definitions that in the cylindrical case, two t-curves that are
related by translation have the same
\nu
-energy,
*\nu
-energy, and
\omega
-energy.

Remark 2.8
.

Just as in
[
BEHWZ
]
, a bound on
{\mathscr{F}}_{\omega}
is the
prerequisite for local compactness results, while a bound on

{\mathscr{F}}_{\nu}
is used to establish global compactness (weak convergence to
broken-trajectories or “buildings”). In the context of
[
Ts
]

where
X
is closed,
{\mathscr{F}}_{\omega}
is simply the LHS of its
Equation (7.10), which also give a bound on this in terms of the

\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
structure of
C
, the cohomology class
[\omega]
, and
the metric on
X
.

The following useful lemma is an immediate consequence of the
compatibility condition of
J
.

Lemma 2.9
.

Under the assumptions of the preceding definition, if
C
is a
pseudo-holomorphic subvariety in
X_{\bullet}
, then
{\mathscr{F}}_{\omega}(C),{\mathscr{F}}_{\nu}(C),{\mathscr{F}}_{\nu}^{*}(C)\geq 0
. Moreover, when
(X_{\bullet}=I\times Y,\nu=\pi_{2}^{*}\underline{\nu})
is cylindrical,
{\mathscr{F}}_{\nu}(C)=0

iff
C
is constant. Namely,
C=I\times\gamma
, where
\gamma
is a union of flow lines
of the vector dual to
*\underline{\nu}
in
Y
.

Given a t-curve
\mathbf{C}=\{(C_{k},m_{k})\}_{k}
in
X_{\bullet}
, its
\nu
-energy
,
*\nu
-energy
,
\omega
-energy
are
respectively defined to be

\begin{split}{\mathscr{F}}_{\nu}(\mathbf{C}):=\sum_{k}m_{k}{\mathscr{F}}_{\nu}(C_{k}|_{X_{\bullet}}),\\
{\mathscr{F}}^{*}_{\nu}(\mathbf{C}):=\sum_{k}m_{k}{\mathscr{F}}^{*}_{\nu}(C_{k}|_{X_{\bullet}}),\\
{\mathscr{F}}_{\omega}(\mathbf{C}):=\sum_{k}m_{k}{\mathscr{F}}_{\omega}(C_{k}|_{X_{\bullet}}).\end{split}

We will see in Proposition
2.16
below that by the
preceding lemma, any t-curve

\mathbf{C}
with finite

\omega
-energy in
X^{{}^{\prime}a}
is
asymptotic to a t-orbit
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{i}
on each of its Morse
end
\hat{Y}_{i}
.
It also follows from the preceding lemma and Proposition
2.4
that

Proposition 2.10
.

Let
X
be an MCE and
\nu
be an admissible form on
X
. Let

\{\mathbf{C}_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
be
a sequence of t-curves on
X^{{}^{\prime}a}
, with a uniform upper bound on
their
\omega
-energy. Then there is a t-curve

\mathbf{C}_{0}
and a subsequence of
\{\mathbf{C}_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
that locally converges to
\mathbf{C}_{0}
.

Definition 2.11
.

Let
(X,\nu)
be an admissible pair.

(i)

Suppose the admissible pair
(X={\mathbb{R}}\times Y,\nu)
is
cylindrical. (And thus
X
has no vanishing ends and
X^{{}^{\prime}a}=X^{\prime\prime}=X
.) Let
{\mathfrak{s}}_{Y}
denote the restriction of the
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}

structure
{\mathfrak{s}}
to
\{s\}\times Y
, for any
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
. For

{\mathfrak{K}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\geq 0}
, a (
{\mathfrak{K}}
-component)

chain of t-curves

{\mathfrak{C}}
on
X^{{}^{\prime}a}
consists of:

–

a sequence of
{\mathfrak{K}}+1
t-orbits with
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
-structure

{\mathfrak{s}}_{Y}
,

\{\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{0},\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{{\mathfrak{K}}}\}
, which we call the
rest
orbits
of
{\mathfrak{C}}
; and

–

a sequence (possibly empty) of non-constant, asymptotically constant
t-curves

\mathbf{C}_{k}
on
X={\mathbb{R}}\times Y
with
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
-structure
{\mathfrak{s}}
, with
-\infty
- and

+\infty
-limits respectively
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{k-1}
and

\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{k}
.

We say that
\mathfrak{C}
has
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
-structure
{\mathfrak{s}}
. The

(
-\infty
)-limit of
\mathfrak{C}
is
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{0}
;
the
+\infty
-limit of
\mathfrak{C}
is

\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{{\mathfrak{K}}}
. The
k
-th component
of
{\mathfrak{C}}
is

{\bf C}_{k}
. We typically write
{\mathfrak{C}}
as an ordered set,
{\mathfrak{C}}=\{{\bf C}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{\mathfrak{K}}
, when

{\mathfrak{K}}>0
. Two chains of t-curves,
{\mathfrak{C}}
,
{\mathfrak{C}}^{\prime}
, are said to be
equivalent
and
denoted
{\mathfrak{C}}\sim_{\tau}{\mathfrak{C}}^{\prime}
, if they have the same number of components
and the same rest orbits, and when
{\mathfrak{K}}>0
, each pair of corresponding components

{\bf C}_{k}
,
{\bf C}^{\prime}_{k}
are related by
translation. I.e.
{\bf C}^{\prime}_{k}=\tau_{L}{\bf C}_{k}
for a certain
L\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

(ii)

Suppose the admissible pair
(X,\nu)
is not
cylindrical. Denote the
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
structure on
X
by
{\mathfrak{s}}
,
and the restriction
{\mathfrak{s}}|_{\text{{\small s}}_{i}^{-1}(0)}=:{\mathfrak{s}}_{i}
for
each
i\in\mathfrak{Y}_{X}
. A

chain of t-curves
on
X^{{}^{\prime}a}
is a pair
\mathfrak{C}=\{\mathbf{C}_{0},\{\mathfrak{C}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathfrak{Y}_{m}}\}
such that:

(1)

\mathbf{C}_{0}
is an asymptotically constant t-curve with
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
-structure

{\mathfrak{s}}
on
X^{{}^{\prime}a}
. For each
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}
, let
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{0,i}
denote the limiting
t-orbit of
\mathbf{C}_{0}
on the
Y_{i}
-end.

(2)

Let
(Y_{i},\nu_{i})
denote the ending pair of the end

\hat{Y}_{i}\subset X
. For each
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}
,
\mathfrak{C}_{i}
is a chain of
t-curves of
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
-structure
\hat{{\mathfrak{s}}}_{i}
on the cylindrical admissible pair

(\mathbb{R}\times Y_{i},\hat{\nu}_{i})
, where
\hat{{\mathfrak{s}}}_{i}
,

\hat{\nu}_{i}
are respectively the pull-back of
{\mathfrak{s}}_{i}
and

\nu_{i}
under the projection
{\mathbb{R}}\times Y_{i}\to Y_{i}
.

(3)

For each
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}
, the
-\infty
-limit of
\mathfrak{C}_{i}
is
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{0,i}
.

We say that the chain
\mathfrak{C}
has
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
-structure

{\mathfrak{s}}
. The rest orbits of
{\mathfrak{C}}_{i}
are said to be the rest orbits of

{\mathfrak{C}}
(in the
\hat{Y}_{i}
-end). Two chains of t-curves,

{\mathfrak{C}}=\{{\bf C}_{0},\{{\mathfrak{C}}_{i}\}_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}}\}
,
{\mathfrak{C}}^{\prime}=\{{\bf C}^{\prime}_{0},\{{\mathfrak{C}}^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}}\}
, are said to be
equivalent
and
denoted
{\mathfrak{C}}\sim_{\tau}{\mathfrak{C}}^{\prime}
, if
{\bf C}_{0}={\bf C}^{\prime}_{0}
and
{\mathfrak{C}}_{i}\sim_{\tau}{\mathfrak{C}}^{\prime}_{i}

\forall i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}
.

An equivalence class of chains of t-curves is an analog of what is called an “(unparametrized) broken
trajectory” or “broken
X
-trajectory” in
[
KM
]
’s Definitions 16.1.2 and 24.6.1.

The
\nu
-energy and
\omega
-energy of chains of
t-curves on
(X^{{}^{\prime}a},\nu)
are defined as follows: When
(X,\nu)
is
cylindrical and
{\mathfrak{C}}=\{\mathbf{C}_{k}\}_{k}
has at least one component,

{\mathscr{F}}_{\nu}({\mathfrak{C}}):=\sum_{k}{\mathscr{F}}_{\nu}(\mathbf{C}_{k});\quad{\mathscr{F}}_{\omega}({\mathfrak{C}}):=\sum_{k}{\mathscr{F}}_{\omega}(\mathbf{C}_{k}).

If
{\mathfrak{C}}
has 0 components and a single rest orbit
\boldsymbol{\gamma}
, set
{\mathscr{F}}_{\nu}({\mathfrak{C}}):={\mathscr{F}}_{\nu}({\mathbb{R}}\times\boldsymbol{\gamma})=0
and
{\mathscr{F}}_{\omega}({\mathfrak{C}}):={\mathscr{F}}_{\omega}({\mathbb{R}}\times\boldsymbol{\gamma})
.

When
(X,\nu)
is non-cylindrical and

\mathfrak{C}=(\mathbf{C}_{0},\{\mathfrak{C}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathfrak{Y}_{m}})
,

{\mathscr{F}}_{\nu}({\mathfrak{C}}):=\sum_{k}{\mathscr{F}}_{\nu}(\mathbf{C}_{0})+\sum_{i}{\mathscr{F}}_{\nu}({\mathfrak{C}}_{i});\quad{\mathscr{F}}_{\omega}({\mathfrak{C}}):=\sum_{k}{\mathscr{F}}_{\omega}(\mathbf{C}_{0})+\sum_{i}{\mathscr{F}}_{\omega}({\mathfrak{C}}_{i}).

Note that two equivalent chains of t-curves have the same
\nu
-energy and
\omega
-energy.

Definition 2.12
.

Let
\Gamma=\{r_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}\subset[1,\infty)
be a sequence of strictly
increasing numbers such that
r_{n}\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
, and

\{(A_{r},\Psi_{r})\}_{r}\in\Gamma
be a corresponding sequence of
admissible solutions to the Seiberg-Witten equation
{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mu_{r},\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}_{r}}(A_{r},\Psi_{r})=0
, where
\mu_{r}
,
\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}_{r}

are as in the statement of Theorem
1.8
. We say that the
sequence
\{(A_{r},\Psi_{r})\}_{r\in\Gamma}

weakly t-converges

to a chain of t-curves
{\mathfrak{C}}
if the following holds:

(i)

When
(X,\mu_{r},\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}_{r})
is cylindrical: If

{\mathfrak{C}}
has 0 components, namely, it has a single rest orbit

\boldsymbol{\gamma}
, then
\{(A_{r},\Psi_{r})\}_{r\in\Gamma}
t-converges
to the constant t-curve
{\mathbb{R}}\times\boldsymbol{\gamma}
. If
{\mathfrak{C}}
has
at least one component, write
\mathfrak{C}=\{\mathbf{C}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{\mathfrak{K}}
. There is
a sequence
\{{\bf L}_{r}=(L_{1,r},\cdots,L_{{\mathfrak{K}},r})\}_{r\in\Gamma}
in

{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathfrak{K}}
, with
\lim_{r\to\infty}(L_{k_{1},r}-L_{k_{2},r})=\infty

for any fixed pair
k_{1},k_{2}
with
k_{1}>k_{2}
, such that

\{\tau_{-L_{k,r}}(A_{r},\Psi_{r})\}_{r\in\Gamma}
t-converges to

\mathbf{C}_{k}
for each
k
. (Note that the admissibility
assumption on
\nu
implies that in this case
X
has no
vanishing ends, and
X^{{}^{\prime}a}=X
.)

(ii)

When
(X,\mu_{r},\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}_{r})
is not cylindrical, write

\mathfrak{C}=(\mathbf{C}_{0},\{\mathfrak{C}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathfrak{Y}_{m}}),\mathfrak{C}_{i}=\{\mathbf{C}_{i,k}\}_{k=1}^{{\mathfrak{K}}_{i}}
in the notation
of Definition
2.11
(ii). There exists a sequence
\{{\bf L}_{r}^{i}=(L^{i}_{1,r},\cdots,L^{i}_{{\mathfrak{K}}_{i},r})\}_{r\in\Gamma}
in

({\mathbb{R}}^{+})^{{\mathfrak{K}}_{i}}
for each
i\in\mathfrak{Y}_{m}
,
with
\lim_{r\to\infty}(L^{i}_{k_{1},r}-L^{i}_{k_{2},r})=\infty
for any fixed pair
k_{1},k_{2}
with
k_{1}>k_{2}
,
such that

\{\tau_{-L^{i}_{k,r}}(A_{r},\Psi_{r})\Big|_{\hat{Y}_{i}}\}_{r\in\Gamma}
t-converges to

\mathbf{C}_{i,k}
, and
\{(A_{r},\Psi_{r})\}_{r\in\Gamma}
t-converges to

\mathbf{C}_{0}
.

Note that if a sequence
\{(A_{r},\Psi_{r})\}_{r}
weakly t-converges to
a chain of t-curves
{\mathfrak{C}}
, and
{\mathfrak{C}}\sim_{\tau}{\mathfrak{C}}^{\prime}
, then
\{(A_{r},\Psi_{r})\}_{r}
also weakly t-converges to
{\mathfrak{C}}^{\prime}
. It therefore
makes sense to talk about weakly t-convergence to equivalence classes
of chains of t-curves.

2.5
Relative homology classes of (chains of) t-curves

Let
(X,\nu)
be an admissible pair as before.

Given two asymptotically constant t-curves
\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
in
X^{{}^{\prime}a}
with the
same
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
-structure and limiting t-orbits on all its Morse ends,

[\mathbf{C^{\prime}}-\mathbf{C}]:=[\tilde{C}^{\prime}-\tilde{C}]
defines a class in

H_{2}(X^{{}^{\prime}a},\partial X^{{}^{\prime}a};{\mathbb{Z}})
. The
two t-curves
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
,
\mathbf{C}
are said to be of
the same
