relative homology class
if
[\mathbf{C^{\prime}}-\mathbf{C}]=0
.
The precise value of
a
does not matter, as all
H_{2}(X^{{}^{\prime}a},\partial X^{{}^{\prime}a};{\mathbb{Z}})
are mutually canonically isomorphic, and the
relative homology classes of two
{\bf C},{\bf C}^{\prime}
in
X^{{}^{\prime}a}
and in

X^{{}^{\prime}b}
,
b\leq a
are identified by such a canonical
isomorphism.
We now describe the set of relative homology classes for (chains of)
t-curves in more detail. Let
\{(Y_{i},\nu_{i})\}_{i}
be the set of ending pairs of
(X,\nu)
, and
let
{\mathfrak{s}}
denote the
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
-structure on
X
. Let

{\mathfrak{s}}_{i}:={\mathfrak{s}}|_{\text{{\small s}}_{i}^{-1}(1)}
.
Consider the following
commutative diagram of relative exact sequences, and recall from
Section
2.3
(a) and (b) the
definitions of the maps and spaces
\varsigma_{X,\nu}\colon\thinspace S_{X,\nu}\to\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}(X)
,
\varsigma_{Y_{i},\nu_{i}}\colon\thinspace S_{Y_{i},\nu_{i}}\to\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}(Y_{i})
respectively via the second and the third
columns of this diagram:

\begin{CD}@ VVV@ VVV@ VVV\\
\cdots H_{2}(X^{{}^{\prime}a},\partial X^{{}^{\prime}a})@>{j_{\mathbb{K}}}>{}>H_{2,cl}\,(X^{{}^{\prime}a},\partial X^{{}^{\prime}a})@>{\partial}>{}>\bigoplus\limits_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}}H_{1}(Y_{i})\cdots\\
@ VVV@ VpVV@ VVV\\
\cdots H_{2}\,(X^{{}^{\prime}a},\partial X^{{}^{\prime}a}\cup Z^{{}^{\prime}a}_{0})@>{}>{}>H_{2,cl}\,(X^{{}^{\prime}a},\partial X^{{}^{\prime}a}\cup Z^{{}^{\prime}a}_{0})@>{\partial}>{}>\bigoplus\limits_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}}H_{1}(Y_{i},\nu_{i}^{-1}(0))\cdots\\
@ VVV@ V\delta VV@ VVV\\
\cdots H_{1}(Z^{{}^{\prime}a}_{0},\partial Z^{{}^{\prime}a}_{0})@>{}>{}>H_{1,cl}\,(Z^{{}^{\prime}a}_{0},\partial Z^{{}^{\prime}a}_{0})@>{}>{}>\bigoplus\limits_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}}H_{0}\,(\nu_{i}^{-1}(0))\cdots\\
@ VVV@ VVV@ VVV\\
\end{CD}

(2.1)

where the homologies are all with coefficient
{\mathbb{K}}
, and
H_{*,cl}:=H_{*,closed}
denote the Borel-Moore homology.

Let
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{i}
be
respectively t-orbits of
(Y_{i},\nu_{i})
with

\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
-structure
{\mathfrak{s}}_{i}
. The relative homology class of a
t-curve
\mathbf{C}
with
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
-structure
{\mathfrak{s}}
and
with limiting t-orbits
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{i}
takes value in the
space
{\mathscr{H}}\big((X^{{}^{\prime}a},\nu,{\mathfrak{s}}),\{\tilde{\gamma}_{i}\}_{i}\big)
defined below.

For each Morse ending pair
(Y_{i},\nu_{i})

i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}
, define the space
{\mathscr{Z}}_{(Y_{i},\nu_{i},{\mathfrak{s}}_{i})}
to be the space of all 1-dimensional integral currents

\tilde{\gamma}_{i}
on

Y_{i}
that represents the class
\varsigma_{(Y_{i},\nu_{i})}^{-1}({\mathfrak{s}}_{i})\in S_{Y_{i},\nu_{i}}\subset H_{1}(Y_{i},\nu_{i}^{-1}(0);{\mathbb{Z}})
.

Given
{\mathfrak{s}}\in\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}(X)
and
\tilde{\gamma}_{i}\in{\mathscr{Z}}_{(Y_{i},\nu_{i},{\mathfrak{s}}_{i})}
for each
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}
, define

{\mathscr{H}}\big((X^{{}^{\prime}a},\nu,{\mathfrak{s}}),\{\tilde{\gamma}_{i}\}_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}}\big)=\{\tilde{C}\}/\sim,

where
\tilde{C}
is a 2-dimensional integral current on

X^{{}^{\prime}a}
satisfying the following conditions.

•

For each
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}
,
\tilde{C}
is asymptotic to
\tilde{\gamma}_{i}
on the

\hat{Y}_{i}
-end in the sense of item (ii) in Definition

2.6
, and the limit
\lim_{l\to\infty}\int_{\tilde{C}|_{\hat{Y}_{i,l}}}\pi_{2}^{*}h<\infty
exists for any

h\in\Omega^{2}(Y_{i})
.

•

\tilde{C}
represents the element in
\varsigma_{X,\nu}^{-1}({\mathfrak{s}})\in S_{X,\nu}\subset H_{2,closed}\,(X^{{}^{\prime}a},\partial X^{{}^{\prime}a}\cup Z_{0}^{{}^{\prime}a};{\mathbb{Z}})
.

Meanwhile, we write
\tilde{C}\sim\tilde{C}^{\prime}
for two
such currents
\tilde{C}
,
\tilde{C}^{\prime}
if

\tilde{C}-\tilde{C}^{\prime}
is the boundary of an integral current that is
asymptotic to
0
on all Morse ends of
X
.

It follows from the preceding definition and Equation (
2.1
)
that the set
{\mathscr{H}}\big((X^{{}^{\prime}a},\nu,{\mathfrak{s}}),\{\tilde{\gamma}_{i}\}_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}}\big)
is an affine space over the abelian
group

{\mathscr{H}}_{X}:=\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}\,}\nolimits j_{\mathbb{Z}}\subset H_{2}(X^{{}^{\prime}a},\partial X^{{}^{\prime}a};{\mathbb{Z}}).

An element in
{\mathscr{H}}\big((X^{{}^{\prime}a},\nu,{\mathfrak{s}}),\{\tilde{\gamma}_{i}\}_{i}\big)
is said to be a (
{\mathbb{Z}}
-)
relative
homology class in
(X^{{}^{\prime}a},\nu,{\mathfrak{s}})
(rel

\{\tilde{\gamma}_{i}\}_{i}
)
. Later on, we sometimes omit
{\mathfrak{s}}

from the notation when the
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
structure is fixed.

Remark 2.13
.

Assertions (c) and (d) of Theorem
1.10
postulates that
b^{1}(Y_{i})=0

for all vanishing ends
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{v}
.
(In particular, this holds if
(X,\nu)

has no vanishing ends; especially, when
(X,\nu)
is cylindrical.) This
constraint implies that

H_{2}(\partial X^{{}^{\prime}a};{\mathbb{Z}})=\bigoplus_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{v}}H_{2}(Y_{i};{\mathbb{Z}})=0
, and hence

H_{2}(X^{{}^{\prime}a},\partial X^{{}^{\prime}a};{\mathbb{Z}})\simeq H_{2}(X^{{}^{\prime}a};{\mathbb{Z}})\simeq H_{2}(X;{\mathbb{Z}})
.
It also implies that the
\nu
-energy of a chain
{\mathfrak{C}}
depends
only on its
relative homology class and its limiting t-orbits.

When
(X^{{}^{\prime}a},\nu)=(X,\nu)=({\mathbb{R}}\times Y_{i},\pi_{2}^{*}\nu_{i})
is cylindrical,

{\mathscr{H}}_{X}=H_{2}(X;{\mathbb{Z}})=H_{2}(Y_{i};{\mathbb{Z}})
. We use
{\mathscr{H}}(Y_{i},\nu_{i},{\mathfrak{s}}_{i};\tilde{\gamma}_{i},\tilde{\gamma}_{i}^{\prime})
to denote
{\mathscr{H}}(({\mathbb{R}}\times Y_{i},\pi_{2}^{*}\nu_{i},\hat{{\mathfrak{s}}}_{i}),\{\tilde{\gamma}_{i},\tilde{\gamma}_{i}^{\prime}\})
, where

\tilde{\gamma}_{i},\tilde{\gamma}_{i}^{\prime}\in{\mathscr{Z}}_{Y_{i},\nu_{i},{\mathfrak{s}}_{i}}
are respectively the limiting currents at the
-\infty
-
and the
+\infty
-ends of
X
.
By concatenation, there is a composition map that sends every pair

(a,b)\in{\mathscr{H}}(Y_{i},\nu_{i},{\mathfrak{s}}_{i};\tilde{\gamma}_{i}^{-},\tilde{\gamma}_{i})\times{\mathscr{H}}(Y_{i},\nu_{i},{\mathfrak{s}}_{i};\tilde{\gamma}_{i},\tilde{\gamma}_{i}^{+})
to an element

b*a\in{\mathscr{H}}(Y_{i},\nu_{i},{\mathfrak{s}}_{i};\tilde{\gamma}_{i}^{-},\tilde{\gamma}_{i}^{+})
. Also, every element
\{h_{i}\}_{i}
in

\prod_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}}{\mathscr{H}}(Y_{i},\nu_{i},{\mathfrak{s}}_{i};\tilde{\gamma}_{i},\tilde{\gamma}_{i}^{\prime})
defines an isomorphism

\operatorname{c}_{\{h_{i}\}_{i}}\colon\thinspace{\mathscr{H}}\big((X^{{}^{\prime}a},\nu,{\mathfrak{s}}),\{\tilde{\gamma}_{i}\}_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}})\to{\mathscr{H}}\big((X^{{}^{\prime}a},\nu,{\mathfrak{s}}),\{\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}}).

Let
\mathfrak{C}=\{\mathbf{C}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{C}_{{\mathfrak{K}}}\}
be a chain of t-curves on
({\mathbb{R}}\times Y_{i},\pi_{2}^{*}\nu_{i})
with
{\mathfrak{K}}>0
. The
relative homology class of
{\mathfrak{C}}
is
the element

[{\mathfrak{C}}]:=[\tilde{C}_{\mathfrak{K}}]*\cdots*[\tilde{C}_{2}]*[\tilde{C}_{1}].

When
{\mathfrak{C}}
is a 0-component chain of t-curves with a single rest
orbit
\boldsymbol{\gamma}
, we set the relative homology class of

{\mathfrak{C}}
to be
the element
[{\mathbb{R}}\times\boldsymbol{\gamma}]\in{\mathscr{H}}(Y_{i},\nu_{i},{\mathfrak{s}}_{i};\tilde{\gamma},\tilde{\gamma})
, which maps to the
element
0\in H_{2}(Y_{i};{\mathbb{Z}})
under the canonical isomorphism
{\mathscr{H}}(Y_{i},\nu_{i},{\mathfrak{s}}_{i};\tilde{\gamma},\tilde{\gamma})\simeq H_{2}(Y_{i};{\mathbb{Z}})
.

Suppose the admissible pair
(X,\nu)
is not cylindrical.
Given a chain of
t-curves
\mathfrak{C}=(\mathbf{C}_{0},\{\mathfrak{C}_{i}\}_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}})
on
X^{{}^{\prime}a}
,
the
relative homology class
of
{\mathfrak{C}}
is defined to be

[{\mathfrak{C}}]=\operatorname{c}_{\{[{\mathfrak{C}}_{i}]\}_{i}}([{\bf C}_{0}]).

It follows directly from the definition that equivalent chains of
t-curves have the same relative homology class.

What follows are some observations for future reference. When the currents
\tilde{\gamma}_{i}
,
\tilde{C}
above are not
required to be integral, the same strategy can be employed to define
the real-variants of the spaces
{\mathscr{Z}}_{(Y_{i},\nu_{i},{\mathfrak{s}}_{i})}
,
{\mathscr{H}}\big((X^{{}^{\prime}a},\nu,{\mathfrak{s}}),\{\tilde{\gamma}_{i}\}_{i}\big)
and other related notions. These are respectively denoted by
{\mathscr{Z}}^{\mathbb{R}}_{(Y_{i},\nu_{i},{\mathfrak{s}}_{i})}
,
{\mathscr{H}}^{\mathbb{R}}\big((X^{{}^{\prime}a},\nu,{\mathfrak{s}}),\{\tilde{\gamma}_{i}\}_{i}\big)
, etc. The set of real relative
homology classes,
{\mathscr{H}}^{\mathbb{R}}\big((X^{{}^{\prime}a},\nu,{\mathfrak{s}}),\{\tilde{\gamma}_{i}\}_{i}\big)
, is now an affine space under

{\mathscr{H}}^{\mathbb{R}}_{X}:=\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}\,}\nolimits j_{\mathbb{R}}\subset H_{2}(X^{{}^{\prime}a},\partial X^{{}^{\prime}a};{\mathbb{R}})
. Note that
\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}\,}\nolimits j_{\mathbb{Z}}={\mathscr{H}}_{X}
is torsion-free; so we may and will often identify it with the
integral lattice in
\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}\,}\nolimits j_{\mathbb{R}}={\mathscr{H}}_{X}^{\mathbb{R}}
.
Similarly, When
\{\tilde{\gamma}_{i}\}_{i}
consists of integral
currents,
{\mathscr{H}}\big((X^{{}^{\prime}a},\nu,{\mathfrak{s}}),\{\tilde{\gamma}_{i}\}_{i}\big)
embeds in
{\mathscr{H}}^{\mathbb{R}}\big((X^{{}^{\prime}a},\nu,{\mathfrak{s}}),\{\tilde{\gamma}_{i}\}_{i}\big)
as an orbit under the
{\mathscr{H}}_{X}\subset{\mathscr{H}}_{X}^{\mathbb{R}}
-action.
{\mathbb{R}}
-coefficient versions of composition
maps
*
and
\operatorname{c}_{\{h_{i}\}_{i}}
are similarly defined and have
similar properties.

Lemma 2.14
.

Fix
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}
. For any
\tilde{\gamma}_{i},\tilde{\gamma}_{i}^{\prime}\in{\mathscr{Z}}^{\mathbb{R}}_{(Y_{i},\nu_{i},{\mathfrak{s}}_{i})}
, the metric on
Y_{i}
determines an isomorphism

I_{\mathscr{H}}
from
{\mathscr{H}}^{\mathbb{R}}(Y_{i},\nu_{i},{\mathfrak{s}}_{i};\tilde{\gamma}_{i},\tilde{\gamma}_{i}^{\prime})
to

H_{2}(Y_{i};{\mathbb{R}})=H_{2}({\mathbb{R}}\times Y_{i};{\mathbb{R}})={\mathscr{H}}_{{\mathbb{R}}\times Y}^{\mathbb{R}}
as affine spaces under
H_{2}(Y_{i};{\mathbb{R}})
. In particular,
all the affine spaces
{\mathscr{H}}^{\mathbb{R}}(Y_{i},\nu_{i},{\mathfrak{s}}_{i};\tilde{\gamma}_{i},\tilde{\gamma}_{i}^{\prime})
are identified
via these isomorphisms. By concatenation (the real versions of

\operatorname{c}_{\{h_{i}\}_{i}}
above, which we shall denote by the same
notation), for any pair
\{\tilde{\gamma}_{i}\}_{i},,\{\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i}
, the metric on
X
determines an
isomorphism between the corresponding affine spaces
{\mathscr{H}}^{\mathbb{R}}\big((X^{{}^{\prime}a},\nu,{\mathfrak{s}}),\{\tilde{\gamma}_{i}\}_{i}\big)
,
{\mathscr{H}}^{\mathbb{R}}\big((X^{{}^{\prime}a},\nu,{\mathfrak{s}}),\{\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i}\big)
as
well. Moreover, given
\tilde{\gamma}_{i},\tilde{\gamma}_{i}^{\prime},\tilde{\gamma}_{i}^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathscr{Z}}^{\mathbb{R}}_{(Y_{i},\nu_{i},{\mathfrak{s}}_{i})}
and
[\tilde{C}]\in{\mathscr{H}}^{\mathbb{R}}(Y_{i},\nu_{i},{\mathfrak{s}}_{i};\tilde{\gamma}_{i},\tilde{\gamma}_{i}^{\prime})
,

[\tilde{C}^{\prime}]\in{\mathscr{H}}^{\mathbb{R}}(Y_{i},\nu_{i},{\mathfrak{s}}_{i};\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}_{i},\tilde{\gamma}_{i}^{\prime\prime})
,

I_{\mathscr{H}}([\tilde{C}]*[\tilde{C^{\prime}}])=I_{\mathscr{H}}([\tilde{C}])+I_{\mathscr{H}}([\tilde{C^{\prime}}]).

(2.2)

Proof.
Let
\tilde{C}
be a 2-current on
{\mathbb{R}}\times Y_{i}
representing and
element
[\tilde{C}]\in{\mathscr{H}}^{\mathbb{R}}(Y_{i},\nu_{i},{\mathfrak{s}}_{i};\tilde{\gamma}_{i},\tilde{\gamma}_{i}^{\prime})
, and let
h
be
a harmonic 2-form on
Y_{i}
representing an element in

[h]\in H^{2}(Y_{i};{\mathbb{R}})
. The integral of
\pi^{*}_{2}h
over
\tilde{C}
is
a real number depending only on the class
[\tilde{C}]
; so
in this way we have a map

I_{\mathscr{H}}\colon\thinspace{\mathscr{H}}^{\mathbb{R}}(Y_{i},\nu_{i},{\mathfrak{s}}_{i};\tilde{\gamma}_{i},\tilde{\gamma}_{i}^{\prime})\to\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}\,}\nolimits(H^{2}(Y_{i};{\mathbb{R}});{\mathbb{R}})\simeq H_{2}(Y_{i};{\mathbb{R}}).

It is straightforward to verify that the
H_{2}(Y_{i};{\mathbb{R}})
-action on
the space
{\mathscr{H}}^{\mathbb{R}}(Y_{i},\nu_{i},{\mathfrak{s}}_{i};\tilde{\gamma}_{i},\tilde{\gamma}_{i}^{\prime})
intertwines with
that on
H_{2}(Y_{i};{\mathbb{R}})
, as well as the composition rule (
2.2
).

\Box

The
{\mathbb{Z}}
-coefficients version of statement in the preceding lemma
does not hold. However, since when
\tilde{\gamma}_{i},\tilde{\gamma}_{i}^{\prime}\in{\mathscr{Z}}_{(Y_{i},\nu_{i},{\mathfrak{s}}_{i})}\subset{\mathscr{Z}}^{\mathbb{R}}_{(Y_{i},\nu_{i},{\mathfrak{s}}_{i})}
, the space
{\mathscr{H}}(Y_{i},\nu_{i},{\mathfrak{s}}_{i};\tilde{\gamma}_{i},\tilde{\gamma}_{i}^{\prime})
embeds in
{\mathscr{H}}^{\mathbb{R}}(Y_{i},\nu_{i},{\mathfrak{s}}_{i};\tilde{\gamma}_{i},\tilde{\gamma}_{i}^{\prime})
as an orbit under
the
H_{2}(Y_{i};{\mathbb{Z}})\subset H_{2}(Y_{i};{\mathbb{R}})
action, the
isomorphism
I_{\mathscr{H}}
above also identifies it as an

H_{2}(Y_{i};{\mathbb{Z}})
-orbit in
H_{2}(Y_{i};{\mathbb{R}})
; that is, an element

t_{\mathscr{H}}(\tilde{\gamma}_{i},\tilde{\gamma}_{i}^{\prime})
in
the orbit space
{\mathbb{T}}_{Y_{i}}:=H_{2}(Y_{i};{\mathbb{R}})/H_{2}(Y_{i};{\mathbb{Z}})
. Equip the
torus

{\mathbb{T}}_{Y_{i}}
with the
standard Euclidean metric. This map,

t_{\mathscr{H}}\colon\thinspace{\mathscr{Z}}_{(Y_{i},\nu_{i},{\mathfrak{s}}_{i})}\times{\mathscr{Z}}_{(Y_{i},\nu_{i},{\mathfrak{s}}_{i})}\to{\mathbb{T}}_{Y_{i}},

is continuous with respect to the
current topology on
{\mathscr{Z}}_{(Y_{i},\nu_{i},{\mathfrak{s}}_{i})}
, and is linear with respect to
\tilde{\gamma}_{i}^{\prime}-\tilde{\gamma}_{i}\in{\mathscr{Z}}(Y_{i})
, where
{\mathscr{Z}}(Y_{i})
is the
space of
exact integral 1-currents on
Y_{i}
. Thus, one has:

Lemma 2.15
.

Suppose
\tilde{\gamma}_{i},\tilde{\gamma}_{i}^{\prime}\in{\mathscr{Z}}_{(Y_{i},\nu_{i},{\mathfrak{s}}_{i})}
are sufficiently close in the sense that
t_{\mathscr{H}}(\tilde{\gamma}_{i},\tilde{\gamma}_{i}^{\prime})
falls in the ball
B_{0}(1/2)\subset{\mathbb{T}}_{Y_{i}}
. Then there is a distinguished element in
{\mathscr{H}}(Y_{i},\nu_{i},{\mathfrak{s}}_{i};\tilde{\gamma}_{i},\tilde{\gamma}_{i}^{\prime})
. The notion of
“sufficiently close” depends on the metric on
Y_{i}
, but the
distinguished element is independent of the
metric on
Y_{i}
.

Suppose a pair
\{\tilde{\gamma}_{i}\}_{i},,\{\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i}\in\prod_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}}{\mathscr{Z}}_{(Y_{i},\nu_{i},{\mathfrak{s}}_{i})}
are sufficiently close in the sense described above, and
let
h_{i}\in{\mathscr{H}}(Y_{i},\nu_{i},{\mathfrak{s}}_{i};\tilde{\gamma}_{i},\tilde{\gamma}_{i}^{\prime})
denote the
aforementioned distinguished element. Then the concatenation map

\operatorname{c}_{\{h_{i}\}_{i}}
defines a canonical isomorphism of affine spaces
under
{\mathscr{H}}_{X}
from
{\mathscr{H}}\big((X^{{}^{\prime}a},\nu,{\mathfrak{s}}),\{\tilde{\gamma}_{i}\}_{i}\big)
to
{\mathscr{H}}\big((X^{{}^{\prime}a},\nu,{\mathfrak{s}}),\{\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i}\big)
.

2.6
Some basic facts about t-curves

Analogs of the results below are well-known in the context of
symplectic field theory.

Proposition 2.16
.

Let
(X,\nu)
be an admissible pair, and let
\{(Y_{i},\nu_{i})\}_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}}
be the ending pairs. A t-curve

\mathbf{C}=[C,\tilde{C}]
with finite
\omega
-energy in
X^{{}^{\prime}a}
is asymptotic to some t-orbit

\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{i}=[\gamma_{i},\tilde{\gamma}_{i}]
in the pair
(Y_{i},\nu_{i})
,
\forall i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}
, in the following sense:

(i)

Given any sequence of positive numbers
\{L_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
with

\lim_{n}L_{n}\to\infty
,

\{(\tau_{-L_{n}}C)|_{\hat{Y}_{i}}\}_{n}
geometrically converges to

[0,\infty)\times\gamma_{i}
in
\hat{Y}_{i}\simeq[0,\infty)\times Y_{i}
. In fact,
for all sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
, there exists an

R(\varepsilon)>0
, such that
\mathop{\mathrm{dist}\,}\nolimits_{\hat{Y}_{i}}(\tau_{-L}C,[0,\infty)\times\gamma_{i})<\varepsilon
,
\forall L>R(\varepsilon)
.

(ii)

The sequence of currents
\{\tau_{-L_{n}}\tilde{C}\}_{n}
converges weakly to

\pi_{2}^{*}\tilde{\gamma}_{i}
, where
\pi_{2}\colon\thinspace\hat{Y}_{i}\simeq[0,\infty)\times Y_{i}\to Y_{i}
is the projection.

Proof.
We first show that the statements (i) and (ii) hold for a
subsequence of
\{L_{n}\}_{n}
.

The finite
\omega
-energy assumption in the definition of
t-curves implies that

\forall i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}
,

\sup_{L>0}\int_{C|_{\hat{Y}_{i,[L,L+1]}}}\omega<z_{i}\quad\text{for some $z_{i}>0$.}

(2.3)

Thus, by the admissibility condition on
\nu
we may apply
Proposition
2.4
to find a subsequence
\{R_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
of
\{L_{n}\}_{n}

with
\lim_{n\to\infty}R_{n}=\infty
, such that

\{\tau_{-R_{n}}C\}_{n}
converges geometrically over

[0,1]\times(Y_{i}\backslash\nu_{i}^{-1}(0))
to a
J_{i}
-holomorphic
subvariety
C_{i}
. Here,
J_{i}
is the almost complex structure
determined by the metric and the symplectic form
\nu_{i}+ds\wedge*_{3}\nu_{i}
on
[0,1]\times(Y_{i}\backslash\nu_{i}^{-1}(0))
. Because of (
2.3
) and the fact that

{\mathscr{F}}_{\nu}(C|_{\hat{Y}_{i}})<\infty
, we know
that
\int_{C_{i}}\omega<z_{i}
, and
\int_{C_{i}}\nu=0
. By Lemma

2.9
and the conditions that define a t-curve,
we know that
C_{i}=[0,1]\times\gamma_{i}
, where
\gamma_{i}
is
the underlying subvariety of a certain t-orbit. (The weights on
closed orbits will be determined later).

Observe that statement (a) below implies statement (b), which in turn
implies statement (c).

(a)

the geometric convergence of
\{\tau_{-R_{n}}C\}_{n}
over

[0,1]\times(Y_{i}\backslash\nu_{i}^{-1}(0))
to
[0,1]\times\gamma_{i}
.

(b)

the geometric convergence of
\{\tau_{-R_{n}}C\}_{n}
over

[0,1]\times Y_{i}
to
[0,1]\times\gamma_{i}
.

(c)

the geometric convergence of (a subsequence of)

\{\tau_{-R_{n}}C\}_{n}
over
{\mathbb{R}}^{+}\times Y_{i}
to
{\mathbb{R}}\times\gamma_{i}
. We use the same notation
\{\tau_{-R_{n}}C\}_{n}
to denote
the subsequence.

The statement (c) follows from statement (b) by a diagonalization
argument. To see why (a) implies (b), let
U_{\epsilon}\subset Y_{i}

denote the set of points with distance less or equal
\epsilon
from

\nu_{i}^{-1}(0)
. By (a), for any small
\varepsilon>0
,
\exists\,N(\varepsilon)
such that

\mathop{\mathrm{dist}\,}\nolimits_{[0,1]\times(Y_{i}\backslash U_{\varepsilon/2})}(\tau_{-R_{n}}C,[0,1]\times\gamma_{i})<\varepsilon/2\quad\forall n>N(\varepsilon).

On the
other hand,
\mathop{\mathrm{dist}\,}\nolimits_{[0,1]\times U_{\varepsilon/2}}(\tau_{-R_{n}}C,[0,1]\times\gamma_{i})<\varepsilon/2
. Together, this means

\mathop{\mathrm{dist}\,}\nolimits_{[0,1]\times Y_{i}}(\tau_{-R_{n}}C,[0,1]\times\gamma_{i})<\varepsilon

\forall n>N(\varepsilon)
.

We have previously established statement (a) for

\{\tau_{-R_{n}}C\}_{n}
. Thus, the above observation establishes
assertion (i) in the statement of the Proposition for
\{\tau_{-R_{n}}C\}_{n}
.
Next we establish assertion (ii) for
\{\tau_{-R_{n}}\mathbf{C}\}_{n}
.
Define the current
\tilde{\gamma}_{i}
such that

\pi_{2}^{*}\tilde{\gamma}_{i}
is the (weak) limit of the currents

\tau_{-R_{n}}\tilde{C}
over
[0,1]\times(Y_{i}\backslash\nu_{i}^{-1}(0))
. The currents
\tau_{-R_{n}}\tilde{C}
,
\pi_{2}^{*}\tilde{\gamma}_{i}
over

[0,1]\times(Y_{i}\backslash\nu_{i}^{-1}(0))
extend over
[0,1]\times Y_{i}
, since
[0,1]\times\nu_{i}^{-1}(0)
is a co-dimension 3
submanifold in
[0,1]\times Y_{i}
. Set
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{i}
to be the t-orbit
with underlying subvariety
\gamma_{i}
, and with associated current
\tilde{\gamma}_{i}
.

To complete the proof of this proposition, it now suffices
to show that
[0,1]\times\gamma_{i}
is the geometric limit
of
\{\tau_{-L_{n}}C|_{\hat{Y}_{i,[0,1]}}\}_{n}

for
any
sequence
\{L_{n}\}_{n}
with
\lim_{n\to\infty}L_{n}=\infty
. Suppose the contrary. Then there is a sequence

\{L_{n}\}_{n}
,
\lim_{n\to\infty}L_{n}=\infty
such that
\forall n
,

\tau_{-L_{n}}C|_{\hat{Y}_{i,[0,1]}}
has distance larger than

\varepsilon
from
[0,1]\times\gamma_{i}
for some small
\varepsilon>0
. The same argument via Theorem

2.4
as above shows that there is a subsequence

\{L^{\prime}_{n}\}_{n}
of
\{L_{n}\}_{n}
, such that
\lim_{n\to\infty}L_{n}^{\prime}\to\infty
and
\{\tau_{-L^{\prime}_{n}}C|_{\hat{Y}_{i,[0,1]}}\}_{n}

converging geometrically to
[0,1]\times\gamma_{i}^{\prime}
. Here,
\gamma_{i}^{\prime}
is the
underlying subvariety of a different t-orbit, and
\mathop{\mathrm{dist}\,}\nolimits_{Y_{i}}(\gamma_{i},\gamma_{i}^{\prime})>\varepsilon
. Without loss of generality, we
may choose
\{L_{n}^{\prime}\}_{n}
such that
L^{\prime}_{n}>3R_{n}
. By Proposition
2.4

again,
\{\tau_{-(2R_{n})}C\}_{n}
converges
geometrically to a pseudo-holomorphic variety
C^{\prime}\subset{\mathbb{R}}\times Y_{i}
asymptotic to
{\mathbb{R}}\times\gamma_{i}
and

{\mathbb{R}}\times\gamma_{i}^{\prime}
respectively on
the
-\infty
- and
+\infty
-ends. However,
\int_{C^{\prime}}\nu=0
,
and thus by Lemma
2.9
,
C^{\prime}={\mathbb{R}}\times\gamma_{i}
and

\gamma_{i}=\gamma^{\prime}_{i}
, contradicting our assumption on
\gamma_{i}^{\prime}
.

\Box

We say that a t-curve in a cylindrical admissible pair

({\mathbb{R}}\times Y_{i},\pi_{2}^{*}\nu_{i})
is
constant
if it is of the form

{\mathbb{R}}\times\boldsymbol{\gamma}
for some t-orbit
\boldsymbol{\gamma}
of

(Y_{i},\nu_{i})
.

Lemma 2.17
.

Let
(Y,{\mathfrak{s}}_{Y})
be a closed
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
3-manifold. Let
g
denote a
riemannian metric on
Y
, and let
(X,\nu)
denote a
cylindrical admissible pair with
X={\mathbb{R}}\times Y
. Let
{\mathfrak{s}}
be the
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
structure on

X={\mathbb{R}}\times Y
induced by
{\mathfrak{s}}_{Y}
. There exists a
constant
\hbar>0
such that any nonconstant t-curve

\mathbf{C}
of
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
-structure
{\mathfrak{s}}
in
(X,\nu)
has
{\mathscr{F}}_{\nu}(\mathbf{C})>\hbar
.

Proof.
By assumption,
\nu=\pi_{2}^{*}\nu_{Y}
, where
\nu_{Y}
is Hodge dual to
a regular Morse-Novikov 1-form. There are finitely many t-orbits
with a fixed
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
structure in
Y
(cf. e.g.
[
HL
]
).
Then without loss of generality, we may restrict to t-curves asymptotic to fixed
t-orbits
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\pm}
respectively in the
\pm\infty
-ends.

Suppose the lemma is false and there is a
sequence of nontrivial t-curves
\{\mathbf{C}_{n}\}_{n}
asymptotic to
t-orbits
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\pm}
on the
\pm\infty
-ends, such
that
{\mathscr{F}}_{\nu}(\mathbf{C}_{n})\to 0
. We claim that
\forall\varepsilon>0
,

\exists N
such that
\forall n>N
,
C_{n}
is of distance less than
\varepsilon
from

{\mathbb{R}}\times\gamma
for some t-orbit

\boldsymbol{\gamma}
. (
C_{n}
and
\gamma
respectively denote the
underlying subvariety of
\mathbf{C}_{n}
and
\boldsymbol{\gamma}
). It would then follow that
C_{n}
has
\gamma
as
both the
+\infty
- and
-\infty
ends, and
\pi_{2}(C_{n})

represents a trivial element in
H_{2}(Y;{\mathbb{Z}})
, since

H_{2}(N_{\varepsilon};{\mathbb{Z}})
is trivial. Here,
N_{\varepsilon}

denotes the tubular neighborhood of
{\mathbb{R}}\times\gamma
consisting
of points of distance less than
\varepsilon
from
{\mathbb{R}}\times\gamma
. Thus,

{\mathscr{F}}_{\nu}(C_{n})=0
and we saw from Lemma
2.9

that
C_{n}
must be constant, contradicting our assumption.

To prove the claim, again suppose the contrary, that there exists of
sequence of t-curves
\{\mathbf{C}_{n}\}_{n}
,
C_{n}\not\subset N_{\varepsilon}

\forall n
for any t-orbit
\boldsymbol{\gamma}

with the prescribed
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
structure.
Let
\Gamma\subset Y
denote the union of all underlying
subvarieties of such t-orbits and let

N^{\Gamma}_{\varepsilon}\subset X
be the set consisting of points with
distance less than
\varepsilon
from
{\mathbb{R}}\times\Gamma
. Any pseudo-holomorphic
variety
C_{n}
from the above sequence is not included in
N_{\varepsilon}^{\Gamma}
.
We may then choose
L_{n}\in{\mathbb{R}}

such that
(\tau_{L_{n}}C_{n})\cap(\{0\}\times Y)
contains a point with
distance larger than
\varepsilon
from
\Gamma
. By Theorem
2.4
,

\{\tau_{L_{n}}C_{n}\}_{n}
geometrically converges to a
nonconstant t-curve
\mathbf{C}
with

{\mathscr{F}}_{\nu}(\mathbf{C})=0
, again contradicting Lemma
2.9
.

\Box

3
Preliminaries: the
SW
side

This section consists of a minimal review of Seiberg-Witten theory
in the context of MCE’s as well as some setups and definitions.

Recall the definitions of
\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits(M)
and related notions in Section

1.4
(19), where
M
is a
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
3- or 4-manifold,
with spinor bundle

{\mathbb{S}}
in the 3-dimensional case or

{\mathbb{S}}^{+}\oplus{\mathbb{S}}^{-}
in the 4-dimensional case. Let
{\mathscr{C}}(M)
denote
\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits(M)\times\Gamma({\mathbb{S}})
in the 3-dimensional case, and
\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits(M)\times\Gamma({\mathbb{S}}^{+})
in the 4-dimensional case. This is said to be the
(Seiberg-Witten)
configuration space
, and an element of
{\mathscr{C}}(M)

is said to be a (Seiberg-Witten)
configuration
. The
gauge group
over

M
is
{\mathscr{G}}={\mathscr{G}}(M)=C^{\infty}(M;S^{1})
. It acts on the space
{\mathscr{C}}(M)

by gauge transformations: Recall in particular that an element
u\in C^{\infty}(M;S^{1})
defines
an automorphism on
\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits(\det{\mathbb{S}}^{+})\times\Gamma({\mathbb{S}}^{+})
(or

\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits(\det{\mathbb{S}})\times\Gamma({\mathbb{S}})
) by

u\cdot(A,\Psi)=(A-2u^{-1}du,u\cdot\Psi).

We use
{\mathscr{B}}(M)
to denote the quotient space

{\mathscr{B}}(M):={\mathscr{C}}(M)/{\mathscr{G}}
. This is called the quotient
configuration space, and its elements quotient configurations (or
gauge equivalence classes of configurations). The gauge equivalence of
a configuration
(A,\Psi)
is denoted by
[(A,\Psi)]
.

3.1
Setup and Assumptions

Let
Y
be a closed connected
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
3-manifold.
We first consider the cylindrical case
X={\mathbb{R}}\times Y
, or more
generally, consider
Z=I\times Y\subset X
for an interval
I\subset{\mathbb{R}}
. Let
{\mathbb{S}}_{X}={\mathbb{S}}^{+}\oplus{\mathbb{S}}^{-}
denote spinor bundle
over
X
, and
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
be an affine coordinate for the first
factor of
X={\mathbb{R}}\times Y
. Let
\mu_{Y}

be a closed 2-form on
Y
, and set
\mu=\pi^{*}_{2}\mu_{Y}
,
\pi_{2}\colon\thinspace{\mathbb{R}}\times Y\to Y
being the projection to the second factor. Let

{\mathfrak{q}}\in{\mathscr{P}}(Y)
, where
{\mathscr{P}}(Y)
denote
Y
’s version of the Banach space of
large tame perturbatons
{\mathscr{P}}
introduced in
[
KM
]
’s Theorem 11.6.1 and Definition 11.6.3.
As
explained in II.4.3 of
[
KM
]
, the Clifford action
\rho(\partial_{s})

defines an isomorphism
{\mathbb{S}}^{+}\simeq{\mathbb{S}}^{-}
, which in turn is
identified with the pull back of the spinor bundle
{\mathbb{S}}={\mathbb{S}}_{Y}
on

Y
. Meanwhile, a
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
-connection
A
on
{\mathbb{S}}^{+}
may be
written (via a gauge tranformation) in temporal gauge as a path of
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
connections

B(s)
on
{\mathbb{S}}
by way of Equation (4.8) of
[
KM
]
. In this manner, an
(A,\Psi)\in{\mathscr{C}}(X)
in temporal gauge on the cylinder
{\mathbb{R}}\times Y
can alternatively be expressed as a path
(B(s),\Phi(s))
in

{\mathscr{C}}(Y)
. By way of the latter interpretation of

(A,\Psi)
, each
{\mathfrak{q}}\in{\mathscr{P}}(Y)
determines a 4-dimensional
perturbation denoted by
\hat{{\mathfrak{q}}}
. (Cf. Equation (10.2)
of
[
KM
]
and the subsequent discussion.)
Also in terms of the aforementioned
temporal gauge
expression, the Seiberg-Witten equation
{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mu,\hat{{\mathfrak{q}}}}(A,\Psi)=0
can be written as a gradient flow equation:

\big(\frac{1}{2}\frac{dB}{ds},\frac{d\Phi}{ds}\big)=-{\mathfrak{F}}_{\mu_{Y}}(B,\Phi)-{\mathfrak{q}}(B,\Phi).

In the above,
{\mathfrak{F}}_{\mu_{Y}}
is as defined in
(
1.3
), regarded as a section of the tangent bundle over

{\mathscr{C}}(Y)
. The term
{\mathfrak{q}}
appearing in the preceding equation is
understood as another section of this bundle: As in
[
KM
]
, the same notation is used to denote both an element

{\mathfrak{q}}\in{\mathscr{P}}(Y)
and its image under the map
{\mathfrak{Q}}
in

[
KM
]
’s Theorem 11.6.1. Write

{\mathfrak{F}}_{\mu_{Y},{\mathfrak{q}}}={\mathfrak{F}}_{\mu_{Y}}+{\mathfrak{q}}.

This is the
formal gradient of the (perturbed) Chern-Simons-Dirac functional on
{\mathscr{C}}(Y)
:

\operatorname{CSD}_{\mu_{Y},{\mathfrak{q}}}(B,\Phi):=\operatorname{CSD}_{\mu_{Y}}(B,\Phi)+f_{\mathfrak{q}}(B,\Phi),

(3.1)

where

\begin{split}\operatorname{CSD}_{\mu_{Y}}(B,\Phi)&:=-\frac{1}{8}\int_{Y}(B-B_{0})\wedge(F_{B}+F_{B_{0}})+\frac{1}{2}\int_{Y}\langle\mbox{\mbox{$\partial\mkern-7.5mu\mbox{/}\mkern 2.0mu$}}_{B}\Phi,\Phi\rangle\,d\operatorname{vol}\\
&\qquad-\frac{i}{8}\int_{Y}\mu_{Y}\wedge(B-B_{0}).\\
\end{split}

(3.2)

In the above,
B_{0}\in{\mathscr{C}}(Y)
is a reference connection, and
f_{\mathfrak{q}}
is the

{\mathbb{R}}
-valued, gauge invariant function on the space

{\mathscr{C}}(Y)
with formal gradient
{\mathfrak{q}}
,
and with
f_{\mathfrak{q}}(B_{0},0)=0
. Given
(B,\Phi)\in{\mathscr{C}}(Y)
, we use

(\hat{B},\hat{\Phi})
to denote the element in
{\mathscr{C}}({\mathbb{R}}\times Y)
corresponding to the constant path at
(B,\Phi)
.
In particular, a solution
(B,\Phi)

to the 3-dimensional Seiberg-Witten equation
{\mathfrak{F}}_{\mu_{Y},{\mathfrak{q}}}(B,\Phi)=0
gives rise to an

{\mathbb{R}}
-invariant Seiberg-Witten solution:
{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mu,\hat{{\mathfrak{q}}}}(\hat{B},\hat{\Phi})=0
on
{\mathbb{R}}\times Y
.

Note that
\operatorname{CSD}_{\mu_{Y}}
is in general not gauge invariant, but is
invariant under the identity component of the gauge group, denoted
{\mathscr{G}}_{0}\subset C^{\infty}(Y,S^{1})
. It also depends implicitly on the choice of the reference
connection
B_{0}
. It is written as
\operatorname{CSD}^{B_{0}}_{\mu_{Y}}
when we
wish to emphasize this dependence. Similarly for
\operatorname{CSD}_{\mu_{Y},{\mathfrak{q}}}
. A further superscript
Y
is added, e.g.

\operatorname{CSD}_{\mu_{Y}}^{Y,B_{0}}=\operatorname{CSD}_{\mu_{Y}}
when needs for specifying the
3-manifold
Y
arise.

Now let
X
be a general
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
MCE with spinor bundle
{\mathbb{S}}_{X}={\mathbb{S}}^{+}\oplus{\mathbb{S}}^{-}
, and let
{\mathbb{S}}_{i}
denote the spinor
bundle on the ending
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
3-manifold
Y_{i}
.
Fix a reference connection

A_{0}\in\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits(X)
such that

A_{0}=\hat{B}_{0,i}

(3.3)

for a reference
connection
B_{0,i}\in\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits(Y_{i})

on each end
\hat{Y}_{i}\subset X
.

Definition 3.1
.

An
admissible
element
(A,\Psi)
of
{\mathscr{C}}(X)
is one with the following property:
There exists
(B_{i},\Phi_{i})\in{\mathscr{C}}(Y_{i})
such that
(A,\Psi)-(\hat{B_{i}},\hat{\Phi}_{i})
is in
L^{2}_{1}(T^{*}\hat{Y}_{i})\times L^{2}_{1,A_{0}}({\mathbb{S}}^{+})
over each end
\hat{Y}_{i}
.
In this case,

(B_{i},\Phi_{i})
is said to be the
Y_{i}
-end limit
of
(A,\Psi)
.

All elements
(A,\Psi)
of
{\mathscr{C}}(X)
are assumed to be
admissible in this article.

Remarks 3.2
.

(a) Note that the preceding definition of admissible
(A,\Psi)
does not depend on the
choice of the reference connection
A_{0}
.

(b) When
(A,\Psi)
is a Seiberg-Witten solution, the definition
of “
Y_{i}
-end limits of
(A,\Psi)
” above is consistent with the
notion of
Y_{i}
-end limits of general sections over
X
given in
Section
1.4
, by way
of a well-known elliptic bootstrapping argument; see e.g.
[
KM
]
.

This article concerns admissible solutions
(A,\Psi)=(A_{r},\Psi_{r})
to the Seiberg-Witten equation
{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mu_{r},\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}_{r}}(A_{r},\Psi_{r})=0
, where
\mu_{r}=r\nu+w_{r}
and

\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}_{r}
are as described in Theorem
1.8
.
The terms
w_{r}
and

\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}_{r}
are regarded as “additional perturbations” to the
dominating perturbation
r\nu
in the Seiberg-Witten equation;
they are ultimately inconsequential to
the
SW\Rightarrow Gr
story told in this article. They are chosen
to satisfy the conditions spelled out in Assumption
3.4

below. These conditions are meant to both
simplify the arguments yet remain sufficiently general
for the purposes of Floer theory (e.g. transversality and compactness). The subscripts
r

are sometimes dropped from
w_{r}
and
\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}_{r}
, as their
dependence on
r
is immaterial.

In order to describe the assumptions as well as for future reference, we list some basic facts on
[
KM
]
’s nonlocal perturbations:

Facts 3.3
.

1)
Given
Y
, there exists a constant
m
such that

\|{\mathfrak{q}}(B,\Phi)\|_{L^{2}}\leq m\|{\mathfrak{q}}\|_{\mathscr{P}}\,(\|\Phi\|_{L^{2}}+1).

(3.4)

for every
{\mathfrak{q}}\in{\mathscr{P}}(Y)
and
(B,\Phi)\in{\mathscr{C}}(Y)
. Here,

\|{\mathfrak{q}}\|_{\mathscr{P}}
denote the norm of
{\mathfrak{q}}
as an element in the
Banach space
{\mathscr{P}}(Y)
. (Cf. Equation (11.18) of
[
KM
]
.) Given an compact interval

I\subset{\mathbb{R}}
, let
Z
denote the corresponding
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}

cylindrical manifold
I\times Y
. Let
{\mathbb{S}}^{+}
denote the spinor
bundle over
Z
and fix a reference connection
A_{0}\in\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits({\mathbb{S}}^{+})
. Then there exists a continuous function over
{\mathbb{R}}
,
denoted

\hat{m}
, such that

\|\hat{{\mathfrak{q}}}(A,\Psi)\|_{L^{2}_{1,A}}\leq\|{\mathfrak{q}}\|_{\mathscr{P}}\,\hat{m}\,(\|(A-A_{0},\Psi)\|_{L^{2}_{1,A_{0}}}).

(3.5)

(See Definition 10.5.1 and Theorem 11.6.1 of
[
KM
]
. Note that
there is a misprint in Theorem 11.6.1 (iv). By a Sobolev inequality,
(
3.5
) is equivalent to
\|\hat{{\mathfrak{q}}}(A,\Psi)\|_{L^{2}_{1,A}}\leq\|{\mathfrak{q}}\|_{\mathscr{P}}\,\hat{{\rm m}}\,(\|(A-A_{0},\Psi)\|_{L^{2}_{1,A}})
for another continuous function
\hat{{\rm m}}
.)

2)
Let
Z
,
{\mathfrak{q}}
be as before, and let the Banach manifolds

{\mathscr{C}}_{k}(Z)
and the Banach bundles

{\mathscr{V}}_{k}(Z)\to{\mathscr{C}}_{k}(Z)
be as in
[
KM
]
’s Definition 10.1.1. (The
subscript
k
signifies
L^{2}_{k}
-completion.) For
k\geq 1
,
the map
{\mathfrak{Q}}
from
{\mathscr{P}}(Y)\times{\mathscr{C}}_{k}(Z)
to
{\mathscr{V}}_{k}(Z)
given
by
({\mathfrak{q}},c)\mapsto\hat{{\mathfrak{q}}}(c)
is a continuous map of Banach
manifolds which vanishes over
\{0\}\times{\mathscr{C}}_{k}(Z)\subset{\mathscr{P}}(Y)\times{\mathscr{C}}_{k}(Z)
. For
k\geq 2
, it is a smooth map.

3)
Let
X
be a
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
MCE with ending 3-manifolds

Y_{i}
. A nonlocal perturbation
\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}
on
X
, as defined
in Equation (24.2) of
[
KM
]
, is of the following
form: For every
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}
, there exists a compact interval
{\mathbb{I}}_{i}=[{\mathfrak{l}}_{i},{\mathfrak{l}}_{i}^{\prime}]\subset{\mathbb{R}}
and
{\mathfrak{q}}_{i},{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}_{i}\in{\mathscr{P}}(Y_{i})
such that

\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}
vanishes over
X_{\boldsymbol{{\mathfrak{l}}}}
; it restricts to

\hat{{\mathfrak{q}}}_{i}
over
\hat{Y}_{i,{\mathfrak{l}}_{i}^{\prime}}
, and over

\hat{Y}_{i,{\mathbb{I}}_{i}}
it takes the form

\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}=\chi_{i}(\text{{\small s}}_{i})\hat{{\mathfrak{q}}}_{i}+\lambda_{i}(\text{{\small s}}_{i})\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\prime}_{i},

(3.6)

where
\chi_{i}
is a
smooth cutoff function supported on
({\mathfrak{l}}_{i},\infty)
and equals

1
as
\text{{\small s}}_{i}>{\mathfrak{l}}^{\prime}_{i}
;
\lambda_{i}
is a smooth bump
function supported on
{\mathbb{I}}_{i}
and equals 1 on a strictly smaller
interval
{\mathbb{I}}^{\prime}_{i}\subset{\mathbb{I}}_{i}
. As usual,
\boldsymbol{{\mathfrak{l}}}
above denotes a
function from
{\mathfrak{Y}}
to
{\mathbb{R}}^{\geq 0}
given by
i\mapsto{\mathfrak{l}}_{i}
.

Assumption 3.4
.

Let
X
be a
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
MCE with ending 3-manifolds

Y_{i}
,
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}
. Assume that
w_{r}
,
\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}_{r}
satisfy the following conditions:

(1)

w_{r}
is a smooth closed 2-form depending smoothly on

r
, and
\|w_{r}\|_{C^{k+3}}<\varsigma_{w}
for some
k\geq 3
and an
r
-independent
positive constant
\varsigma_{w}
.

(2)

w_{r}
are in the cohomology class
4\pi c_{1}({\mathfrak{s}}_{X})
for all
r
;

(3)

Over each end
\hat{Y}_{i}\subset X
,
w_{r}=\pi_{2}^{*}w_{i,r}
for a closed 2-forms
w_{i,r}
on

Y_{i}
. If
\hat{Y}_{i}
is a
vanishing end, the closed form
w_{i,r}
is independent of
of
r
and is written as
w_{i}
.

(4)

\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}_{r}=\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}
is of the type given
in Equation (24.2) of
[
KM
]
. Adopt the notations from
Item 3) of Facts
3.3
, with the ingredients for constructing

\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}
(i.e.
{\mathfrak{q}}_{i}
,
{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}_{i}
,
{\mathfrak{l}}_{i}
,

{\mathfrak{l}}_{i}^{\prime}
,
\chi_{i}
,
\lambda_{i}
) understood to
(implicitly) depend on
r
. (A subscript
r
is added,
e.g.
{\mathfrak{q}}_{i}={\mathfrak{q}}_{i,r}
, when the
r
-dependence is
emphasized.) We require:

a)

{\mathfrak{q}}_{i}=0={\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}_{i}
when
\hat{Y}_{i}
is a Morse
end. In this case we formally set
{\mathfrak{l}}_{i}={\mathfrak{l}}_{i}^{\prime}=\infty
and

{\mathbb{I}}_{i}=\emptyset
.

b)

The triples
{\mathbb{I}}_{i}
,
\chi_{i}
,
\lambda_{i}

corresponding to different
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{v}
are related by
translations. Namely, they take the form of

{\mathbb{I}}_{i}=\tau_{{\mathfrak{l}}_{i}}{\mathbb{I}}_{0};\quad\chi_{i}=\tau_{{\mathfrak{l}}_{i}}^{*}\chi_{0};\quad\lambda_{i}=\tau_{{\mathfrak{l}}_{i}}^{*}\lambda_{0},

where
{\mathbb{I}}_{0}:=[0,\zeta_{0}]
,
\chi_{0},\lambda_{0}
are all independent of both
r
and
i
.

c)

{\mathfrak{l}}_{i}>\textup{l}_{i}^{+}

\forall i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{v}
and
r
, where
\textup{l}_{i}^{+}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
is as in Definition
1.6
. In
particular,
\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}
vanishes over
X^{\prime\prime}
.

d)

Abusing notation, let
\|\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}\|_{{\mathscr{P}}}:=\sum_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{v}}\big(\|{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}_{i}\|_{{\mathscr{P}}}+\|{\mathfrak{q}}_{i}\|_{{\mathscr{P}}}\big)
. There is an
r
-independent constant
\textsl{z}_{\mathfrak{p}}<1/8

such that

\|\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}\|_{{\mathscr{P}}}=\sum_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{v}}\big(\|{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}_{i}\|_{{\mathscr{P}}}+\|{\mathfrak{q}}_{i}\|_{{\mathscr{P}}}\big)<\textsl{z}_{\mathfrak{p}}\quad\forall r.

(5)

Let
k
be as in Item (1) above, and let
{\mathbb{I}}_{\epsilon}:=(-\epsilon,\zeta_{0}+\epsilon)\supset{\mathbb{I}}_{0}
be a slightly larger open interval containing

{\mathbb{I}}_{0}
. Denote
\upsilon_{i}(r):=\|\mu_{r}\|_{C^{k+3}(\hat{Y}_{i,{\mathfrak{l}}_{i}})}
. Fix an
r
-independent constant
\zeta_{\mathfrak{p}}<\zeta_{w}/8
. With the constants and functions
\zeta_{0}
,
\lambda_{0}
,
\chi_{0}
,
{\mathfrak{l}}_{i}
from Assumption
3.4

4b)-4c) fixed,
\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}
is determined by
{\mathfrak{q}}_{i}
and

{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}_{i}
. We imposes a stronger,
r
-dependent constraint
(cf. the upcoming remark) on their
sizes (controlled by

\|\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}_{r}\|_{\mathscr{P}}
) than that from Item 4d) above,
such that the following holds
for every
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{v}
and
r\geq 1
: Fix
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{v}
and drop the
subscript
i
for the rest of this item. Write
Z:={\mathbb{I}}_{0}\times Y
;
Z_{\epsilon}:={\mathbb{I}}_{\epsilon}\times Y
, and let

\hat{{\mathfrak{x}}}_{r}:=\chi_{0}\hat{{\mathfrak{q}}}_{r}+\lambda_{0}\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\prime}_{r}=\tau_{{\mathfrak{l}}_{r}}^{*}\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}_{r}
. Then give
r\geq 1
:

\|\hat{{\mathfrak{x}}}_{r}(A,\Psi)\|_{C^{k}_{A}(Z)}<\zeta_{\mathfrak{p}}

(3.7)

for any
(A,\Psi)\in{\mathscr{C}}(Z_{\epsilon})
that satisfies:

(i)

\|F_{A}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(Z_{\epsilon})}+\|\nabla_{A}\Psi\|^{2}_{L^{2}(Z_{\epsilon})}+\|\Psi\|^{4}_{L^{4}(Z_{\epsilon})}\leq 2^{8}\upsilon(r)^{2}
;

(ii)

Let
{\mathfrak{W}}
denote the space of
C^{k+3}
closed
2-forms on
Z_{\epsilon}
cohomologous to
\pi_{2}^{*}w
. Then
{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\rm w},\hat{{\mathfrak{x}}}_{r}}(A,\Psi)=0
for a
{\rm w}\in{\mathfrak{W}}
with
\|{\rm w}\|_{C^{k+3}(Z_{\epsilon})}\leq\upsilon(r)
.

Likewise,

\|\hat{{\mathfrak{q}}}_{r}(A,\Psi)\|_{C^{k}_{A}(Z)}<\zeta_{\mathfrak{p}}

(3.8)

for any
(A,\Psi)\in{\mathscr{C}}(Z_{\epsilon})
that satisfies both item
(i) above and

(ii’)

{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\rm w},\hat{{\mathfrak{q}}}_{r}}(A,\Psi)=0
for a
{\rm w}\in{\mathfrak{W}}
with

\|{\rm w}\|_{C^{k+3}(Z_{\epsilon})}\leq\upsilon(r)
.

Remarks 3.5
.

(a)
Item (5) in the preceding assumption is invoked only in Section

5
to make the contribution from
\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}

ignorable while performing pointwise estimates. It may likely be
removed or weakened with extra work, appealing to the weak maximum principle
in lieu of the strong-maximum-principle arguments in Section

5
.
The stronger upper bound on

\|\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}_{r}}\|_{{\mathscr{P}}}
mentioned in Item (5) may also be
weakened by imposing stronger
(
r
-dependent) lower bounds on
{\mathfrak{l}}_{i}
than those required by
Item 4c).

(b)
Here is a more detailed description of the stronger bound on

\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}_{r}
that is
required by Item (5) to guarantee the conditions (
3.8
) and
(
3.7
).
Fix
r\geq 1
. Given
{\mathfrak{q}}\in{\mathscr{P}}(Y)
, let
{\mathscr{M}}({\rm w};{\mathfrak{q}})

denote the space of gauge equivalences of
(A,\Psi)
satisfying
Condition (i) of Item (5) and the Seiberg-Witten equation

{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\rm w},\hat{{\mathfrak{q}}}}(A,\Psi)=0
. Given
{\mathfrak{q}},{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}\in{\mathscr{P}}(Y)
, let
{\mathscr{M}}({\rm w};{\mathfrak{q}},{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime})

denote the space of gauge equivalences of
(A,\Psi)
satisfying
Condition (i) of Item (5) and the Seiberg-Witten equation

{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\rm w},\hat{{\mathfrak{x}}}}(A,\Psi)=0
, where

\hat{{\mathfrak{x}}}:=\chi_{0}\hat{{\mathfrak{q}}}+\lambda_{0}\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\prime}
. Then both the space

\tilde{{\mathscr{M}}}_{r}:=\bigcup_{{\rm w},\|{\rm w}\|_{C^{k+3}}\leq\upsilon(r)}\bigcup_{{\mathfrak{q}},\|{\mathfrak{q}}\|_{\mathscr{P}}\leq\textsl{z}_{\mathfrak{p}}}{\mathscr{M}}({\rm w};{\mathfrak{q}})
and the space
\tilde{{\mathscr{M}}}^{\prime}_{r}:=\bigcup_{{\rm w},\|{\rm w}\|_{C^{k+3}}\leq\upsilon(r)}\bigcup_{{\mathfrak{q}},\|{\mathfrak{q}}\|_{\mathscr{P}}\leq\textsl{z}_{\mathfrak{p}}}\bigcup_{{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime},\|{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}\|_{\mathscr{P}}\leq\textsl{z}_{\mathfrak{p}}}{\mathscr{M}}({\rm w};{\mathfrak{q}},{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime})
are
represented by compact subspaces in
{\mathscr{C}}_{k+3}(Z)
. (To see this, repeat the elliptic
bootstrapping arguments in
[
KM
]
’s proofs of Theorems 10.7.1 and
5.2.1, with the condition (i) of Item (5) playing the role of
[
KM
]
’s
(10.15), and noting the following: First, the

C^{k+3}
-bound on
{\rm w}
in conditions (ii) and
(ii’) implies that such
{\rm w}
lies in a compact subspace in the
space of
L^{2}_{j}
closed 2-forms in cohomology class
\pi^{*}_{2}w
,

\forall\,0\leq j<k+3
. Second, instead of appealing to Condition
(ii) of Definition 10.5.1 as in
[
KM
]
’s proof of its Theorems
10.7.1, one makes use of its parametrized generalization
established in
[
KM
]
Theorems 11.6.1 (iii)-(iv).)
Let
{\mathscr{P}}(Y;\delta)\subset{\mathscr{P}}(Y)
denote the ball in

{\mathscr{P}}(Y)
of radius
\delta
centered at 0, and let

N_{\mathscr{V}}(\delta)
denote the tubular neighborhood of the
zero-section of
{\mathscr{V}}_{k+3}(Z_{\epsilon})
whose intersection with
the fiber of
{\mathscr{V}}_{k+3}(Z_{\epsilon})
over any given
(A,\Psi)\in{\mathscr{C}}_{k+3}(Z_{\epsilon})
, namely
L^{2}_{k+3,A}
, is a ball of
radius
\delta
. By the
Sobolev embedding theorem, there is a constant
\zeta>0
such that

\|\eta\|_{C^{k}_{A}(Z)}\leq\zeta\|\eta\|_{L^{2}_{k+3,A}(Z_{\epsilon})}
. According
to Fact
3.3
(2), there is an
\textsl{z}^{\prime}(r)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
such that
{\mathscr{P}}(Y;\textsl{z}^{\prime})\times(\tilde{{\mathscr{M}}}_{r}\cup\tilde{{\mathscr{M}}}^{\prime}_{r})\subset{\mathfrak{Q}}^{-1}N_{\mathscr{V}}(\zeta^{-1}\zeta_{\mathfrak{p}})
. We
require that
2\|\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}_{r}\|_{\mathscr{P}}
is smaller than every

i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{v}
’s version of
\textsl{z}^{\prime}(r)
.

(c)

See Remark
3.13
and
[
KLT1
]
-
[
KLT5
]
for motivations of the
condition Item (2) in the preceding assumption.

Remarks 3.6
.

(a)
The notions
{\mathscr{C}}(M)
,
{\mathscr{B}}(M)
are defined for possibly
disconnected 3- or 4-manifold
M
. The formula (
3.2
) for

\operatorname{CSD}_{\mu}
also applies when the 3-manifold
Y
is
disconnected. Though in
[
KM
]
the Banach space of large tame perturbations
{\mathscr{P}}
is defined
for connected 3-manifolds, it generalizes readily to the disconnected
case: Suppose
M=\bigcup_{k}M_{k}
is a closed
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}

3-manifold with connected components
M_{k}
. Take

{\mathscr{P}}(M):=\prod_{k}{\mathscr{P}}(M_{k})
. Then each
{\mathfrak{q}}=({\mathfrak{q}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathfrak{q}}_{k},\ldots)\in\prod_{k}{\mathscr{P}}(M_{k})={\mathscr{P}}(M)
defines a section of the tangent bundle
T{\mathscr{C}}(M)=\boxtimes_{k}T{\mathscr{C}}(M_{k})
over
{\mathscr{C}}(M)=\prod_{k}{\mathscr{C}}(M_{k})
: given

{\mathfrak{c}}=({\mathfrak{c}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathfrak{c}}_{k},\ldots)
in

\prod_{k}{\mathscr{C}}(M_{k})={\mathscr{C}}(M)
,

{\mathfrak{q}}({\mathfrak{c}}):=({\mathfrak{q}}_{1}({\mathfrak{c}}_{1}),,\ldots,{\mathfrak{q}}_{k}({\mathfrak{c}}_{k}),\ldots)\in\boxtimes_{k}T{\mathscr{C}}(M_{k})=T{\mathscr{C}}(M)
. As before, given a
reference connection
B_{0}\in{\mathscr{C}}(M)
, the function
f_{\mathfrak{q}}\colon\thinspace{\mathscr{C}}(M)=\prod_{k}{\mathscr{C}}(M_{k})\to{\mathbb{R}}
is defined to be the unique
gauge-invariant function with formal gradient
{\mathfrak{q}}
and with

f_{\mathfrak{q}}(B_{0},0)=0
. (So
f_{\mathfrak{q}}({\mathfrak{c}})=\sum_{k}f_{{\mathfrak{q}}_{k}}({\mathfrak{c}}_{k})
.) The properties (1)-(2) in Fact
3.3

remain valid for such generalized
{\mathfrak{q}}
when
M
is compact.
In this way, the definitions of
{\mathfrak{F}}_{\mu,{\mathfrak{q}}}
,
\operatorname{CSD}_{\mu,{\mathfrak{q}}}
both generalize to the case
of disconnected manifolds.

(b)
Let
X
be a MCE with ending 3-manifolds
Y_{i}
,
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}
and let
\mu
be a closed 2-form on

X
with the closed 2-form
\mu_{i}
on
Y
as it
Y_{i}
-end
limit for each
i
. Let
\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}
be a nonlocal perturbation of the type
given in Equation (24.2) of
[
KM
]
. For our applications, the
relevant disconnected 3-manifold

M=\bigcup_{k}M_{k}
usually consists of slices of
X
of the form

M_{k}=Y_{i_{k}:s_{k}}
. When the 3-manifold
M
under discussion is
clear from the context, we often abbreviate the
restriction of
\mu
to
M
as
\mu\big|=\mu\big|_{M}
. For any such

M
, the nonlocal perturbation
\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}
on
X
also induces
an element in the Banach space of large tame perturbations, denoted

\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}\big|_{M}
or simply
\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}\big|\in{\mathscr{P}}(M)
: In
the notation of (
3.6
),
\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}|_{Y_{i:s}}=\chi_{i}(s)\hat{{\mathfrak{q}}}_{i}+\lambda_{i}(s)\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\prime}_{i}\in{\mathscr{P}}(Y_{i})
. To make the notations less cumbersome, when
M
is
understood from the context,

f_{\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}|}
,
\operatorname{CSD}_{\mu|,\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}|}^{M}
are also
abbreviated respectively as
f_{\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}}
,
\operatorname{CSD}_{\mu,\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}}
.

3.2
Y_{i}
-end limits of admissible Seiberg-Witten
solutions

We begin with some general definitions and observations.

Definition 3.7
.

Let
Y
be a closed
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
3-manifold and
B_{0}\in\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits(Y)
be a reference connection as in
(
3.2
). A connection
B\in\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits(Y)
is said to be in a

Coulomb gauge
(with respect to
B_{0}
) if the following holds:

d^{*}(\delta B)=0,\quad\text{where $\delta B=B-B_{0}$.}

(3.9)

An element
(B,\Phi)\in{\mathscr{C}}(Y)
is said to be in a

Coulomb gauge
(with respect to
B_{0}
) if
B
is in a Coulomb
gauge.

Fix an orthonormal basis
\{[h_{k}]\}_{k}
of

H^{1}(Y;{\mathbb{Z}})
, where the latter space is equipped with the inner product
given by the Riemannian metric on
Y
. Let
h_{k}\in\Omega^{1}(Y)
denote the
harmonic representative of
[h_{k}]
. We say that a
B\in\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits(Y)

or a
(B,\Phi)\in{\mathscr{C}}(Y)
is in
the
normalized Coulomb gauge
(with
respect to
B_{0}
and the basis
\{[h_{k}]\}_{k}
) if in addition to
(
3.9
), it also satisfies

\text{$0\leq\Big\langle h_{k},\frac{i}{4\pi}\delta B\Big\rangle<1$ $\forall k$}.

(3.10)

For each
gauge equivalence class
[(B,\Phi)]\in\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits({\mathbb{S}}_{Y})\times\Gamma({\mathbb{S}}_{Y})\big)/{\mathscr{G}}
, there is a unique representative in the
normalized Coulomb gauge. We usually denote this particular
representative as
[(B,\Phi)]_{c}\in{\mathscr{C}}(Y)
.

Working in the normalized Coulomb gauge has the following advantage:
If
B
(or
(B,\Phi)
is in a normalized Coulomb gauge, then by a Gårding inequality,

\|\delta B\|_{L^{2}}\leq\zeta_{b}\big(\|(d+d^{*})\delta B\|_{L^{2}}+1\big)=\zeta_{b}\big(\|F_{B}-F_{B_{0}})\|_{L^{2}}+1\big)

(3.11)

for a positive constant
\zeta_{b}
depending only on the metric on

Y
. We shall need the following simple consequence of the preceding observations:

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
Y
be closed
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
3-manifold
Y
, and fix a reference
connection
B_{0}\in\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits(Y)
. Recall that
f_{\mathfrak{q}}
denotes the
gauge-invariant function on
{\mathscr{C}}(Y)
with formal gradient

{\mathfrak{q}}\in{\mathscr{P}}(Y)
and with
f_{\mathfrak{q}}(B_{0},0)=0
.
There is a constant
\zeta
depending only on the metric of
Y
such that
such that
\forall

{\mathfrak{q}}\in{\mathscr{P}}(Y)
and
(B,\Phi)\in{\mathscr{C}}(Y)
,

|f_{\mathfrak{q}}(B,\Phi)|\leq\zeta\|{\mathfrak{q}}\|_{\mathscr{P}}\,\big(\|F_{B}-F_{B_{0}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\|\Phi\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+1\big).\quad

Proof.
Since
f_{\mathfrak{q}}
is gauge-invariant, it suffices to consider the
case when
(B,\Phi)
is in the normalized Coulomb gauge. Write

\delta B:=B-B_{0}
as before and let

(B_{t},\Phi_{t}):=(B_{0}+t\delta B,t\Phi)
for
t\in[0,1]
. Then
according to (
3.11
) and (
3.4
),

\begin{split}|f_{\mathfrak{q}}(B,\Phi)|&\leq\|(\delta B,\Phi)\|_{L^{2}}\|{\mathfrak{q}}(B_{t},\Phi_{t})\|_{L^{2}}\quad\text{for a certain $t\in[0,1]$}\\
&\leq m\|{\mathfrak{q}}\|_{\mathscr{P}}\big(\zeta_{b}\|F_{B}-F_{B_{0}}\|_{L^{2}}+\zeta_{b}+\|\Phi\|_{L^{2}}\big)(\|\Phi_{t}\|_{L^{2}}+1)\\
&\leq\zeta\|{\mathfrak{q}}\|_{\mathscr{P}}\,\big(\|F_{B}-F_{B_{0}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\|\Phi\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+1\big).\end{split}

(3.12)

\Box

Returning now to the setting of this article: Let
(X,\nu)
be an admissible pair and let
(Y_{i},\nu_{i})
denote
the limiting pair of
(X,\nu)
of the
Y_{i}
-end. Let
\mu_{r}
,

\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}_{r}
be as in the statement of Theorem
1.8
, and
write
\mu_{i,r}=r\nu_{i}+w_{i,r}\in\Gamma(Y_{i})
for the

Y_{i}
-end limit of
\mu_{r}
. As previously mentioned, we frequently drop the subscript
r

when
Y_{i}
is a vanishing end, since in this case

\mu_{i,r}=w_{i}
is independent of
r
.
Let
(A,\Psi)=(A_{r},\Psi_{r})
be an admissible solution to the Seiberg-Witten
equations
{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mu_{r},\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}_{r}}(A,\Psi)=0
. The standard
compactness/properness results in
Seiberg-Witten theory (see e.g. §10.7 in
[
KM
]
) imply that
the
Y_{i}
-end limit of
(A,\Psi)
is a solution to the
3-dimensional Seiberg-Witten equations
{\mathfrak{F}}_{\mu_{i,r},{\mathfrak{q}}_{i}}(B_{i},\Phi_{i})=0
. Moreover,
(A,\Psi)\big|_{Y_{i:L}}

converges to
(B_{i},\Phi_{i})
in
C^{k}(Y_{i})
topology as
L\to\infty
.
In the remainder of this subsection, we gather some facts about
such 3-dimensional Seiberg-Witten
solutions that will be of use later. In what follows, all statements
concern only the pair of 3-manifold and harmonic 2-form

(Y_{i},\nu_{i})
, the closed 2-form and the nonlocal perturbation,

w_{i,r}
and
{\mathfrak{q}}_{i,r}
, over
Y_{i}
; they are not necessarily ending pairs and
Y_{i}
-end
limits of a 4-dimensional
(X,\nu)
,
w_{r}
,

\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}_{r}
. The expression
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{v}
or
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}

only serves to indicate whether
\nu_{i}\equiv 0
or
otherwise.
w_{i,r}
and
{\mathfrak{q}}_{i,r}
are assumed to satisfy the
uniform bounds
\|w_{i,r}\|_{C^{k+3}}\leq\varsigma_{w}
and

\|{\mathfrak{q}}_{i,r}\|_{\mathscr{P}}\leq\textsl{z}_{\mathfrak{p}}
for an integer
k\geq 2
. For simplicity we also
assume that the cohomology class
[w_{i,r}]
is independent of

r
, and that

{\mathfrak{q}}_{i,r}=0
when
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}
, as in Assumption

3.4
. (The preceding conditions hold if
w_{i,r}
and
{\mathfrak{q}}_{i,r}
arise as
the
Y_{i}
-end limits of certain 4-dimensional
w_{r}
and

\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}_{r}
that satisfy Assumption
3.4
.) The constants
mentioned below will depend on
w_{i,r}
,
{\mathfrak{q}}_{i,r}
only
through
\varsigma_{w}
,
\textsl{z}_{\mathfrak{p}}
, and
[w_{i,r}]
, not on the precise form of

w_{i,r}
,
{\mathfrak{q}}_{i,r}
.

The next lemma is more or less standard, and is usually the first step
in typical proofs of compactness results in Seiberg-Witten theory. A short sketch of proof
is nevertheless provided, both in order to keep track of the
r
-dependence of
various coefficients, and because some formulas appearing therein
will be used again later.

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
(Y_{i},\nu_{i})
,
{\mathfrak{q}}_{i}={\mathfrak{q}}_{i,r}
,

i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}

\varsigma_{w}
,
\textsl{z}_{\mathfrak{p}}
be as described above, and define

{\rm a}_{i}:=\begin{cases}0&\text{when $i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{v}$;}\\
1&\text{ when $i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}$. }\end{cases}

Let
\mu_{i,r}=r\nu_{i}+w_{i,r}
and let
(B_{i},\Phi_{i})=(B_{i,r},\Phi_{i,r})
be a solution to the 3-dimensional
Seiberg-Witten equation
{\mathfrak{F}}_{\mu_{i,r},{\mathfrak{q}}_{i}}(B_{i},\Phi_{i})=0
.
There exist an
r
-independent constants
\zeta_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{+}

such that

\|\Phi_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\zeta_{i}\,r^{{\rm a}_{i}/2}.

(3.13)

Let
k\geq 2
be as in Assumption
3.4
. In the case when
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{v}
and
(B_{i},\Phi_{i})
is in the normalized Coulomb gauge with respect to a
reference connection
B_{0,i}
,
\eta:=(\delta B_{i}=B_{i}-B_{0,i},\Phi_{i})
satisfies

\|\eta\|_{L^{2}_{k+4,B_{0,i}}}+\|\eta\|_{C^{k+2}_{B_{0,i}}}+\|\Phi_{i}\|_{L^{4}}\leq\zeta^{\prime}_{i}

for some

r
-independent positive constant

\zeta^{\prime}_{i}
. The constant
\zeta_{i}
is
determined by the metric
on
Y_{i}
and the norms of the
perturbations via
\|\nu_{i}\|_{C^{2}}
,
\|w_{i,r}\|_{C^{2}}\leq\varsigma_{w}
, and

\|{\mathfrak{q}}_{i}\|_{{\mathscr{P}}}\leq\textsl{z}_{\mathfrak{p}}
. The constant
\zeta^{\prime}_{i}
is determined by
all the above, plus
B_{0,i}
.

Proof.
The proof hinges on the 3-dimensional Weitzenböck
formula

\mbox{\mbox{$\partial\mkern-7.5mu\mbox{/}\mkern 2.0mu$}}_{B_{i}}^{2}\Phi_{i}=\nabla_{B_{i}}^{*}\nabla_{B_{i}}\Phi_{i}+\frac{R_{g}}{4}\Phi_{i}+\frac{1}{2}\rho(F_{B_{i}})\Phi_{i},

(3.14)

where
R_{g}
denotes the scalar curvature.
Applying this formula together with (
3.4
) to the identity

\|{\mathfrak{F}}_{\mu_{i,r}}(B_{i},\Phi_{i})\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=\|{\mathfrak{q}}_{i}(B_{i},\Phi_{i})\|_{L^{2}}^{2}
, one has:

\begin{split}&\frac{1}{4}\|F_{B_{i}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\|\nabla_{B_{i}}\Phi_{i}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\big\|\frac{i}{4}\rho(\mu_{i,r})-(\Phi_{i}\Phi_{i}^{*})_{0}\big\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\\
&\qquad+\int_{Y_{i}}\frac{R_{g}}{4}|\Phi_{i}|^{2}-\frac{i}{4}\int_{\hat{Y}_{[l,L]}}F_{B_{i}}\wedge*\mu_{i,r}\leq 4m^{2}\|{\mathfrak{q}}_{i}\|_{\mathscr{P}}^{2}\,\big(1+\|\Phi_{i}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\big).\\
\end{split}

Judicious applications of the triangle inequality then leads to

\begin{split}\frac{1}{8}\|F_{B_{i}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\|\nabla_{B_{i}}\Phi_{i}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}&+\frac{1}{2}\big\|\frac{i}{4}\rho(\mu_{i,r})-(\Phi_{i}\Phi_{i}^{*})_{0}\big\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\\
&\leq\zeta\big(\|\mu_{i,r}\|_{C^{0}}^{2}+\|R_{g}\|_{C^{0}}^{2}+m^{4}\|{\mathfrak{q}}_{i}\|_{\mathscr{P}}^{4}\big)+\zeta^{\prime},\end{split}

where
\zeta
,
\zeta^{\prime}
are constants independent of
anything. (For more details, see (
4.18
) and subsequent discussions
for 4-dimensional analogs.)

According to Assumption
3.4
, when
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{v}
,
\|\mu_{i,r}\|_{C^{0}}=\|w_{i,r}\|_{C^{0}}\leq\varsigma_{w}
and

\|{\mathfrak{q}}_{i}\|_{\mathscr{P}}\leq\textsl{z}_{\mathfrak{p}}
, and so it follows that

\|F_{B_{i}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\|\nabla_{B_{i}}\Phi_{i}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\|\Phi_{i}\|_{L^{4}}^{4}\leq\zeta_{v},

where
\zeta_{v}
is an
r
-independent constant determined by the
metric,
\varsigma_{w}
and
\textsl{z}_{\mathfrak{p}}
. With the preceding bound at
hand, a standard elliptic bootstrapping argument (see e.g. the proofs
of
[
KM
]
’s Lemma 5.1.2 and Theorem 10.7.1 for the 4-dimensional version) then leads to

\|\eta\|_{L^{2}_{k+4,B_{0,i}}}+\|\Phi_{i}\|_{C^{k+2}_{B_{0,i}}}\leq\zeta_{v}^{\prime},

where
\zeta_{v}^{\prime}
is a constant determined by the
metric,
B_{0}
,
\varsigma_{w}
and
\textsl{z}_{\mathfrak{p}}
. Together with the
preceding bound on
\|\Phi_{i}\|_{L^{4}}
, this verified the assertions of the lemma in the
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{v}
case.

In the case when
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}
,
{\mathfrak{q}}_{i}=0
and
\|\mu_{i,r}\|_{C^{2}}\leq r\|\nu_{i}\|_{C^{2}}+\varsigma_{w}
.
The preceding argument provides an
r
-dependent bound on
\|\Phi_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}}
that grows faster with
r
than what we
need. Meanwhile, in this case the right hand side of (
3.14
)
vanishes by the second line of the Seiberg-Witten equation
{\mathfrak{F}}_{\mu_{i,r}}(B_{i},\Phi_{i})=0
. Combining this resulting identity with
the first line of the Seiberg-Witten equation, one has a pointwise
inequality of the form

d^{*}d|\Phi_{i}|^{2}+|\nabla_{B_{i}}\Phi_{i}|^{2}+\frac{|\Phi_{i}|^{4}}{2}\leq\zeta r|\Phi_{i}|^{2},

where
\zeta
is a constant depending only on the metric,
\|\nu_{i}\|_{C^{2}}
, and
\varsigma_{w}
. This permits
a straightforward application of the strong maximum principle, which
leads to the asserted bound on
\|\Phi_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}}
.
\Box

The next lemma is an ingredient for the proof of Theorem

1.3
.

Suppose
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}
. Let
\sigma(x)=\sigma_{Y}(x)
denote the distance from
x\in Y_{i}
to

\nu_{i}^{-1}(0)
, and let
Y_{i,\delta}\subset Y_{i}

denote the set of all points with distance greater or equal to

\delta
from
\nu_{i}^{-1}(0)
. Over
Y_{i}-\nu_{i}^{-1}(0)
, the Clifford action of
\nu_{i}

splits the spinor bundle
{\mathbb{S}}
into a direct sum of
eigen-bundles,

{\mathbb{S}}=E\oplus E\otimes K^{-1}
, where
E
to be the summand
corresponding to the eigenvalue
-i|\nu_{i}|
, and

K^{-1}=\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}\,}\nolimits(*\nu_{i})\subset TY_{i}
is a subbundle of oriented
2-planes over
Y_{i}
. The Clifford action by
\nu_{i}/|\nu_{i}|^{-1}

endows
K^{-1}
with a complex structure. Write

\phi_{i}:=(2r)^{-1/2}\Phi_{i}=(\alpha,\beta)

in terms of the
preceding
decomposition. Let
\nabla_{B_{i}}\alpha
,
\nabla_{B_{i}}\beta

respectively denote the covariant derivatives of
\alpha\in\Gamma(E)
and
\beta\in\Gamma(E\otimes K^{-1})
with respect to
the connections induced fron
B_{i}
and the Levi-Civita connection.

Let

\tilde{\sigma}:=\sigma\chi(\sigma)+1-\chi(\sigma).

(3.15)

Note that
our assumption that
\nu_{i}
has nondegenerate zeros implies that
there exists a positive constant
\zeta_{i}
satisfying

\zeta_{i}^{-1}\tilde{\sigma}\leq|\nu_{i}|\leq\zeta_{i}\tilde{\sigma}.

(3.16)

Lemma 3.10
.

Adopt the assumptions and notations in Lemma
3.9
, and
suppose that
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}
. There is a positive constant
r_{0}>1
such that for all
r\geq r_{0}
, the following holds: Let
(B_{i},\Phi_{i})
be as in the
previous lemma, and
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}
. Then:

(1)

There exists an

r
-independent positive constants
z^{\prime}
,
z^{\prime\prime}
such that over
Y_{i}
,

\begin{split}|\phi_{i}|^{2}&\leq|\nu_{i}|+zr^{-1/3};\\
|\phi_{i}|^{2}&\leq|\nu_{i}|+z^{\prime}r^{-1}(\sigma^{-2}+1).\end{split}

(3.17)

(2)

There exist positive constants
\textsc{o}>100
,
c
,

c^{\prime}
,
\zeta^{\prime}
and

\zeta
that are independent of
r
and
(B,\Phi)
, such that
for
\delta_{0}:=\textsc{o}r^{-1/3}
,
the following pointwise estimates
hold on
Y_{i,\delta_{0}}
:

\displaystyle|\beta|^{2}\leq c\tilde{\sigma}^{-3}r^{-1}(|\nu|-|\alpha|^{2})+\zeta\tilde{\sigma}^{-5}r^{-2};

(3.18)

\displaystyle|\beta|^{2}\leq c^{\prime}\tilde{\sigma}^{-3}r^{-1}(|\nu|-|\phi_{i}|^{2})+\zeta^{\prime}\tilde{\sigma}^{-5}r^{-2}.

(3.19)

All the constants above (
\zeta
,
z
,
c
,
o

r_{0}
etc.) are determined
by
\|\nu_{i}\|_{C^{1}}
,
\varsigma_{w}
, and the metric on
Y_{i}
.

That
r
is no less than the constant
r_{0}
above will be a standing
assumption throughout
the rest
of this article.

Proof.
The estimates
(
3.17
) are the
3-dimensional analogs of Lemma 3.2 in
[
Ts
]
and follow from the
same proof. Similarly, a straightforward modification of the proof of
Proposition 3.1 in
[
Ts
]
, with the
dependence of constants therein taken into account, yields the
following:

There exist positive constants
r_{0}
,
o
,
c
,

c^{\prime}
,
\zeta^{\prime}
and

\zeta
, which depend only on by
\|\nu_{i}\|_{C^{1}}
,

\varsigma_{w}
, and the metric on
Y_{i}
,
such that for any
r\geq r_{0}
and

\delta\geq\textsc{o}r^{-1/3}/2=\delta_{0}/2
,
the following pointwise estimate
holds on
Y_{i,\delta}
:

|\beta|^{2}\leq\frac{c}{8}\delta^{-3}r^{-1}(|\nu|-|\alpha|^{2})+\zeta\,(2\delta)^{-5}r^{-2}.

Now, for a fixed
x\in Y_{i,\delta_{0}}
, we can apply the
preceding formulae for any choice of

\delta\geq\delta_{0}/2
, and thus obtaining infinitely many bounds on
the value
|\beta(x)|^{2}
. The inequality (
3.18
) is given by the
(
x
-dependent) choice by setting
\delta=\tilde{\sigma}/2
. (Note that for
any
x\in Y_{i,\delta_{0}}
,
\tilde{\sigma}/2\geq\delta_{0}/2
and so it is an
admissible choice of
\delta
).

The inequality (
3.19
) is a direct
consequence of (
3.18
).
\Box

Lemma 3.11
.

Adopt the assumptions and notations in Lemma
3.9
, and
when
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}
, suppose in addition that
r\geq r_{0}
, where
r_{0}>1
is as
in Lemma
3.10
.
There exist an
r
-independent constant
\textsl{z}_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{+}

such that

\|F_{B_{i}}\|_{L^{1}(Y_{i})}\leq\textsl{z}_{i}.

In the case when
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{v}
, the constant

\textsl{z}_{i}
above is determined by the metric on
Y_{i}
,

\varsigma_{w}
,and
\|{\mathfrak{q}}_{i}\|_{{\mathscr{P}}}\leq\textsl{z}_{\mathfrak{p}}
. In the case when
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}
, the constant

\textsl{z}_{i}
above is determined by the metric on
Y_{i}
,

\|\nu_{i}\|_{C^{1}}
,
[w_{i,r}]
,

\varsigma_{w}
, and the
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
structure
{\mathfrak{s}}_{i}
through the quantity

\wp_{i}:=\frac{\pi}{2}c({\mathfrak{s}}_{i})\cdot[*\nu_{i}].

Proof.
When
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{v}
, by the 3-dimensional Seiberg-Witten
equation
{\mathfrak{F}}_{\mu_{i,r,{\mathfrak{q}}_{i}}}(B_{i},\Phi_{i})=0
, one may bound

\int_{Y_{i}}|F_{B_{i}}|\leq\zeta_{1}(\|\Phi_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}+\varsigma_{w})+\zeta_{2}\|{\mathfrak{q}}_{i}(B_{i},\Phi_{i})\|_{L^{2}}
, where
\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2}
depend
only on the metric of
Y_{i}
. The asserted
L^{1}
-bound on

F_{B_{i}}
then follows from Lemma
3.9
and
(
3.4
).

Suppose now that
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}
. The proof in this case takes four
steps.

Step 1.

This step derives some pointwise bounds on

|F_{B_{i}}|
. By direct computation:

\displaystyle|2F_{B_{i}}-iw_{i,r}|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r\big((|\nu_{i}|-|\alpha|^{2}+|\beta|^{2})^{2}+4|\alpha|^{2}\,|\beta|^{2}\big)^{1/2}

(3.20)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r\big((|\nu_{i}|-|\phi_{i}|^{2})^{2}+4|\nu_{i}|\,|\beta|^{2}\big)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle r\Big||\nu_{i}|-|\phi_{i}|^{2}\Big|+2r|\nu_{i}|^{1/2}|\beta|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle r\Big||\nu_{i}|-|\phi_{i}|^{2}\Big|+\zeta^{\prime}r^{2}\tilde{\sigma}^{3}|\beta|^{2}+\zeta^{\prime\prime}\tilde{\sigma}^{-2}\quad\text{ over $Y_{i}$,}

(3.21)

where
\zeta^{\prime}
,
\zeta^{{}^{\prime\prime}}
are constants depending only on
\nu_{i}
. Thus, for
r\geq r_{0}
, one has:

|F_{B_{i}}|\leq\begin{cases}\zeta_{f}\,r\tilde{\sigma}+\zeta^{\prime}_{f}\,\tilde{\sigma}^{-2}&\text{ over
$Y_{i}$ by (\ref {17.1}) and (\ref {eq:Phi-ptws});}\\
\zeta_{0}r\Big||\nu_{i}|-|\phi_{i}|^{2}\Big|+\zeta^{\prime}_{0}\tilde{\sigma}^{-2}&\text{ over $Y_{i,\delta_{0}}$ by (\ref {17.2}) and (\ref {ineq:3d-beta'}).}\end{cases}

(3.22)

In the above,
r_{0}
and
\delta_{0}
are as in Lemma

3.10
, and
\zeta_{f}
,
\zeta_{f}^{\prime}
,
\zeta_{0}
,
\zeta_{0}^{\prime}

are constants depending only on
\nu_{i}
,
\varsigma_{w}
, and the metric.

Step 2.

Use the first line of
Seiberg-Witten equation
{\mathfrak{F}}_{\mu_{i,r}}(B_{i},\Phi_{i})=0
to write:

\begin{split}\frac{r}{2}&\int_{Y_{i}}\big(|\nu_{i}|\,(|\nu_{i}|-|\alpha|^{2}+|\beta|^{2})\big)\\
&\quad=\int_{Y_{i}}(iF_{B_{i}}-w_{i}/2)\wedge*\nu_{i}\\
&\quad=\big(2\pi c({\mathfrak{s}}_{i})-[w_{i}]/2\big)\cdot[*\nu_{i}]=4\wp_{i}-[w_{i}]\cdot[*\nu_{i}]/2.\end{split}

Since
r\int_{Y_{i}}\big(|\nu_{i}|(|\phi_{i}|^{2}-|\nu_{i}|)_{+}\big)\leq\zeta
by
Lemma
3.10
(1), the preceding formula implies that

r\int_{Y_{i}}|\nu_{i}|\Big||\nu_{i}|-|\phi_{i}|^{2}\Big|+2r\int_{Y_{i}}|\nu_{i}||\beta|^{2}\leq\zeta_{c},

(3.23)

where
\zeta_{c}
depends only on the metric,
\nu_{i}
,
\varsigma_{w}
,
[w_{i}]
, and
\wp_{i}
.

Step 3.

While (
3.23
) is useful for bounding

r\int_{Y_{i,\rho}}\Big||\nu_{i}|-|\phi_{i}|^{2}\Big|
for
\rho\geq\delta_{0}
, the bound is
hardly efficient when
\rho
is small. This step derives a better
integral bound for
r\Big||\nu_{i}|-|\phi_{i}|^{2}\Big|
near the zeros of
\nu_{0}
.

By assumption, the zero locus
\nu_{i}^{-1}(0)
is nondegenerate;
therefore it consists of finitely many points
\nu_{i}^{-1}(0)=\bigcup_{p}p
, and there exists a small positive
\rho_{0}<1
and a function
f
over
Y_{i}-Y_{i,\rho_{0}}
, such that
Y_{i}-Y_{i,\rho_{0}}
consists of mutually
disjoint balls
B_{p}(\rho_{0})
, and over
Y_{i}-Y_{i,\rho_{0}}=\bigcup_{p}B_{p}(\rho_{0})
,

df=*\nu_{i}
and
|f|\leq\frac{|\nu|\sigma}{2}+O(\sigma^{3})
. Recall also from
(
3.16
) that
\zeta_{i}\sigma\geq|\nu_{i}|\geq\zeta_{i}^{-1}\sigma
over
Y_{i}-Y_{i,\rho_{0}}
.

Let
p\in\nu_{i}^{-1}(0)
, and let
{\mathscr{W}}_{p}(\rho):=\displaystyle\frac{r}{2}\displaystyle\int_{B_{p}(\rho)}\Big(|\nu_{i}|\Big||\nu_{i}|-|\phi_{i}|^{2}\Big|\Big)
. The first line of the
Seiberg-Witten equation
{\mathfrak{F}}_{\mu_{r}}(B_{i},\Phi_{i})=0
, together with the second line of
(
3.22
), implies that for
\delta_{0}\leq\rho\leq\rho_{0}
and
r\geq r_{0}
,

\begin{split}{\mathscr{W}}_{p}(\rho)-\zeta_{1}\rho^{2}&\leq\frac{r}{2}\int_{B_{p}(\rho)}\big(|\nu_{i}|(|\nu_{i}|-|\phi_{i}|^{2}+2|\beta|^{2})\big)\\
&=\int_{\partial B_{p}(\rho)}f\,(iF_{B_{i}}-w_{i,r}/2)\\
&\leq\frac{\rho(1+\zeta^{\prime}\rho)}{2}\frac{d}{d\rho}{\mathscr{W}}_{p}(R)+\zeta_{2}\rho^{2},\\
\end{split}

(3.24)

where
\zeta_{1}
,
\zeta_{2}
,
\zeta^{\prime}
are constants depending only on the metric,
\nu_{i}
and
\varsigma_{w}
. Rewrite the preceding differential inequality as:

\frac{d}{d\rho}\big(e^{h}{\mathscr{W}}_{p}(R)\big)\geq-\zeta\rho,\quad\text{where $h:=-2\ln\big(\frac{\rho}{1+\zeta^{\prime}\rho}\big)$,}

and integrate from
\rho
to
\rho_{0}
. One has:

\rho^{-2}_{0}{\mathscr{W}}_{p}(\rho_{0})-\rho^{-2}{\mathscr{W}}_{p}(\rho)\geq-\zeta_{3}(\rho_{0}^{2}-\rho^{2}).

Thus, from (
3.23
) one has

{\mathscr{W}}_{p}(\rho)\leq\zeta_{4}\rho^{2}.

(3.25)

let
N\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\geq 0}
be the largest integer such that
\delta_{0}^{\prime}:=2^{-N+1}\rho_{0}\geq\delta_{0}
. (Therefore
2\delta_{0}\geq\delta_{0}^{\prime}\geq\delta_{0}
.) Write
A_{n}:=A(2^{-n+1}\rho_{0},2^{-n}\rho_{0})
. Then it follows from (
3.25
) that there is a constant

\zeta^{\prime\prime}
depending only on the metric,
\nu_{i}
and
\varsigma_{w}
, such that for all
n,r,
satisfying
0\leq n\leq N
and

r\geq r_{0}
,
r\int_{A_{n}}\Big||\nu_{i}|-|\phi_{i}|^{2}\Big|\leq\zeta^{\prime\prime}\,2^{-n}
. Consequently

r\int_{Y_{i,\delta_{0}^{\prime}}-Y_{i,\rho_{0}}}\Big||\nu_{i}|-|\phi_{i}|^{2}\Big|\leq\zeta_{b}

(3.26)

for a constant
\zeta_{b}
depending only on the metric,
\nu_{i}
and
\varsigma_{w}
.

Step 4.

Divide
Y_{i}
into the
three regions:
Y_{i}-Y_{i,\delta_{0}^{\prime}}
,
Y_{i,\delta_{0}^{\prime}}-Y_{i,\rho_{0}}
, and
Y_{i,\rho_{0}}
. Use the first line of the pointwise
bounds (
3.22
)
to estimate the
L^{1}
norm of
F_{B_{i}}
on the first region. Use the
second line of the pointwise bounds (
3.22
) for both the second and the third region;
and combine with the integral bounds (
3.26
) and (
3.23
)
respectively for the second and the third region. We have

\begin{split}\int_{Y_{i}}|F_{B_{i}}|&=\int_{Y_{i}-Y_{i,\delta_{0}^{\prime}}}|F_{B_{i}}|+\int_{Y_{i,\delta_{0}^{\prime}}-Y_{i,\rho_{0}^{\prime}}}|F_{B_{i}}|+\int_{Y_{i,\delta_{0}^{\prime}}}|F_{B_{i}}|\leq\textsl{z}_{i},\end{split}

where
\textsl{z}_{i}
is a constant depending only on
\nu_{i}
,
\varsigma_{w}
, and the
metric.
\Box

Lemma 3.12
.

Adopt the assumptions and notations in Lemma
3.9
. When

i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}
, assume in addition that
r\geq r_{0}
, where
r_{0}
is as in Lemma
3.11
.
There exist
r
-independent constants
\zeta_{i},\zeta_{i}^{\prime},z^{\prime}_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
with the
following significance:

Suppose that
(B_{i},\Phi_{i})=[(B_{i},\Phi_{i})]_{c}
is in the
normalized Coulomb gauge with respect to
B_{0,i}
. Then:

(a)

One has
the following bounds for
\delta B_{i}:=B_{i}-B_{i,0}
:

\|\delta B_{i}\|_{L^{1}(Y_{i})}\leq\zeta_{i}
and
\|\delta B_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}(Y_{i})}\leq\zeta^{\prime}_{i}r^{2\mathrm{a}_{i}/3}.

(b)

The values of various versions of
\operatorname{CSD}
functionals
at
(B_{i},\Phi_{i})
are
bounded as follows:

\begin{split}|\operatorname{CSD}_{0}&(B_{i},\Phi_{i})|+|\operatorname{CSD}_{w_{i,r}}(B_{i},\Phi_{i})|+|\operatorname{CSD}_{w_{i,r},{\mathfrak{q}}_{i}}(B_{i},\Phi_{i})|\\
&+r^{-\mathrm{a}_{i}/3}|\operatorname{CSD}_{\mu_{i,r}}(B_{i},\Phi_{i})|+r^{-\mathrm{a}_{i}/3}|\operatorname{CSD}_{r\nu_{i}}(B_{i},\Phi_{i})|\leq z_{i}^{\prime}r^{2\mathrm{a}_{i}/3}.\end{split}

In the case when
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{v}
, the constants

\zeta_{i},\zeta^{\prime}_{i},z^{\prime}_{i}
above are determined by the metric on
Y_{i}
,

\varsigma_{w}
,and
\|{\mathfrak{q}}_{i}\|_{{\mathscr{P}}}\leq\textsl{z}_{\mathfrak{p}}
. In the case when
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}
, the constants

\zeta_{i},\zeta^{\prime}_{i},z^{\prime}_{i}
above are determined by the metric on
Y_{i}
,

\|\nu_{i}\|_{C^{1}}
,

\varsigma_{w}
,
[w_{i}]
, and
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
structure
{\mathfrak{s}}_{i}
through the quantity
\wp_{i}
.

Proof.

(a)
When
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{v}
, the asserted
L^{\infty}
-bound on
\delta B_{i}
follows from Lemma
3.9
. The asserted
L^{1}
-bound on
\delta B_{i}

follows from Lemma
3.9
together with
the fact that
\|\delta B_{i}\|_{L^{1}}\leq\zeta\|\delta B_{i}\|_{L^{2}}
, where
\zeta
is a constant depending only on the
metric of
Y_{i}
.

When
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}
, combine the previous lemma and (
3.10
) to get:

\begin{split}\|\delta B_{i}\|_{L^{1}(Y_{i})}&\leq\zeta\|G\|_{L^{1}(Y_{i})}(\|F_{B_{i}}\|_{L^{1}(Y_{i})}+1)\\
&\leq\zeta_{i},\end{split}

where
G
denotes the Green’s function for
d+d^{*}
on
Y_{i}
. In
the above,
\zeta
and
\zeta_{i}
are
r
-independent constants:

\zeta
depends only on the metric of
Y_{i}
;

\zeta_{i}
is determined by the metric on
Y_{i}
,

\|\nu_{i}\|_{C^{1}}
,
[w_{i,r}]
,

\varsigma_{w}
, and
\wp_{i}
.

To get the
asserted
L^{\infty}
bound of
\delta B_{i}
, notice first that
Lemma
3.9
together with the Seiberg-Witten equation

{\mathfrak{F}}_{\mu_{i,r}}(B_{i},\Phi_{i})=0
imply that

\|F_{B}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\zeta r^{\mathrm{a}_{i}},

(3.27)

where
\zeta
is a constant depending only on
\|\nu_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}}
and
\varsigma_{w}
. The assumption that
(B_{i},\Phi_{i})
is in the normalized Coulomb
gauge implies that
\delta B_{i}=b_{h}+b
, where
b_{h}
is harmonic
and
b
is coexact. Moreover,

\|b_{h}\|_{L^{\infty}(Y_{i})}\leq\zeta_{h},

(3.28)

where
\zeta_{h}
only depends on the first betti number of

Y_{i}
. Meanwhile, using the fact that the Green’s function satisfies

|G(x,y)|\leq\zeta_{g}\mathop{\mathrm{dist}\,}\nolimits(x,y)^{-2}
, one has for a given
x\in Y_{i}
,

\begin{split}|b(x)|&\leq\zeta_{g}^{\prime}\int_{Y_{i}}\mathop{\mathrm{dist}\,}\nolimits(x,\cdot)^{-2}\big(|F_{B_{i}}|+|F_{B_{i,0}}|\big)\\
&\leq\zeta_{g}^{\prime}\int_{B_{x}(\rho)}\mathop{\mathrm{dist}\,}\nolimits(x,\cdot)^{-2}|F_{B_{i}}|\,+\zeta_{g}^{\prime}\int_{Y_{i}-B_{x}(\rho)}\mathop{\mathrm{dist}\,}\nolimits(x,\cdot)^{-2}|F_{B_{i}}|\,+\zeta^{\prime}.\end{split}

Using (
3.27
) for the first integral in the last line above,
and applying Lemma
3.11
to the second integral therein, we
have:

|b(x)|\leq\zeta_{1}r^{\mathrm{a}_{i}}\rho+\zeta_{2}\rho^{-2}+\zeta^{\prime},

where
\zeta_{1}
only depends on the metric,
\|\nu_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}}
and
\varsigma_{w}
, the constant
\zeta_{2}
only depends on
the metric and the constant
\textsl{z}_{i}
in Lemma
3.11
, and

\zeta^{\prime}
depends only on the metric and the choice of
B_{i,0}
.
Now take
\rho=r^{\mathrm{a}_{i}/3}
in the preceding inequality, and
combine with (
3.28
), we have
\|\delta B_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}(Y_{i})}\leq\zeta_{i}^{\prime}r^{2\mathrm{a}_{i}/3}
as asserted.

(b)
When
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{v}
, the asserted bounds on

\operatorname{CSD}_{0}=\operatorname{CSD}_{r\nu_{i}}
and
\operatorname{CSD}_{w_{i}}=\operatorname{CSD}_{\mu_{i,r}}
follows directly from Lemma
3.9
. The bound
on
\operatorname{CSD}_{w_{i},{\mathfrak{q}}_{i}}
follows from the preceding bound on

\operatorname{CSD}_{w_{i}}
, together with a bound on
|f_{{\mathfrak{q}}_{i}}(B_{i},\Phi_{i})|
. This bound is provided by combining
Lemma
3.8
and Lemma
3.9
.

Now suppose
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}
. Use the 3-dimensional Seiberg-Witten equation,
{\mathfrak{F}}_{\mu_{i,r}}(B_{i},\Phi_{i})=0
, Part (a) of the lemma and Lemma
3.11

to get:

\begin{split}|\operatorname{CSD}_{\mu}&(B_{i},\Phi_{i})|=\Big|-\frac{1}{8}\int_{Y}\delta B_{i}\wedge\big(F_{B_{0,i}}+F_{B_{i}}+i\mu\Big)\Big|\\
&\leq\frac{1}{8}\|\delta B_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}(Y_{i})}\|F_{B_{0,i}}+F_{B_{i}}\|_{L^{1}(Y_{i})}+\frac{1}{8}\|\delta B_{i}\|_{L^{1}(Y_{i})}\|\mu\|_{L^{\infty}(Y_{i})}\\
&\leq\zeta_{1}r^{2\mathrm{a}_{i}/3}+\zeta_{2}\|\mu\|_{L^{\infty}(Y_{i})},\end{split}

(3.29)

where
\zeta_{1}
,
\zeta_{2}
are constants determined by the
constants
\zeta_{i}
,
\zeta_{i}^{\prime}
from Part (a), and the constant

\textsl{z}_{i}
in Lemma
3.11
.
Setting
\mu
above to be
0
,
w_{i,r}
,
\mu_{i,r}
, and

r\nu_{i}
, we arrive at the asserted bounds for the values of
\operatorname{CSD}_{0}
,
\operatorname{CSD}_{w_{i}}=\operatorname{CSD}_{w_{i},{\mathfrak{q}}_{i}}
,
\operatorname{CSD}_{\mu_{i,r}}
and
\operatorname{CSD}_{r\nu_{i}}
respectively.
\Box

Remark 3.13
.

(1) Recall that any
(B_{i},\Phi_{i})\in{\mathscr{C}}(Y_{i})
may be written as
(B_{i},\Phi_{i})=u_{i}\cdot[(B_{i},\Phi_{i})]_{c}
for a gauge transformation
u_{i}\in{\mathscr{G}}
. The values of various
\operatorname{CSD}
functionals at
(B_{i},\Phi_{i})

may be expressed in terms of those at
[(B_{i},\Phi_{i})]_{c}
and the cohomology class of
u_{i}
,
[u_{i}]:=\frac{1}{2\pi}[-iu_{i}^{-1}du_{i}]\in H^{1}(Y_{i};{\mathbb{Z}})
, via the following identity:

\begin{split}&\operatorname{CSD}_{\mu,{\mathfrak{q}}_{i}}(B_{i},\Phi_{i})-\operatorname{CSD}_{\mu,{\mathfrak{q}}_{i}}([(B_{i},\Phi_{i})]_{c})\\
&\qquad=\operatorname{CSD}_{\mu}(B_{i},\Phi_{i})-\operatorname{CSD}_{\mu}([(B_{i},\Phi_{i})]_{c})\\
&\qquad=2\pi\big(\pi c_{1}({\mathfrak{s}}_{i})-\frac{[\mu]}{4}\big)\cdot[u_{i}],\end{split}

In particular, when
[w_{i,r}]=4\pi c_{1}({\mathfrak{s}}_{i})
, the bound for
|\operatorname{CSD}_{w_{i,r}}(B_{i},\Phi_{i})|+|\operatorname{CSD}_{w_{i,r},{\mathfrak{q}}_{i}}(B_{i},\Phi_{i})|
given in Part
(b) of the preceding lemma holds for
(B_{i},\Phi_{i})

in
arbitrary gauge
.

(2) Via a more involved argument, the
L^{\infty}
bound for
\delta B_{i}
in the preceding lemma
may be improved as
\|\delta B_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}(Y_{i})}\leq\zeta^{\prime}_{i}r^{\mathrm{a}_{i}/2}
, and accordingly, the bounds for the CSD functionals
may be improved as
|\operatorname{CSD}_{0}(B_{i},\Phi_{i})|+|\operatorname{CSD}_{w_{i,r}}(B_{i},\Phi_{i})|+|\operatorname{CSD}_{w_{i,r},{\mathfrak{q}}_{i}}(B_{i},\Phi_{i})|\leq z_{i}^{\prime}r^{\mathrm{a}_{i}/2}
. This argument makes use of 3-dimensional analogs
(which are simpler and stronger) of Lemma
6.16
and
Proposition
6.19
below.

3.3
t-convergence of large-
r
Seiberg-Witten solutions

Let
({\mathscr{X}},\omega)
be a symplectic 4-manifold
with a riemannian metric

g
and an almost complex structure
J
so that
|\omega|\,g=\omega(\cdot,J\cdot)
. Let
\mu_{r}^{+}
be defined in terms of
\omega
as in
Equation (
1.2
). Fix a
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
-structure on
{\mathscr{X}}
and
let
{\mathbb{S}}={\mathbb{S}}^{+}\oplus{\mathbb{S}}^{-}
be the associated spinor bundle. The
Clifford action of
\omega
splits
{\mathbb{S}}^{+}
into a direct sum of
eigen-bundles,
E\oplus E\otimes K^{-1}
, where
E
corresponds to
the eigenvalue
-|\omega|i

and
K^{-1}
is the anti-canonical
bundle. Given a
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
connection
A
on
{\mathbb{S}}^{+}
, let
A^{E}\in\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits(E)
denote
the connection that it induces on
E
. Conversely,
with the riemannian metric on
{\mathscr{X}}
fixed, an
A^{E}\in\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits(E)
uniquely determines
a
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
connection on
{\mathbb{S}}^{+}
, and we have an isomorphism

\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits({\mathbb{S}}^{+})\simeq\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits(E)
.

Definition 3.14
.

Let
({\mathscr{X}},\omega)
,
J
be as above. Let
\mathbf{C}:=[C,\tilde{C}]
be a weighted
J
-holomorphic subvariety in
{\mathscr{X}}
.
Let
{\mathscr{X}}_{1}\subset\cdots{\mathscr{X}}_{n}\subset{\mathscr{X}}_{n+1}\subset\cdots\subset{\mathscr{X}}
be a countable exhaustion of
{\mathscr{X}}
by open subsets with compact closure,
and let
\{(A_{n},\Psi_{n})\}_{n}
be a corresponding sequence in

\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits({\mathbb{S}}^{+})\times\Gamma({\mathbb{S}}^{+})
, where
(A_{n},\Psi_{n})
is
defined over
{\mathscr{X}}_{n}
. This sequence is said to
t-converge
to
\mathbf{C}
if the
following hold for any compact subset
\mathcal{K}\subset X-\omega^{-1}(0)
,

(1)

Let
\Psi^{E}_{n}
denote the
E
-component of
\Psi_{n}
. Then

(\Psi^{E}_{n})^{-1}(0)
are closed sets of Hausdorff dimension 2, and
\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathop{\mathrm{dist}\,}\nolimits_{\mathcal{K}}\big(C\cap\mathcal{K},(\Psi^{E}_{n})^{-1}(0)\cap\mathcal{K}\big)=0
;

(2)

\{\frac{i}{2\pi}F_{A_{n}^{E}}\big|_{\mathcal{K}}\}_{n}
converges weakly as
currents to
\tilde{C}|_{\mathcal{K}}
.

Note that the definition of t-convergence above only depends on the
gauge equivalence class of
(A_{n},\Psi_{n})
. Therefore, we shall often
refer to the “t-convergence” of gauge equivalence classes of configurations.

The notion of t-convergence is similarly defined in the
3-dimensional case: Let
M
be a
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
3-manifold and

\theta
be a nowhere vanishing harmonic 1-form on
M
. Replace

\omega
in the preceding definition by
*\theta
, and replace
C_{k}
by
orbits of the dual vector to
\theta
. Let
\Psi_{n}
be sections of
the spinor bundle
{\mathbb{S}}
instead, and assume that

(\Psi^{E}_{n})^{-1}(0)
now have Hausdorff dimension 1. Here,
E

denotes the first summand in
{\mathbb{S}}=E\oplus E\otimes K^{-1}
, where

K^{-1}
is the 2-plane bundle
\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}\,}\nolimits\theta\subset TM
, equipped
with a complex structure given by Clifford multiplication by
|\theta|^{-1}\theta
.

In either the 3-dimensional case
(M,\theta)
or the 4-dimensional
case
({\mathscr{X}},\omega)
described above,
A^{K}
will denote the
connection on
K^{-1}
induced from the Levi-Civita connection.
For our applications,
({\mathscr{X}},\omega)
typically takes the form
((X_{\bullet}\cap X_{0})^{\circ},2\nu^{+})
for an admissible pair
(X,\nu)
. (Recall from Section
1.4
the definition of

X_{0}:=X-\nu^{-1}(0)
and
X_{\bullet}
.) In the 3-dimensional case, typically

M=Y-\theta^{-1}(0)
