for a harmonic Morse 1-form on a closed

\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
3-manifold
Y
. In the former case, we say that a
sequence of configurations on
X

t-converges to a t-curve

\mathbf{C}
in
X_{\bullet}
if it t-converges on the interior of

X_{\bullet}\cap X_{0}
. In the latter case,
a sequence of configurations on
Y
is said to
t-converge to a
t-orbit
\boldsymbol{\gamma}
if it t-converges on
Y-\theta^{-1}(0)
.
In the 3-dimensional case, the assumption that the zero locus of

\theta
is nondegenerate implies the existence of a constant

\zeta
such that
|F_{A^{K}}|\leq\zeta\tilde{\sigma}^{-2}
, and therefore

\|F_{A^{K}}\|_{L^{1}(Y)}\leq\zeta^{\prime}
is finite. In the 4-dimensional
case, the assumption that

\nu^{-1}(0)
is non-degenerate, together with the asymptotic
condition of
\nu
, imply a similar pointwise bound for

|F_{A^{K}}|
and that
\|F_{A^{K}}\|_{L^{1}(X_{\bullet})}\leq\zeta^{\prime}\,|X_{\bullet}|
for a constant
\zeta^{\prime}
depending only on the metric and
\nu
. (Cf. Section
1.4
for the definition of
|X_{\bullet}|
.)

What follows is a restatement of Theorem
1.3
in weaker form.

Proposition 3.15
.

Adopt the notations and assumption in Theorem
1.3
.
Then there exists a t-orbit
\boldsymbol{\gamma}
on
Y-\theta^{-1}(0)
and a subsequence
of the gauge equivalence classes
\{[(B_{r},\Phi_{r})]\}_{r}
which t-converges to
\boldsymbol{\gamma}
.

Furthermore,
\int_{\tilde{\gamma}}\theta\geq 0
and equals

\frac{1}{2}(c_{1}({\mathfrak{s}})\cdot[\theta]-\zeta_{\theta})
, where

\zeta_{\theta}=\int_{Y}\frac{i}{2\pi}F_{A^{K}}\wedge\theta,

and
A^{K}
is the connection on
K^{-1}
induced by the
Levi-Civita connection. In
particular, sequences
\{(B_{r},\Phi_{r})\}_{r}
satisfying the assumption of Theorem
1.3

exist only when
c_{1}({\mathfrak{s}})\cdot[\theta]\geq\zeta_{\theta}
.

Proof.
As mentioned in the introduction, the claim about t-convergence is
a consequence of
[
Ts
]
’s Theorem
1.1
. Alternatively, it can
be proved by going through the proof in
[
Ts
]
, which
simplifies significantly in the 3-dimensional setting. (See
[
LT
]

Section 3 for the case when
\theta^{-1}(0)=\emptyset
). As the constituent
flow lines of
\boldsymbol{\gamma}
are flow lines of
-\check{\theta}
,

\int_{\tilde{\gamma}}\theta\geq 0
. By the t-convergence we also
have

\int_{\tilde{\gamma}}\theta=\lim_{r\to\infty}\int_{Y}\frac{i}{2\pi}F_{B^{E}_{r}}\wedge\theta=\frac{1}{2}(c_{1}({\mathfrak{s}})\cdot[\theta]-\zeta_{\theta}),

where
B^{E}_{r}
is the connection on
E
induced from
B_{r}
.
\Box

Remarks 3.16
.

Note the cohomological nature of
\zeta_{\theta}
. Remove small radius
\delta
balls centered at points in

\theta^{-1}(0)
, and denote the resulting 3-manifold with boundary

Y_{\delta}
. Then
\frac{i}{2\pi}F_{A^{K}}
defines a class
c_{1}(K^{-1})\in H^{2}(Y_{\delta};{\mathbb{Z}})
, while
\theta
defines a class
[\theta]
in

H^{1}(Y_{\delta},\partial Y_{\delta};{\mathbb{R}})\simeq H_{2}(Y_{\delta};{\mathbb{R}})
.
\zeta_{\theta}
is then the pairing
\zeta_{\theta}=c_{1}(K^{-1})\cdot[\theta]
. In the case when
\theta=df
for a circle-valued Morse function
f\colon\thinspace Y\to{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}
,

\zeta_{\theta}
is the maximal Euler characteristic among the regular
level surfaces of
f
. Note that the latter is invariant under
homotopy of
f
. Thus,
-\zeta_{\theta}\geq\|\theta\|_{T}
. In
fact,
-\zeta_{\theta}
often coincides with
\|\theta\|_{T}
, such
as in the case when
\theta
has no zeros. We believe that when

[\theta]
is an integral primitive class with its Poincaré dual
represented by a connected embedded minimal
\chi_{-}
surface,
-\zeta_{\theta}=\|\theta\|_{T}
. In the above,
\chi_{-}
is as defined in
[
Th
]
.

3.4
Strong t-convergence

Fix a closed connected
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
3-manifold
(Y,{\mathfrak{s}})
and let
{\mathbb{S}}

denote its associated

\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
bundle. Let
\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits=\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits({\mathbb{S}})=:\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits(Y)
denote the
space of
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
-connections on
{\mathbb{S}}
, and let

{\mathscr{C}}:=\{(A,\Psi)\}=\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits(Y)\times\Gamma(Y,{\mathbb{S}})
. Let

{\mathscr{G}}=C^{\infty}(Y,S^{1})
be the gauge group. We use the short
hand

{\mathscr{B}}={\mathscr{B}}_{Y}={\mathscr{B}}(Y)={\mathscr{C}}/{\mathscr{G}}
. Let
\Pi\colon\thinspace{\mathscr{C}}\to\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits

be the projection map taking
(B,\Phi)\in{\mathscr{C}}
to

B\in\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits
. It induces a map, also denoted
\Pi
, from
{\mathscr{B}}
to

\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits/{\mathscr{G}}
.

Definition 3.17
.

A sequence in
{\mathscr{B}}(Y)
is said to be
strongly t-convergent
if
it is t-convergent and its image under
\Pi
in
\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits(Y)/{\mathscr{G}}(Y)
is
convergent in the “current topology”, to be specified below.

The aforementioned topology is weaker than the customary
C^{\infty}

topology, and requires knowledge of some basic structure of

\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits/{\mathscr{G}}
to describe. To begin, note that
\pi_{0}({\mathscr{G}})=H^{1}(Y;{\mathbb{Z}})
is the
fundamental group of both
{\mathscr{B}}
and
\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits/{\mathscr{G}}
. Let

{\mathscr{G}}_{0}\subset{\mathscr{G}}
denote the identity component. The
universal cover of
\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits/{\mathscr{G}}
,
\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits/{\mathscr{G}}_{0}
, is an affine space under
\Omega^{1}(Y)/(d\Omega^{0}(Y))
. The latter has a bundle structure
by way of the following short exact sequence:

0\to H^{1}(Y;{\mathbb{R}})\to\Omega^{1}(Y)/(d\Omega^{0}(Y))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}d\Omega^{1}(Y)\to 0.

(3.30)

The deck transformations by
H^{1}(Y;{\mathbb{Z}}))
on
\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits/{\mathscr{G}}_{0}
preserves the
fibers,
H^{1}(Y;{\mathbb{R}})
,
of this fibration; it acts freely on
H^{1}(Y;{\mathbb{R}})
by
addition. (Recall that
H^{1}(Y;{\mathbb{Z}})
is torsion-free,
cf. e.g.
[
D
]
’s Exercise 7.22, and we may and will identify it with the
integral lattice in
H^{1}(Y;{\mathbb{R}})
.) Consequently,
\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits/{\mathscr{G}}
inherits the structure
of a torus bundle:

\begin{CD}H^{1}(Y;{\mathbb{R}})/H^{1}(Y;{\mathbb{Z}})@>{}>{}>\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits/{\mathscr{G}}\\
@V{\operatorname{d}}V{}V\\
\operatorname{B}^{2}({\mathfrak{s}}),\end{CD}

(3.31)

where
\operatorname{B}^{2}({\mathfrak{s}})
is the affine space under
\operatorname{B}^{2}:=d\Omega^{1}(Y)

consisting of all closed 2-forms with cohomology class

c_{1}({\mathfrak{s}})/2\in H^{2}(Y;{\mathbb{Z}})
modulo torsions, and
\operatorname{d}
is the
map sending the gauge-equivalence class
[B]\in\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits/{\mathscr{G}}

to the closed 2-form
\frac{i}{4\pi}F_{B}
. Note that by
Poincaré duality, the space
H^{1}(Y;{\mathbb{R}})/H^{1}(Y;{\mathbb{Z}})
is canonically
isomorphic to the
torus denoted as
{\mathbb{T}}_{Y}
in Section

2.5
; and we shall denote it by the same notation.
With the preliminaries out of the way, the
current topology
on
\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits/{\mathscr{G}}
is that induced by the current topology on the
base space,
\operatorname{B}^{2}({\mathfrak{s}})
, of (
3.31
), together with the
standard topology on the fiber,
{\mathbb{T}}_{Y}
, as a

b^{1}(Y)
-dimensional torus. Let
\operatorname{C}(Y)
,

\tilde{\operatorname{C}}(Y)
respectively denote the completion
of
\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits/{\mathscr{G}}
and
\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits/{\mathscr{G}}_{0}
(with
C^{\infty}

topology) with respect to the current topology, and let

{\mathscr{Z}}^{\mathbb{R}}({\mathfrak{s}})\supset\operatorname{B}^{2}({\mathfrak{s}})
denote the space of closed
1-currents with cohomology class

c_{1}({\mathfrak{s}})/2\in H^{2}(Y;{\mathbb{Z}})
modulo torsions. This space is an
affine space under
{\mathscr{Z}}^{\mathbb{R}}(Y)
, the space of exact
1-currents. (Recall the
notation convention from Section
2.5
.) We have fibrations:

\begin{CD}{\mathbb{T}}_{Y}@>{}>{}>\operatorname{C}(Y)\\
@V{\operatorname{d}}V{}V\\
{\mathscr{Z}}^{\mathbb{R}}({\mathfrak{s}}),\end{CD}\qquad\begin{CD}H^{1}(Y;{\mathbb{R}})@>{}>{}>\tilde{\operatorname{C}}(Y)\\
@V{\operatorname{d}}V{}V\\
{\mathscr{Z}}^{\mathbb{R}}({\mathfrak{s}}).\end{CD}

(3.32)

When
(Y_{i},\nu_{i})
is a Morse end,
\nu_{i}
together with the metric on
Y_{i}

determines an isomorphism from
{\mathscr{Z}}^{\mathbb{R}}({\mathfrak{s}})
to

{\mathscr{Z}}^{\mathbb{R}}_{(Y_{i},\nu_{i},{\mathfrak{s}}_{i})}
as affine spaces under
{\mathscr{Z}}^{\mathbb{R}}(Y)
, given by

\frac{i}{4\pi}F_{B}\mapsto\frac{i}{2\pi}F_{B^{E}}
.

Proof of Theorem
1.3
.

As explained in the
paragraph prior to statement of the Theorem, by the 3-dimensional
version of Theorem
1.1
, there is a subsequence of
\{(B_{r},\Phi_{r})\}_{r}
that t-converges to a t-orbit
\boldsymbol{\gamma}
. We
show that this subsequence is in fact strongly t-convergent.

Choose
b^{1}:=b^{1}(Y)
smooth closed
2-forms
\{\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{b^{1}}\}
whose cohomology classes
form a basis of
H^{2}(Y;{\mathbb{R}})
, and denote by
\{\xi_{k}^{*}\}_{k=1}^{b^{1}}
the
dual basis for
H^{1}(Y;{\mathbb{R}})
. Define a splitting
\Omega^{1}(Y)/d\Omega^{0}(Y)\to H^{1}(Y;{\mathbb{R}})
of the exact sequence (
3.30
) given by

a\mapsto\sum_{k}(\int_{Y}a\wedge\xi_{k})\,\xi_{k}^{*}.

Since
(B_{r},\Phi_{r})
are t-convergent, their curvature currents
already converge in
\operatorname{B}^{2}({\mathfrak{s}})
. Thus, by (
3.30
) it suffices to
check that there exists
u_{r}\in{\mathscr{G}}
such that
B_{r}^{\prime}=u_{r}\cdot B_{r}
satisfies

i\int_{Y}(B_{r}^{\prime}-B_{0})\wedge\xi_{k}\quad\text{converges as $r\to\infty$}

(3.33)

for all
k
. By Stokes’ theorem and the convergence
of
F_{B_{r}}
, for this purpose the choice of these 1-forms may be arbitrary.
By the 3-dimensional version of
Proposition 6.1 in
[
Ts
]
, (
3.33
)
can easily shown to be true if the
2-forms
\xi_{k}
are chosen to be supported on small neighborhoods of
1-cycles in
Y
, which are disjoint from
\alpha^{-1}_{r}(0)
and

\nu^{-1}(0)
for all sufficiently large
r
.

\Box

3.5
Relative homotopy classes and relative homology classes

Let
X
be
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
MCE, and use
{\mathbb{S}}={\mathbb{S}}_{X}={\mathbb{S}}^{+}\oplus{\mathbb{S}}^{-}
to denote its
associated spinor bundle. For each end
\hat{Y}_{i}
of

X
, fix a
{\mathfrak{c}}_{i}\in{\mathscr{B}}_{Y_{i}}
. Denote by

{\mathscr{B}}_{X}(\{{\mathfrak{c}}_{i}\}_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}})=\{(A,\Psi)\}/\sim
the quotient
configuration space, where
(A,\Psi)
is a
pair consisting of a
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
connection on
{\mathbb{S}}^{+}
and a

\Psi\in\Gamma({\mathbb{S}}^{+})
, such that
(A,\Psi)
is asymptotic to a
representative of
{\mathfrak{c}}_{i}
on the
\hat{Y}_{i}
-end. Here,
(A^{\prime},\Psi^{\prime})\sim(A,\Psi)
if they are related by a gauge transformation,
i.e.,

(A^{\prime},\Psi^{\prime})=u\cdot(A,\Psi)
for some
u\in C^{\infty}(X,S^{1})
. Given an
element in
{\mathscr{B}}_{X}(\{{\mathfrak{c}}_{i}\}_{i})
, its
relative homotopy
class
refers to the element in
\pi_{0}{\mathscr{B}}_{X}(\{{\mathfrak{c}}_{i}\}_{i})
given
by the path component it is in. In the case when
X={\mathbb{R}}\times Y

and
Y
is a
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
closed 3-manifold, given
{\mathfrak{c}}_{-},{\mathfrak{c}}_{+}\in{\mathscr{B}}_{Y}
, we use
\pi_{Y}({\mathfrak{c}}_{-},{\mathfrak{c}}_{+})
to denote the
set of relative homotopy classes of quotient configurations with

-\infty
-limit
{\mathfrak{c}}_{-}
and
+\infty
-limit
{\mathfrak{c}}_{+}
. This is
a torsor under the group
\pi_{1}({\mathscr{B}}_{Y})=H^{1}(Y;{\mathbb{Z}})
. More
generally, given any
X_{\bullet}\subset\overline{X}
, let

{\mathfrak{Y}}_{X_{\bullet}}:=\pi_{0}(\partial\overline{X_{\bullet}})\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathscr{B}}_{\partial\overline{X_{\bullet}}}:=\prod_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{X_{\bullet}}}{\mathscr{B}}_{Y_{i}}.

Given
\{{\mathfrak{c}}_{i}\}_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{X_{\bullet}}}\in{\mathscr{B}}_{\partial\overline{X_{\bullet}}}
, let

{\mathscr{B}}_{X_{\bullet}}(\{{\mathfrak{c}}_{i}\}_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{X_{\bullet}}})=\{(A,\Psi)\}/\sim\quad\text{and}\quad\pi_{0}{\mathscr{B}}_{X_{\bullet}}(\{{\mathfrak{c}}_{i}\}_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{X_{\bullet}}})

be respectively the direct generalization
of
{\mathscr{B}}_{X}(\{{\mathfrak{c}}_{i}\})
and
\pi_{0}{\mathscr{B}}_{X}(\{{\mathfrak{c}}_{i}\})
, with

(A,\Psi)
now required to either be asymptotic to or restricts to a representative of

{\mathfrak{c}}_{i}
, depending on whether
Y_{i}\subset\partial\overline{X_{\bullet}}
is a
ending 3-manifold of
X
or lies in the interior of
X
. The set

\pi_{0}{\mathscr{B}}_{X_{\bullet}}(\{{\mathfrak{c}}_{i}\})
is a torsor under
the group

\pi_{X_{\bullet}}:=H^{1}(\partial\overline{X_{\bullet}};{\mathbb{Z}})/\mathop{\mathrm{Im}\,}\nolimits(i^{*}).

Here,
i^{*}\colon\thinspace H^{1}(\overline{X_{\bullet}};{\mathbb{Z}})\to H^{1}(\partial\overline{X_{\bullet}};{\mathbb{Z}})
is the map which forms part
of the commutative diagram of long exact sequences below:

\begin{CD}\cdots H_{3}(\overline{X_{\bullet}},\partial\overline{X_{\bullet}};{\mathbb{Z}})@>{}>{}>H_{2}(\partial\overline{X_{\bullet}};{\mathbb{Z}})@>{i}>{}>H_{2}(X_{\bullet};{\mathbb{Z}})@>{j}>{}>H_{2}(\overline{X_{\bullet}},\partial\overline{X_{\bullet}};{\mathbb{Z}})\cdots\\
@V{\iota_{PD}}V{}V@V{\iota_{PD}}V{}V@V{\iota_{PD}}V{}V@V{\iota_{PD}}V{}V\\
\cdots H^{1}(X_{\bullet};{\mathbb{Z}})@>{i^{*}}>{}>H^{1}(\partial\overline{X_{\bullet}};{\mathbb{Z}})@>{d}>{}>H^{2}(\overline{X_{\bullet}},\partial\overline{X_{\bullet}};{\mathbb{Z}})@>{j^{*}}>{}>H^{2}(X_{\bullet};{\mathbb{Z}})\cdots.\end{CD}

(3.34)

The rows above are parts of relative exact sequences of the pair

(\overline{X_{\bullet}},\partial\overline{X_{\bullet}})
, and the vertical maps

\iota_{PD}
are the Poincaré
duality maps.
As a result of the above commutative diagram, we have a chain of
canonical isomorphisms:

\begin{split}\pi_{X_{\bullet}}&=H^{1}(\partial\overline{X_{\bullet}};{\mathbb{Z}})/\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}\,}\nolimits d\simeq\mathop{\mathrm{Im}\,}\nolimits(d)\\
&\quad=\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}\,}\nolimits j^{*}\simeq\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}\,}\nolimits j\subset H_{2}(X_{\bullet};{\mathbb{Z}}),\end{split}

where the first isomorphism

H^{1}(\partial\overline{X_{\bullet}};{\mathbb{Z}})/\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}\,}\nolimits d\simeq\mathop{\mathrm{Im}\,}\nolimits(d)
is
induced from
d
, and the second isomorphism
\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}\,}\nolimits j^{*}\simeq\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}\,}\nolimits j
is the inverse of the
Poincaré duality map.

Remark 3.18
.

Note that when
X_{\bullet}=X^{{}^{\prime}a}
and
(
1.6
) holds, the long exact sequence in first row in
(
3.34
) agrees with the
{\mathbb{K}}={\mathbb{Z}}
-version of the long exact
sequence in the first row in
(
2.1
) (but with each showing a different portion of the long exact
sequence). Thus, recalling the defintion of
{\mathscr{H}}_{X}
from Section

2.5
, from (
3.34
) we have a canonical
isomorphism

\operatorname{h}\colon\thinspace\pi_{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{\mathscr{H}}_{X}\quad\text{
under the assumption (\ref {b_1=0}).}

More generally, applying
(
3.34
) to the case
X_{\bullet}=X^{{}^{\prime}a}
yields a map,
\operatorname{h}
, from
\pi_{X}\simeq\pi_{X^{{}^{\prime}a}}\simeq\mathop{\mathrm{Im}\,}\nolimits(d)\subset H^{2}(\overline{X^{{}^{\prime}a}},\partial\overline{X^{{}^{\prime}a}};{\mathbb{Z}})

to
{\mathscr{H}}_{X}\subset H_{2}(X^{{}^{\prime}a},\partial X^{{}^{\prime}a};{\mathbb{Z}})
via

j^{\prime}\circ\iota_{PD}^{-1}
, where
j^{\prime}\colon\thinspace H_{2}(X^{{}^{\prime}a};{\mathbb{Z}})\to H_{2}(X^{{}^{\prime}a},\partial X^{{}^{\prime}a};{\mathbb{Z}})
is part of the relative long exact
sequence of the pair
(X^{{}^{\prime}a},\partial X^{{}^{\prime}a})
.

Parallel to the relative homology classes discussed in Section

2.5
, concatenation defines maps among sets of relative
homotopy classes in analogy to the operations
*
,

\operatorname{c}_{\{h_{i}\}_{i}}
in Section

2.5
, and we use the same notations for them: Assigned to
every
(c,c^{\prime})\in\pi_{Y}({\mathfrak{c}}_{-},{\mathfrak{c}})\times\pi_{Y}({\mathfrak{c}},{\mathfrak{c}}_{+})

is an element
c^{\prime}*c\in\pi_{Y}({\mathfrak{c}}_{-},{\mathfrak{c}}_{+})
. (This is nothing but
the composition in the fundamental groupoid of
{\mathscr{B}}_{Y}
. In
general, we use
\pi(M;a,b)
to denote set of morphisms from
a
to
b
in the fundamental
groupoid of a topological space
M
. Namely, it is the set of relative homotopy classes of paths in
M

from
a
to
b
.) Fix

X_{\bullet}\subset\overline{X}
and two arbitrary elements
{\mathfrak{c}}=\{{\mathfrak{c}}_{i}\}_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{X_{\bullet}}},{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}=\{{\mathfrak{c}}_{i}^{\prime}\}_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{X_{\bullet}}}
in

{\mathscr{B}}_{\partial\overline{X_{\bullet}}}
. Then corresponding to every
c\in\pi_{\partial\overline{X_{\bullet}}}({\mathfrak{c}},{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}):=\prod_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{X_{\bullet}}}\pi_{Y_{i}}({\mathfrak{c}}_{i},{\mathfrak{c}}_{i}^{\prime})
, there is an
isomorphism (the concatenation map):

\operatorname{c}_{c}\colon\thinspace\pi_{0}{\mathscr{B}}_{X_{\bullet}}({\mathfrak{c}})\to\pi_{0}{\mathscr{B}}_{X_{\bullet}}({\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}).

(3.35)

This is used to make sense of Condition (2)
in the statement of Theorem
1.8
.

Lemma 3.19
.

(a) Given any
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}
and
a strongly converging sequence
\{{\mathfrak{c}}_{i,r}\}_{r}
, there is an

r_{0}>1
with the following significance: There is a
distinguished element
o_{Y_{i}}({\mathfrak{c}}_{i,r},{\mathfrak{c}}_{i,r^{\prime}})
in
\pi_{Y_{i}}({\mathfrak{c}}_{i,r},{\mathfrak{c}}_{i,r^{\prime}})

for any pairs of
r
,
r^{\prime}>r_{0}
. The number
r_{0}
depends on the
metric on
Y_{i}
, but the distinguished element is independent of
it.

(b) Let
\{\boldsymbol{{\mathfrak{c}}}_{r}|\,\boldsymbol{{\mathfrak{c}}}_{r}\in\Pi_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}}{\mathscr{B}}_{Y_{i}}\}_{r}
be a sequence satisfying condition (1) of
Theorem
1.8
. Namely,
\boldsymbol{{\mathfrak{c}}}_{r}=({\mathfrak{c}}_{i,r})_{i}
is
such that
{\mathfrak{c}}_{i,r}={\mathfrak{c}}_{i}\in{\mathscr{B}}_{Y_{i}}
is independent of
r

when
\hat{Y}_{i}
is a vanishing end,
and
\{{\mathfrak{c}}_{i,r}\}_{r}\subset{\mathscr{B}}_{Y_{i}}
is a strongly t-converging sequence when

\hat{Y}_{i}
is a Morse end. Let
r_{0}>1
be such that for any
pair of
r,r^{\prime}>r_{0}
, and any
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}
, the distinguished element
o_{Y_{i}}({\mathfrak{c}}_{i,r},{\mathfrak{c}}_{i,r^{\prime}})
from
item (a) is defined. Set
o_{Y_{i}}({\mathfrak{c}}_{i,r},{\mathfrak{c}}_{i,r^{\prime}}):=1\in\pi_{1}({\mathscr{B}}_{Y_{i}})=\pi_{Y_{i}}({\mathfrak{c}}_{i,r},{\mathfrak{c}}_{i,r^{\prime}})
when
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{v}
. Let
c(r,r^{\prime}):=\big(o_{Y_{i}}({\mathfrak{c}}_{i,r},{\mathfrak{c}}_{i,r^{\prime}})\big)_{i}\in\prod_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}}\pi_{Y_{i}}({\mathfrak{c}}_{i,r},{\mathfrak{c}}_{i,r^{\prime}})
. Then the concatenation map
\operatorname{c}_{c(r,r^{\prime})}
defines a canonical isomorphism from

\pi_{0}{\mathscr{B}}_{X}(\boldsymbol{{\mathfrak{c}}}_{r})
to

\pi_{0}{\mathscr{B}}_{X}(\boldsymbol{{\mathfrak{c}}}_{r^{\prime}})
.

Proof.
Item (b) in the statement of the lemma is a direct consequence of
Item (a). Item (a) follows from Item (a) of the next lemma, together with the
following observations:

(i) Recall the map
\Pi\colon\thinspace{\mathscr{B}}_{Y}\to\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits/{\mathscr{G}}\subset\operatorname{C}(Y)

from the previous subsection. For every pair
{\mathfrak{c}},{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}\in{\mathscr{B}}_{Y}
,
The map
\Pi
induces an isomorphism

\Pi_{*}\colon\thinspace\pi_{Y}({\mathfrak{c}},{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime})\to\pi\,(\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits/{\mathscr{G}};\Pi{\mathfrak{c}},\Pi{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime})=\pi\,(\operatorname{C}(Y);\Pi{\mathfrak{c}},\Pi{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}).

Thus, to assign a distinguished element in
\pi_{Y}({\mathfrak{c}},{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime})
, it suffices to assign distinguished elements in
\pi\,(\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits/{\mathscr{G}};\Pi{\mathfrak{c}},\Pi{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime})
or
\pi(\operatorname{C}(Y);\Pi{\mathfrak{c}},\Pi{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime})
.

(ii) By definition,
\{{\mathfrak{c}}_{r}\}_{r}\subset{\mathscr{B}}_{Y}
is strongly
convergent iff
\{\Pi{\mathfrak{c}}_{r}\}_{r}\subset\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits/{\mathscr{G}}\subset\operatorname{C}(Y)
converges
in the current topology.
\Box

The upcoming lemma is the
SW
counterpart of Lemma
2.15
on
the
Gr
side. In preparation for stating it, we introduce a
counterpart of the map
t_{\mathscr{H}}
in Section
2.5
.

Let
\tilde{c}\colon\thinspace{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits/{\mathscr{G}}
be a path from
b
to
b^{\prime}

in
\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits/{\mathscr{G}}
. This corresponds to an element
[A]
in

\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits\,(X;b,b^{\prime})/C^{\infty}(X,S^{1})
, where
X={\mathbb{R}}\times Y
,
and

\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits\,(X;b,b^{\prime})\subset\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits\,(X)
denotes the space of
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
connections
on
{\mathbb{S}}^{+}
asymptotic respectively to representatives of
b,b^{\prime}
on its
-\infty
- and
+\infty
-end. We extend the space

\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits\,(X;b,b^{\prime})/C^{\infty}(X,S^{1})
to a space, denoted
\operatorname{C}(X;b,b^{\prime})
, that corresponds to the space of paths in
\operatorname{C}(Y)
from
b

to
b^{\prime}
, where
b,b^{\prime}\in\operatorname{C}(Y)\supset\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits/{\mathscr{G}}
. Let
A
be a unitary
connection on
\det{\mathbb{S}}^{+}
representing
[A]
, and let
h
be a
harmonic 2-form on
Y
representing an element
[h]
in
H^{2}(Y;{\mathbb{R}})
. The
integral
\int_{X}\frac{iF_{A}}{4\pi}\wedge\pi_{2}^{*}h
is finite and
depends only on the relative homotopy class of
\tilde{c}
,
[h]
, and the
metric on
Y
. In this way, the metric on
Y
determines a map

\jmath_{c}\colon\thinspace\pi\,(\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits/{\mathscr{G}};b,b^{\prime})\to H^{1}(Y;{\mathbb{R}})=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}\,}\nolimits(H^{2}(Y;{\mathbb{R}}),{\mathbb{R}}).

In fact,
\jmath_{c}
extends to a map from
\pi\,(\operatorname{C}(Y);b,b^{\prime})
to

H^{1}(Y;{\mathbb{R}})
, where
b,b^{\prime}\in\operatorname{C}(Y)
. The latter map is also
denoted by
\jmath_{c}
, and may be regarded as a SW analog of the map

I_{\mathscr{H}}
introduced in Lemma
2.14
. A more precise
description of the relation between
\jmath_{c}
and
I_{\mathscr{H}}
will
be given prior to Lemma
3.23
.
It follows straightforwardly from construction that
\jmath_{c}

intertwines with the
\pi_{1}(\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits/{\mathscr{G}})=\pi_{1}(\operatorname{C}(Y))\simeq H^{1}(Y;{\mathbb{Z}})
-action on
\pi\,(\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits/{\mathscr{G}};b,b^{\prime})
(or more generally
\pi_{1}(\operatorname{C}(Y),b,b^{\prime})
) and the
H^{1}(Y;{\mathbb{Z}})\subset H^{1}(Y;{\mathbb{R}})
-action on
H^{1}(Y;{\mathbb{R}})
. Thus, with the metric on

Y
fixed, one has
a canonical isomorphism from
\pi\,(\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits/{\mathscr{G}};b,b^{\prime})
(or more generally
\pi_{1}(\operatorname{C}(Y),b,b^{\prime})
) to an orbit of
H^{1}(Y;{\mathbb{Z}})
in
H^{1}(Y;{\mathbb{R}})
. Let

t_{\operatorname{C}}(b,b^{\prime})
denote the corresponding element in the orbit
space,
H^{1}(Y;{\mathbb{R}})/H^{1}(Y;{\mathbb{Z}})={\mathbb{T}}_{Y}
. Summarizing, we have a map

t_{\operatorname{C}}\colon\thinspace\operatorname{C}(Y)\times\operatorname{C}(Y)\to{\mathbb{T}}_{Y}.

This map is an analog of the map
t_{\mathscr{H}}
defined after Lemma

2.14
in Section
2.5
.
It is continuous with respect to the current topology on
\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits/{\mathscr{G}}
and
\operatorname{C}(Y)
. Furthermore, recall from (
3.31
) and (
3.32
)
the structure of
\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits/{\mathscr{G}}
,
\operatorname{C}(Y)
as
{\mathbb{T}}_{Y}
-bundles,
t_{\operatorname{C}}

is
{\mathbb{T}}_{Y}
-equivariant both with respect to the
{\mathbb{T}}_{Y}
-action
on the right factor of
\operatorname{C}(Y)\times\operatorname{C}(Y)
, and the
{\mathbb{T}}_{Y}^{op}
-action on the left factor of
\operatorname{C}(Y)\times\operatorname{C}(Y)
(or
(\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits/{\mathscr{G}})\times(\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits/{\mathscr{G}})
). (
{\mathbb{T}}_{Y}^{op}
above refers to the inverse
action of the torus group.) It maps the diagonal to the identity
element
0\in{\mathbb{T}}_{Y}
.

Lemma 3.20
.

(a) Suppose
b,b^{\prime}\in\operatorname{C}(Y)
are sufficiently close in the sense that
t_{\operatorname{C}}(b,b^{\prime})
falls in the ball
B_{0}(1/2)\subset{\mathbb{T}}_{Y}
. Then there is a distinguished element
\tilde{o}_{Y}(b,b^{\prime})
in
\pi(\operatorname{C}(Y);b,b^{\prime})
. This distinguished element is independent of the
metric on
Y
, though the notion of being “sufficiently close” does.

(b) Fix
X_{\bullet}\subset\overline{X}
. Suppose a pair
\{b_{i}\}_{i},\{b^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i}\in\prod_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{X_{\bullet}}}\operatorname{C}(Y_{i})
are sufficiently close in the sense
described above. Given any
{\mathfrak{c}}_{i}\in\Pi^{-1}b_{i}
,

{\mathfrak{c}}_{i}^{\prime}\in\Pi^{-1}b_{i}^{\prime}
, let
o_{Y_{i}}({\mathfrak{c}}_{i},{\mathfrak{c}}_{i}^{\prime}):=(\Pi_{*})^{-1}\tilde{o}_{Y_{i}}(b_{i},b^{\prime}_{i})\in\pi_{Y_{i}}({\mathfrak{c}}_{i},{\mathfrak{c}}_{i}^{\prime})
. Then the concatenation map

\operatorname{c}_{\{o_{Y_{i}}({\mathfrak{c}}_{i},{\mathfrak{c}}_{i}^{\prime})\}_{i}}
defines a canonical isomorphism from
\pi_{0}{\mathscr{B}}_{X_{\bullet}}(\{{\mathfrak{c}}_{i}\}_{i})
to
\pi_{0}{\mathscr{B}}_{X_{\bullet}}(\{{\mathfrak{c}}_{i}^{\prime}\}_{i})
as affine spaces under
\pi_{X_{\bullet}}
.

Proof.
Item (a) follows directly from the discussion preceding to
the statement of the lemma. Item (b) generalizes Item (b) of the
Lemma
3.19
, and follows from Item (a) above together
with Part (i) in the proof of Lemma
3.19
.

\Box

Remark 3.21
.

The arguments in the proof of the preceding lemma
also establishes the following: A choice of basis for
H^{1}(Y;{\mathbb{Z}})
gives a
way of (simultaneously) identifying all sets of relative
homotopy classes
\pi_{Y}({\mathfrak{c}},{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime})
,
{\mathfrak{c}},{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}\in{\mathscr{B}}_{Y}
,
with
H^{1}(Y;{\mathbb{Z}})
(as affine spaces). (Recall that such a choice
(cf. (
3.10
)) is
required to define the normalized Coulomb gauge, and has been fixed
implicitly in this article.) To see this, observe that as explained in Part (i) in the proof of
Lemma
3.19
, it suffices to identify sets of relative
homotopy classes
\pi(\operatorname{C}(Y);b,b^{\prime})
,
with
H^{1}(Y;{\mathbb{Z}})
(as affine spaces) for every pair
b,b^{\prime}\in\operatorname{C}(Y)
.
This is equivalent to
(consistently) choosing a base point in
\pi(\operatorname{C}(Y);b,b^{\prime})
for each pair
b,b^{\prime}\in\operatorname{C}(Y)
. To do so, note
that a choice of basis for
H^{1}(Y;{\mathbb{Z}})
defines an isomorphism

i_{h}\colon\thinspace{\mathbb{R}}^{b^{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}H^{1}(Y;{\mathbb{R}}).

Recall also that
t_{\operatorname{C}}(b,b^{\prime})
corresponds to an isomorphism from
\pi(\operatorname{C}(Y);b,b^{\prime})
to an orbit of

H^{1}(Y;{\mathbb{Z}})
in
H^{1}(Y;{\mathbb{R}})
. We define the
base point in
\pi(\operatorname{C}(Y);b,b^{\prime})
to be the unique element
whose image under this isomorphism lies in
i_{h}\,([0,1)^{\times b^{1}})\subset H^{1}(Y;{\mathbb{R}})
. Denote this base point by
\tilde{o}_{Y}(b,b^{\prime})
, and given
{\mathfrak{c}},{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}\in{\mathscr{B}}_{Y}
, let

o_{Y}({\mathfrak{c}},{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}):=(\Pi_{*})^{-1}\tilde{o}_{Y}(\Pi{\mathfrak{c}},\Pi{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime})\in\pi_{Y}({\mathfrak{c}},{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime})

denote the corresponding base point in
\pi_{Y}({\mathfrak{c}},{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime})
.
The argument for Item (b) of Lemma

3.20
then implies that once a choice of basis is fixed for
every
H^{1}(Y_{i};{\mathbb{Z}})
, then for any given
X_{\bullet}
,
\pi_{0}{\mathscr{B}}_{X_{\bullet}}(\{{\mathfrak{c}}_{i}\}_{i})
,
\pi_{0}{\mathscr{B}}_{X_{\bullet}}(\{{\mathfrak{c}}_{i}^{\prime}\}_{i})
are identified for every pair

\{{\mathfrak{c}}_{i}\}_{i},\{{\mathfrak{c}}_{i}^{\prime}\}_{i}
in
{\mathscr{B}}_{\partial\overline{X_{\bullet}}}
.

Remark 3.22
.

Fix a reference connections
A_{0}
on
{\mathbb{S}}^{+}
as in
(
3.3
). Then for every given
X_{\bullet}
,
(A_{0},0)|_{X_{\bullet}}
determines a base
point in
\pi_{0}({\mathscr{B}}_{X_{\bullet}}(\{{\mathfrak{c}}_{0,i}\}_{i}))
, where
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{X_{\bullet}}
, and
{\mathfrak{c}}_{0,i}\in{\mathscr{B}}_{Y_{i}}
denotes the
restriction of
(A_{0},0)
to the
i
-th connected component of

\partial\overline{X_{\bullet}}
. This in turn defines an
isomorphism

\imath_{A_{0}}\colon\thinspace\pi_{0}({\mathscr{B}}_{X_{\bullet}}(\{{\mathfrak{c}}_{0,i}\}_{i}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\pi_{X_{\bullet}}

as affine spaces.

Thus, a choice of
A_{0}
determines, for any given
X_{\bullet}\subset\overline{X}
, a way of simultaneously identifying
\pi_{0}({\mathscr{B}}_{X_{\bullet}}(\{{\mathfrak{c}}_{i}\}_{i}))\simeq\pi_{X_{\bullet}}
for all
\{{\mathfrak{c}}_{i}\}_{i}\in{\mathscr{B}}_{\partial\overline{X_{\bullet}}}
: Given any
\{{\mathfrak{c}}_{i}\}_{i}\in\prod_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{X_{\bullet}}}{\mathscr{B}}_{Y_{i}}
, Let
h_{A_{0}}
denote the
isomorphism

h_{A_{0}}:=\imath_{A_{0}}\circ\operatorname{c}_{\{o_{Y_{i}}({\mathfrak{c}}_{i},{\mathfrak{c}}_{0,i})\}_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{X_{\bullet}}}}\colon\thinspace\pi_{0}({\mathscr{B}}_{X_{\bullet}}(\{{\mathfrak{c}}_{i}\}_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{X_{\bullet}}}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\pi_{X_{\bullet}}.

When
X_{\bullet}
is of the form
\hat{Y}_{i,I}
, this isomorphism
agrees with the isomorphism
\pi_{Y_{i}}({\mathfrak{c}},{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}H^{1}(Y_{i};{\mathbb{Z}})
introduced in Remark

3.21
under our assumptions on
A_{0}
.

We next compare the relative homology classes of
t-curves and the relative homotopy classes of Seiberg-Witten quotient
configurations. Recall from
Section
2.5
the definition of
{\mathscr{H}}^{\mathbb{R}}(X^{{}^{\prime}a},\nu,{\mathfrak{s}},\{\tilde{\gamma}_{i}\})
and various other notions. Let
c=[(A,\Psi)]\in{\mathscr{B}}_{X}(\{{\mathfrak{c}}_{i}\}_{i})
, with
{\mathfrak{c}}_{i}=[(B_{i},\Phi_{i})]\in{\mathscr{B}}_{Y_{i}}
. Assign to
c
the 2-current
\tilde{c}=\frac{i}{2\pi}F_{A^{E}}
on
X^{{}^{\prime}a}
. This
current determines a class in
{\mathscr{H}}^{\mathbb{R}}(X^{{}^{\prime}a},\nu,{\mathfrak{s}},\{\tilde{{\mathfrak{c}}}_{i}\}_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}})
, where

\tilde{{\mathfrak{c}}}_{i}=\frac{i}{2\pi}F_{B_{i}^{E}}\in{\mathscr{Z}}(Y_{i},\nu_{i},{\mathfrak{s}}_{i})\subset{\mathscr{Z}}^{\mathbb{R}}(Y_{i},\nu_{i},{\mathfrak{s}}_{i})
. This class only depends on the
relative homotopy class of
c
, and so in this way we have a map

{\mathfrak{h}}^{\prime}\colon\thinspace\pi_{0}{\mathscr{B}}_{X}(\{{\mathfrak{c}}_{i}\}_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}})\to{\mathscr{H}}^{\mathbb{R}}((X^{{}^{\prime}a},\nu,{\mathfrak{s}}),\{\tilde{{\mathfrak{c}}}_{i}\}_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}}).

This map intertwines with the
\pi_{X}
-action on
\pi_{0}{\mathscr{B}}_{X}(\{{\mathfrak{c}}_{i}\}_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}})
, and the
\operatorname{h}(\pi_{X})\subset{\mathscr{H}}^{\mathbb{R}}_{X}
-action on
{\mathscr{H}}^{\mathbb{R}}((X^{{}^{\prime}a},\nu,{\mathfrak{s}}),\{\tilde{{\mathfrak{c}}}_{i}\}_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}})
. (The map
\operatorname{h}\colon\thinspace\pi_{X}\to{\mathscr{H}}_{X}
is as defined in Remark

3.18
.) It is also natural with respect to
the concatenation maps on both sides. In the special case when
(X,\nu)=({\mathbb{R}}\times Y_{i},\pi_{2}^{*}\nu_{i})
,
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}
, is cylindrical,
the map

{\mathfrak{h}}^{\prime}\colon\thinspace\pi_{Y_{i}}({\mathfrak{c}},{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime})\to{\mathscr{H}}^{\mathbb{R}}(Y_{i},\nu_{i},{\mathfrak{s}}_{i};\tilde{{\mathfrak{c}}},\tilde{{\mathfrak{c}}}^{\prime})
by construction factors through a map

\underline{{\mathfrak{h}}}\colon\thinspace\pi\,(\operatorname{C}(Y_{i}),\Pi{\mathfrak{c}},\Pi{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime})\to{\mathscr{H}}^{\mathbb{R}}(Y_{i},\nu_{i},{\mathfrak{s}}_{i};\tilde{{\mathfrak{c}}},\tilde{{\mathfrak{c}}}^{\prime}):

{\mathfrak{h}}^{\prime}=\underline{{\mathfrak{h}}}\circ\Pi_{*}
. It follows from construction that in this case,

\jmath_{c}=\iota_{PD}\circ I_{{\mathscr{H}}}\circ\underline{{\mathfrak{h}}},

where
\iota_{PD}
again denotes the Poincaré map.

Next, consider the case when (
1.6
) holds. Recall from Remark
3.18
that

\pi_{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\rm h}}}{{\simeq}}{\mathscr{H}}_{X}
in this case.

Lemma 3.23
.

Assume that all vanishing ends of
(X,\nu)
has
zero first betti numbers. (See (
1.6
).) For each
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}
, let

\{{\mathfrak{c}}_{i,r}\}_{r}\in{\mathscr{B}}_{Y_{i}}
be a sequence that strongly
t-converges to a t-orbit
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{i}
, and for each
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{v}
, let
{\mathfrak{c}}_{i,r}={\mathfrak{c}}_{i}\in{\mathscr{B}}_{Y_{i}}
be independent of

r
.
Let
r_{0}>1
be as in Lemma
3.19
. Then:

(a) For all
r>r_{0}
, there is a canonical
isomorphism
{\mathfrak{h}}
from the set
\pi_{0}{\mathscr{B}}_{X}(\{{\mathfrak{c}}_{i,r}\}_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}})
to an

{\mathscr{H}}_{X}
-orbit in
{\mathscr{H}}^{\mathbb{R}}\big((X^{{}^{\prime}a},\nu),\{\tilde{\gamma}_{i}\}_{i}\big)
as affine spaces under

\pi_{X}\simeq{\mathscr{H}}_{X}
.

(b) Let
\{(A_{r},\Psi_{r})\}_{r}
be as in the statement of Theorem

1.8
, and suppose that for each
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}
, the sequence
\{{\mathfrak{c}}_{i,r}\}_{r}
,
consists of the
Y_{i}
-end limits of a sequence of admissible solutions

\{(A_{r},\Psi_{r})\}_{r}
.

Then the
image of the map
{\mathfrak{h}}
is
{\mathscr{H}}\big((X^{{}^{\prime}a},\nu),\{\tilde{\gamma}_{i}\}_{i}\big)\subset{\mathscr{H}}^{\mathbb{R}}\big((X^{{}^{\prime}a},\nu),\{\tilde{\gamma}_{i}\}_{i}\big)
. Thus, in this case
{\mathfrak{h}}
is a canonical
isomorphism from
\pi_{0}{\mathscr{B}}_{X}(\{{\mathfrak{c}}_{i,r}\}_{i})
to
{\mathscr{H}}\big((X^{{}^{\prime}a},\nu),\{\tilde{\gamma}_{i}\}_{i}\big)
as affine spaces under
\pi_{X}\simeq{\mathscr{H}}_{X}
.

Proof.
By construction, we have the following commutative diagram under
the assumptions of the lemma: For any
r,r^{\prime}>r_{0}

\textstyle{\pi_{0}{\mathscr{B}}_{X}(\{{\mathfrak{c}}_{i,r^{\prime}}\}_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathfrak{h}}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\pi_{0}{\mathscr{B}}_{X}(\{{\mathfrak{c}}_{i,r}\}_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathfrak{h}}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{H}}^{\mathbb{R}}\big((X^{{}^{\prime}a},\nu),\{\tilde{{\mathfrak{c}}}_{i,r^{\prime}}\}_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}}\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{H}}^{\mathbb{R}}\big((X^{{}^{\prime}a},\nu),\{\tilde{{\mathfrak{c}}}_{i,r}\}_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}}\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{\infty}}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{H}}^{\mathbb{R}}\big((X^{{}^{\prime}a},\nu),\{\tilde{\gamma}_{i}\}_{i}\big).}

(3.36)

where all the maps are morphisms of affine spaces under
\pi_{X}\simeq{\mathscr{H}}_{X}\subset{\mathscr{H}}_{X}^{\mathbb{R}}
; the horizontal map in the top
row is the canonical isomorphism from Lemma
3.19
; the
horizontal maps in the bottom
row are the canonical isomorphisms from Lemma
2.14
.
The composition
i_{\infty}\circ{\mathfrak{h}}^{\prime}
maps

\pi_{0}{\mathscr{B}}_{X}(\{{\mathfrak{c}}_{i,r}\}_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}})
to another orbit of the

{\mathscr{H}}_{X}
-action in
{\mathscr{H}}^{\mathbb{R}}\big((X^{{}^{\prime}a},\nu),\{\tilde{\gamma}_{i}\}_{i}\big)
. The canonical isomorphism claimed in Item (a) of the
statement of the present lemma is defined to be this composition map:

{\mathfrak{h}}:=i_{\infty}\circ{\mathfrak{h}}^{\prime}
.

The proof of Item (b) will follows as a by-product of the proof of
Theorem
1.10
(c), and will be deferred to Section

7.3

\Box

3.6
Taubes’ proof for
SW\Rightarrow Gr
: A synopsis

The proof of Theorem
1.8
follows the outline of Taube’s
arguments in
[
T
]
. To serve as a roadmap for the remainder of this
article, a brief summary of the ingredients in
[
T
]
is
provided here for reader’s convenience. For each step listed below, we
indicate where it takes place in
[
T
]
,
[
Ts
]
, and
the present article, under their respectively contexts.

Let
(X,\nu)
be an admissible pair and let

(A,\Psi)=(A_{r},\Psi_{r})
be as in the statement of Theorem

1.8
. Write

\Psi=(r/2)^{1/2}\psi,\quad\text{and $\psi=(\alpha,\beta)$}

according to the decomposition
{\mathbb{S}}^{+}=E\oplus E\otimes K^{-1}
on

X^{{}^{\prime}a}
. The
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
-connection corresponding to
A
induces a connection
on
E
, and together with the Levi-Civita connection, also a
connection on
E\otimes K^{-1}
. We use
\nabla_{A}\alpha
,

\nabla_{A}\beta
to denote the covariant derivative with respect to
the aforementioned induced connections.

Taubes’ proof proceeds with the steps listed below:

(1)

Obtaining pointwise estimates of
|\beta|,|F_{A}|,|\nabla_{A}\alpha|,|\nabla_{A}\beta|
in terms of the “energy
density”
r|\varpi|:=r||\nu|-|\alpha|^{2}|
.
The estimates show that these quantities are interesting where
\alpha
is
small. Cf.
[
Ts
]
Sections 3 b)-e),
[
T
]
Section I.2.
Section
5
in this article contains the corresponding estimates
on a MCE.

(2)

Obtaining an integral bound for
r|\varpi|
in terms of constants depending
only on the
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
structure and the metric. This is basically
equivalent to an integral bound on

\frac{iF_{A}}{2\pi}\wedge\nu
. Cf.
[
Ts
]
Section 3 a) and
references therein. The
corresponding results are establishes in §
4.7
below.

(3)

Establishing a monotonicity formula for
\mathcal{W}_{B}
,
which is a certain notion of “energy”
over a ball
B
in
X
.
Cf.
[
Ts
]
Section 4 and
[
T
]
Section I.3 and the beginning of
Section
6
below.
Roughly speaking,
{\mathscr{W}}_{B}
is the integral of the “energy
density”
r|\varpi|
over
B
. Note that this notion of energy
is however of a
different nature from other, more typical notions of energy for
Seiberg-Witten theory on MCE, such as
the Chern-Simons-Dirac functional, or more generally, the topological
energy
{\mathscr{E}}_{top}
introduced in
[
KM
]
. This
{\mathscr{W}}_{B}
should be regarded
as an analogue of the notion of energy on the Gromov side, namely the
area of the holomorphic curves. As a result of the monotonicity
formula, one obtains an
r
-independent bound on the
2-dimensional Hausdorff measure of
\alpha^{-1}(0)
, and hence also
on that of the t-curve that
\alpha^{-1}(0)
geometrically converges to as

r\to\infty
. The relevant monotonicity formula in our context is
given in Section
6
below.

(4)

Rescaling that
(A,\psi)
over a ball of radius
O(r^{-1/2})

yields an approximate solution to the version of the
Seiberg-Witten equation (
1.1
) on the Euclidean space
X={\mathbb{R}}^{4}=\{(x_{1},\cdots,x_{4})\}
and

\mu^{+}=\frac{1}{2}(dx_{1}\wedge dx_{2}+dx_{3}\wedge dx_{4})
. The behavior of
Seiberg-Witten solutions for such
(X,\mu^{+})
are
well-understood in terms of vortex solutions on
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
, and these
give the local models
for
(A,\psi)
. In particular, combining with Items (1)–(3) above, this
implies the exponential decay of
|\beta|,|F_{A^{E}}|,|\nabla_{A}\alpha|,|\nabla_{A}\beta|
away from
\alpha^{-1}(0)
. This step appeared in
Section 6 of
[
Ts
]
and Section I.4 of
[
T
]
. This
adapts easily to our context; see Sections
6.6
and

6.5
below.

(5)

Regarding
\frac{i}{2\pi}F_{A^{E}}
as a 2-current , Items (1) and (2) above imply that this
current is bounded independently of
r
. Thus, Alaoglu’s theorem
(cf. e.g.
[
Rd
]
Theorem 10.6.17) implies that these currents
converge to a current
\mathcal{F}
in weak
∗

topology. Furthermore, via (3) and (4) it is shown that the support of

\mathcal{F}
, denoted temporarily by
C
, is a closed space of 2-dimensional measure,
and is the
r\to\infty
limit of
\alpha^{-1}(0)
in the sense of
(
(1)
). Cf. the first half of Section I.5 of
[
T
]
;
Sections 7(a) and (b) of
[
Ts
]
, and Section
7.1
in
this article.

(6)

Using Item (4), it is shown that the current
\mathcal{F}
defines a
“positive cohomology assignment”, namely, the map from the set of a
certain kind of generic disks in
X
(the “admissible disks”) to
{\mathbb{Z}}
, whose
value is positive for admissible pseudo-holomorphic disks which
intersect
C
. Taubes showed that this fact guarantees that
C
is
a pseudo-holomorphic curve. Cf. Section 6 of
[
T
]
and Sections
7(c)-(e) of
[
Ts
]
. This part of Taubes’ argument applies directly in our setting with
no need for modification.

4
L^{2}_{1,loc}
-bounds and integral estimates

The main results of this section come in two groups. The first group of main results, Propositions

4.12
and
4.13
, provide

L^{2}_{1}
-bounds on solutions
(A_{r},\Psi_{r})
to the Seiberg-Witten
equation
{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mu_{r},\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}}(A_{r},\Psi_{r})=0
, with careful
control on the growth rate of these bounds on
r
. While (for each
fixed
r
) such

L^{2}_{1}
bounds usually serve as the starting point of typical
proofs compactness results in Seiberg-Witten
theory (cf. e.g.
[
KM
]
’s Corollary 10.6.2 and Theorem 5.2.1
(ii)), the usual arguments to derive them (e.g. those in
[
KM
]
),
do not provide sufficient bounds on the
r
-growth rate needed for t-convergence.
The second group of main results, Lemmas
4.16
and

4.17
, supply the type of “energy bounds” required to begin the proof
of
t
-convergence results. This corresponds to Step (2) of the list
in Section
3.6
.

4.1
{\mathscr{E}}_{top}
and
{\mathscr{E}}_{anal}
: the topological energy
and the analytic energy

We begin with some general definitions and observations.
Similar to what was done in
[
KM
]
(Definition 4.5.4 therein), we introduce two
Floer-theoretic notion of “energy”,
{\mathscr{E}}_{anal}(A,\Psi)
and

{\mathscr{E}}_{top}(A,\Psi)
below. They are related when
(A,\Psi)

satisfies a Seiberg-Witten equation. (The subscripts “anal” and “top” signify
“analytical” and “topological” respectively.) As is typical in
Floer theories,
{\mathscr{E}}_{anal}
is useful for estimating

L^{2}_{1}
-norms, while
{\mathscr{E}}_{top}
depends only on the end points
of the Floer trajectory and its relative homotopy class. In the
cylindrical case,
{\mathscr{E}}_{top}
is the difference in the values of the CSD functional at end points
(cf. §
3.1
for the definition of CSD functionals).

Let
X
be a
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
MCE with ending 3-manifolds
Y_{i}
, and let
\mu

be a closed 2-form on
X
that has a closed 2-form
\mu_{i}
as the

Y_{i}
-end limit for each
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}
, in the sense defined in
Section
1.4
. Recall also from Section
1.4
the
definition of
X_{\bullet}
. (Note in particular that the boundary
components of
X_{\bullet}
consist of slices of the form
Y_{i:l}
).

Definition 4.1
.

Fix a compact
X_{\bullet}\subset X
. Given
(A,\Psi)\in{\mathscr{C}}(X_{\bullet})
, let

\begin{split}{\mathscr{E}}_{anal}^{\mu}(X_{\bullet})(A,\Psi)=&\frac{1}{4}\int_{X_{\bullet}}|F_{A}|^{2}+\int_{X_{\bullet}}|\nabla_{A}\Psi|^{2}+\int_{X_{\bullet}}\Big|\frac{i}{4}\rho(\mu^{+})-(\Psi\Psi^{*})_{0}\Big|^{2}\\
&\qquad+\int_{X_{\bullet}}\frac{R_{g}}{4}|\Psi|^{2}-\frac{i}{4}\int_{X_{\bullet}}F_{A}\wedge*_{4}\mu,\\
\end{split}

(4.1)

where
R_{g}
denotes the scalar curvature.

Over a slice

Y_{i:s}:=\text{{\small s}}_{i}^{-1}(s)\simeq Y_{i}
of
\bar{X}
, let
(B,\Phi)=(B(s),\Phi(s))=(A,\Psi)\big|_{Y_{i:s}}
denote
the restriction of
(A,\Psi)
to
\text{{\small s}}_{i}^{-1}(s)\subset X
. We define

{\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{\mu}(X_{\bullet})(A,\Psi)=\frac{1}{4}\int_{X_{\bullet}}F_{A}\wedge F_{A}-\int_{\partial\overline{X_{\bullet}}}\langle\Phi,\mbox{\mbox{$\partial\mkern-7.5mu\mbox{/}\mkern 2.0mu$}}_{B}\Phi\rangle+\frac{i}{4}\int_{X_{\bullet}}F_{A}\wedge\mu.

(4.2)

We then define

{\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{\mu,\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}}(X_{\bullet})(A,\Psi)={\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{\mu}(X_{\bullet})(A,\Psi)-2\,f_{\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}}((A,\Psi)|_{\partial\overline{X_{\bullet}}}).

(4.3)

The preceding definition of
{\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{\mu}
and
{\mathscr{E}}^{\mu}_{anal}
is motivated by the following identity:

\|\mathfrak{S}_{\mu_{r}}(A,\Psi)\|_{L^{2}(X_{\bullet})}^{2}=\mathcal{E}_{anal}^{\mu_{r}}(X_{\bullet})(A,\Psi)-{\mathcal{E}}_{top}^{\mu_{r}}(X_{\bullet})(A,\Psi).\quad

(4.4)

In particular, when
(A,\Psi)\in{\mathscr{C}}(X_{\bullet})
is a solution to the
Seiberg-Witten equation
{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mu,\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}}(A,\Psi)=0
, one
has:

{\mathscr{E}}_{anal}^{\mu}(X_{\bullet})(A,\Psi)={\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{\mu,\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}}(X_{\bullet})(A,\Psi)+\|\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}(A,\Psi)\|_{L^{2}(X_{\bullet})}^{2}.

This identity is used in Part 1 of the next subsection to bound the square
terms in the first line of (
4.1
) in terms of

{\mathscr{E}}_{top}
. What follows are some observations demonstrating the
topological nature of
{\mathscr{E}}_{top}
.

Let
A_{0}
,
B_{0,i}
be the reference connections from
(
3.3
). Recall the definitions of CSD functionals from (
3.1
),
(
3.2
), and Remark
3.6
. It is often convenient to re-express the topological
energies
{\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{\mu}
,
{\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{\mu,\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}}
in
terms of the CSD functionals:
By the Stokes’ theorem,

\begin{split}{\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{\mu}(X_{\bullet})(A,\Psi)&=\frac{1}{4}\int_{X_{\bullet}}F_{A_{0}}\wedge F_{A_{0}}+\frac{i}{4}\int_{X_{\bullet}}F_{A_{0}}\wedge\mu-2\operatorname{CSD}_{\mu}^{\partial\overline{X_{\bullet}}}(B,\Phi);\\
{\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{\mu,\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}}(X_{\bullet})(A,\Psi)&=\frac{1}{4}\int_{X_{\bullet}}F_{A_{0}}\wedge F_{A_{0}}+\frac{i}{4}\int_{X_{\bullet}}F_{A_{0}}\wedge\mu_{r}-2\operatorname{CSD}_{\mu,\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}}^{\partial\overline{X_{\bullet}}}(B,\Phi).\\
\end{split}

(4.5)

Given the formulas (
4.2
), (
4.3
),

{\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{\mu}
and
{\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{\mu,\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}}
are by
definition gauge-invariant. Moreover,
{\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{\mu}(X_{\bullet})(A,\Psi)
,
{\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{\mu,\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}}(X_{\bullet})(A,\Psi)
are can be computed from the relative homotopy class of

[(A,\Psi)]
via the following explicit formulas:
Recalling that
[(B,\Phi)]_{c}\in{\mathscr{C}}(Y)
denotes the representative of
[(B,\Phi)]\in{\mathscr{B}}(Y)

in the normalized Coulomb gauge, one may re-express (
4.5
) as

\begin{split}&{\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{\mu}(X_{\bullet})(A,\Psi)=\frac{1}{4}\int_{X_{\bullet}}F_{A_{0}}\wedge F_{A_{0}}+\frac{i}{4}\int_{X_{\bullet}}F_{A_{0}}\wedge\mu\\
&\qquad\quad-2\operatorname{CSD}_{\mu}^{\partial\overline{X_{\bullet}}}([(B,\Phi)]_{c})-2i^{*}\big((\pi c_{1}({\mathfrak{s}}_{X}|_{X_{\bullet}})-\frac{\mu|_{X_{\bullet}}}{4}\big)\cdot h\,(A,\Psi);\\
&{\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{\mu,\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}}(X_{\bullet})(A,\Psi)=\frac{1}{4}\int_{X_{\bullet}}F_{A_{0}}\wedge F_{A_{0}}+\frac{i}{4}\int_{X_{\bullet}}F_{A_{0}}\wedge\mu\\
&\qquad\quad-2\operatorname{CSD}_{\mu,\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}}^{\partial\overline{X_{\bullet}}}([(B,\Phi)]_{c})-2i^{*}\big((\pi c_{1}({\mathfrak{s}}_{X}|_{X_{\bullet}})-\frac{\mu|_{X_{\bullet}}}{4}\big)\cdot h\,(A,\Psi),\end{split}

(4.6)

where
h\,(A,\Psi)\in H^{1}(\partial\overline{X_{\bullet}};{\mathbb{Z}})/\mathop{\mathrm{Im}\,}\nolimits i^{*}\simeq\pi_{X_{\bullet}}
denotes the image of the relative homology class of

[(A,\Psi)]
under the map
h_{A_{0}}
from Remark
3.22
, and
i^{*}\colon\thinspace H^{*}(\overline{X_{\bullet}};{\mathbb{Z}})\to H^{*}(\partial\overline{X_{\bullet}};{\mathbb{Z}})
is part of the
relative long exact sequence of the pair
(\overline{X_{\bullet}},\partial\overline{X_{\bullet}})
. Note that the pairing between the modules
H^{1}(\partial\overline{X_{\bullet}};{\mathbb{Z}})/\mathop{\mathrm{Im}\,}\nolimits i^{*}\simeq H_{2}(\partial\overline{X_{\bullet}};{\mathbb{Z}})/\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}\,}\nolimits i
and
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}\,}\nolimits(i^{*}\colon\thinspace H^{2}(\overline{X_{\bullet}};{\mathbb{Z}})\to H^{2}(\partial\overline{X_{\bullet}};{\mathbb{Z}}))
is
well-defined because the submodule
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}\,}\nolimits(i^{*}\colon\thinspace H^{2}(\overline{X_{\bullet}};{\mathbb{Z}})\to H^{2}(\partial\overline{X_{\bullet}};{\mathbb{Z}}))
pairs trivially with
\mathop{\mathrm{Ker}\,}\nolimits(i\colon\thinspace H_{2}(\partial\overline{X_{\bullet}};{\mathbb{Z}})\to H_{2}(\overline{X_{\bullet}};{\mathbb{Z}}))
.

To summarize:

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
X
,
\mu
,
\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}
be as before. Given arbitrary
(possibly non-compact)
X_{\bullet}\subset X
and an admissible
(A,\Psi)\in{\mathscr{C}}(X_{\bullet})
, the topological
energy
{\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{\mu}(X_{\bullet})(A,\Psi)
is
well-defined. Moreover, their values depend only on the

\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
-structure of
X
,
[(A,\Psi)|_{\partial\overline{X_{\bullet}}}]\in{\mathscr{B}}(\partial\overline{X_{\bullet}})
, and the relative
homotopy class of
[(A,\Psi)]
.

Moreover, combining (
4.6
)
and Lemmas
3.12
,
3.23
, one
has:

Lemma 4.3
.

Adopt the assumptions and notations in the statement of Theorem

1.8
. (In particular, recall that
(A_{r},\Psi_{r})
are
Seiberg-Witten solutions
{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mu_{r},\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}}(A_{r},\Psi_{r})=0

with
Y_{i}
-end limits proscribed by
\{\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{i}\}_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}}
,
\{(B_{i},\Phi_{i})\}_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{v}}
,
and with fixed relative homotopy class

h
.)
Then there exist a constant

\textsc{e},\in{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
depending only on
\|\nu\|_{C^{1}}
,

\varsigma_{w}
, and:

\hss\left\{\mkern-20.0mu\parbox{310.4979pt}{\begin{itemize}\par\itemize@item@the $\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}$-structure on
$X$,
\par\itemize@item@the relative homotopy class $\text{{\scriptsize h}}$, or equivalently
${\mathfrak{h}}(\text{{\scriptsize h}})$ (and hence also
implicitly on $\{\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{i}\}_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}}$), \par\itemize@item@the cohomology class $[\nu]$, \par\end{itemize}}\right.

(4.7)

such that for
all
r\geq r_{0}
(
r_{0}
being larger or equal to that in Lemma
3.12
),

|{\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{\mu_{r}}(X)(A_{r},\Psi_{r})|\leq\textsc{e}r,\quad\text{and}\quad|{\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{\mu_{r},\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}}(X)(A_{r},\Psi_{r})|\leq\textsc{e}\,r.

(4.8)

The next lemma says more about the coefficient
e
above.

Lemma 4.4
.

(a) Let
Y_{i}
be a Morse end.
Suppose
\{(B_{r},\Phi_{r})\}_{r}
is a sequence of
Seiberg-Witten solutions
{\mathfrak{F}}_{\mu_{i,r}}(B_{r},\Phi_{r})=0
that
strongly t-converges to
\boldsymbol{\gamma}
. Then the
limit

\lim_{r\to\infty}(r^{-1}\operatorname{CSD}_{\mu_{i,r}}([B_{r},\Phi_{r}]_{c}))=\lim_{r\to\infty}(r^{-1}\operatorname{CSD}_{\mu_{i,r},{\mathfrak{q}}_{i}}([B_{r},\Phi_{r}]_{c}))

exists, and

\Big|\lim_{r\to\infty}(r^{-1}\operatorname{CSD}_{\mu_{i,r}}([B_{r},\Phi_{r})]_{c})\Big|\leq\frac{\pi}{2}\sqrt{b^{1}(Y_{i})}\,|[\nu_{i}]|.

(4.9)

(b) Adopt the notations and assumptions of Theorem
1.8
. Then the
limit

{\mathbb{E}}:=\lim_{r\to\infty}(r^{-1}{\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{\mu_{r},\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}}(A_{r},\Psi_{r}))

exists, and equals
\lim_{r\to\infty}(r^{-1}{\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{\mu_{r}}(A_{r},\Psi_{r}))
. It is determined by the items listed in (
4.7
)
via the formula (
4.11
) below.

Proof.

(a):
Let
{\mathfrak{c}}_{r}
denote the gauge equivalence class of
(B_{r},\Phi_{r})
.
Recall the map
\jmath_{c}\colon\thinspace\pi\,(\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits/{\mathscr{G}};[B_{0}],\Pi{\mathfrak{c}}_{r})\to H^{1}(Y_{i};{\mathbb{R}})\simeq H_{2}(Y_{i};{\mathbb{R}})
from Section
3.5
and let

\tilde{c}_{0}({\mathfrak{c}}_{r})\in\pi\,(\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits/{\mathscr{G}};[B_{0}],\Pi{\mathfrak{c}}_{r})
be the element represented
by the path
s\mapsto B_{0}+(1-\chi(s))(B_{r}-B_{0})
on
\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits(Y_{i})
. Then

\begin{split}r^{-1}\operatorname{CSD}_{\mu_{i,r}}([B_{r},\Phi_{r}]_{c})&=r^{-1}\operatorname{CSD}_{\mu_{i,r},{\mathfrak{q}}_{i}}([B_{r},\Phi_{r}]_{c})\\
&=r^{-1}\operatorname{CSD}_{w_{i,r}}({\mathfrak{c}}_{r})-\frac{i}{8}\int_{Y_{i}}([B_{r}]_{c}-B_{0})\wedge\nu_{i}\\
&=r^{-1}\operatorname{CSD}_{w_{i,r}}({\mathfrak{c}}_{r})-\frac{\pi}{2}[\nu_{i}]\cdot\jmath_{c}(\tilde{c}_{0}({\mathfrak{c}}_{r})).\\
\end{split}

(4.10)

The assumption that
(B_{r},\Phi_{r})
strongly converges implies that
the limit

\lim_{r\to\infty}\jmath_{c}(\tilde{c}_{0}({\mathfrak{c}}_{r}))
exists, and its norm is bounded
by
\sqrt{b^{1}(Y_{i})}
. Denote this limit by

\jmath_{h}(\boldsymbol{\gamma})\in H_{2}(Y_{i};{\mathbb{R}})
.
Together with Lemma

3.12
, this implies that for
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}
,

\lim_{r\to\infty}(r^{-1}\operatorname{CSD}_{\mu_{i,r}}([B_{r},\Phi_{r}]_{c}))=-\frac{\pi}{2}[\nu_{i}]\cdot\jmath_{h}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}),

and hence Assertion (a) of the lemma.

(b):
By (
4.6
),

\begin{split}&r^{-1}{\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{\mu_{r}}(A_{r},\Psi_{r})\\
&\qquad=\frac{1}{4r}\int_{X}F_{A_{0}}\wedge F_{A_{0}}+\frac{i}{4r}\int_{X}F_{A_{0}}\wedge\mu_{r}\\
&\qquad\qquad-2r^{-1}\sum_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}}\operatorname{CSD}_{\mu_{i,r}}^{Y_{i}}([(B_{i,r},\Phi_{i,r})]_{c})+i^{*}[\nu]\cdot h_{A_{0}}(\operatorname{\text{{\scriptsize h}}})/2;\\
&r^{-1}{\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{\mu_{r},\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}}(A_{r},\Psi_{r})\\
&\qquad=\frac{1}{4r}\int_{X}F_{A_{0}}\wedge F_{A_{0}}+\frac{i}{4r}\int_{X}F_{A_{0}}\wedge\mu_{r}\\
&\qquad\qquad-2r^{-1}\sum_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}}\operatorname{CSD}_{\mu_{i,r},{\mathfrak{q}}_{i}}^{Y_{i}}([(B_{i,r},\Phi_{i,r})]_{c})+i^{*}[\nu]\cdot h_{A_{0}}(\operatorname{\text{{\scriptsize h}}})/2,\end{split}

where
h_{A_{0}}
is the map defined in Remark
3.22
.

Combined with Assertion (a) of the lemma and Lemma

3.12
, this gives

\begin{split}{\mathbb{E}}&:=\lim_{r\to\infty}\big(r^{-1}{\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{\mu_{r},\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}}(A_{r},\Psi_{r})\big)=\lim_{r\to\infty}\big(r^{-1}{\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{\mu_{r}}(A_{r},\Psi_{r})\big)\\
&=\frac{i}{4}\int_{X}F_{A_{0}}\wedge\nu+\pi\sum_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}}[\nu_{i}]\cdot\jmath_{h}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{i})+i^{*}[\nu]\cdot h_{A_{0}}(\operatorname{\text{{\scriptsize h}}})/2.\end{split}

(4.11)

\Box

Remarks 4.5
.

(a) By Theorem
1.3
and the fact that there are finitely
many t-orbits with a fixed
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
structure, the bound
(
4.9
) holds for any sequence of solutions to the 3-dimensional
Seiberg-Witten equations
{\mathfrak{F}}_{\mu_{i,r}}(B_{r},\Phi_{r})=0
.

(b) By Item (b) of the previous lemma, the constant
e
in Lemma

4.3
may be chosen to depend only on
(
4.7
) (though
r_{0}
may still depend on
\|\nu\|_{C^{1}}
and
\varsigma_{w}
).

To obtain the first group of results mentioned in the beginning of the
present section, we need a generalization of Lemma
4.3
to general

X_{\bullet}\subset X
, with counterparts of the coefficient
e
in
(
4.8
) independent of both
r
and
X_{\bullet}
. This
is much more difficult to achieve, mainly due to the fact that the
perturbation form
\nu
is not translation-invariant on the ends of

X
; cf. the second paragraph of Section
1.3
. In
fact, instead of bounding
{\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{\mu_{r}}
and

{\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{\mu_{r},\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}}
, we find it more convenient to
work with a modified version of them, which agree with them in the
case when
\nu
is translation-invariant on the ends.

First, on each

Y_{i:s}\subset X-X_{c}
, let
\nu_{+}=\nu_{+}(s)
,
\mu_{+}=\mu_{+}(s)
respectively
denote the
s
-dependent closed 2-forms on
Y_{i}
:

\nu_{+}(s):=2\nu^{+}|_{Y_{i:s}};\quad\mu_{+}(s)=2(\mu_{r}^{+})|_{Y_{i:s}}=r\nu_{+}+w_{i,r}

Modifying (
4.5
), we set

\begin{split}{\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{{}^{\prime}\mu_{r}}(X_{\bullet})(A,\Psi)&:={\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{\mu_{r}}(X_{\bullet})(A,\Psi)+\frac{i}{4}\int_{\partial\overline{X_{\bullet}}}(A-A_{0})\wedge(*_{4}\mu_{r})\\
&=\frac{1}{4}\int_{X_{\bullet}}F_{A_{0}}\wedge F_{A_{0}}+\frac{i}{4}\int_{X_{\bullet}}F_{A_{0}}\wedge\mu_{r}-2\operatorname{CSD}_{\mu_{+}}^{\partial\overline{X_{\bullet}}}(B,\Phi).\\
\end{split}

(4.12)

Define
{\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{{}^{\prime}\mu_{r},\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}}(X_{\bullet})
similarly by
replacing the term
{\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{\mu_{r}}(X_{\bullet})
above with

{\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{\mu_{r},\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}}(X_{\bullet})
.
Note that

{\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{{}^{\prime}\mu_{r}}(X)={\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{\mu_{r}}(X);\qquad{\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{{}^{\prime}\mu_{r},\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}}(X)={\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{\mu_{r},\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}}(X).

Therefore the bounds in (
4.8
) hold
for
{\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{{}^{\prime}\mu_{r}}(X)
,

{\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{{}^{\prime}\mu_{r},\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}}(X)
as well.

A preliminary version of the aforementioned generalization of Lemma

4.3
is stated in terms of
{\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{{}^{\prime}\mu_{r}}
and

{\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{{}^{\prime}\mu_{r},\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}}
as follows:

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
(A,\Psi)=(A_{r},\Psi_{r})
be an admissible solution to the Seiberg-Witten
equation
{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mu_{r},\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}}(A_{r},\Psi_{r})=0
on
X
that satisfies in addition

\text{either (a)}\,\,{\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{\mu_{r}}(X)(A_{r},\Psi_{r})\leq\textsc{e}\,r\quad\text{ or (b)}\,\,{\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{\mu_{r},\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}}(X)(A_{r},\Psi_{r})\leq\textsc{e}\,r

(4.13)

for
a positive constant
e
independent of
r
and
(A,\Psi)
. (In particular, according to Lemma
4.3
and Remarks
4.5
, this
holds for those
(A_{r},\Psi_{r})
from the statement of Theorem

1.8
, with
e
determined by (
4.7
) via
(
4.11
).)
Then there exist constants

\zeta>0

\zeta^{\prime}>0
, a function
\boldsymbol{\hat{\mathop{\mathrm{l}}}}\colon\thinspace{\mathfrak{Y}}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
depending only
on
\nu
, and an
r_{0}>8
depending only on the constants

e
,
{\mathfrak{l}}^{\prime}_{i}
,
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{v}
,
and
\nu
, such that for all
r\geq r_{0}
and

{\bf l}_{r}:=(\ln r)\,\boldsymbol{\hat{\mathop{\mathrm{l}}}}
,

\begin{split}\mathrm{(i)}&\begin{cases}-\zeta^{\prime}r\ln r\leq{\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{{}^{\prime}\mu_{r}}(X_{\bullet})(A_{r},\Psi_{r})\leq r(\textsc{e}+\zeta)&\text{ assuming (\ref {assume:EtopX-ubdd}) (a)}\\
-\zeta^{\prime}_{p}r\ln r\leq{\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{{}^{\prime}\mu_{r},\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}}(X_{\bullet})(A_{r},\Psi_{r})\leq r(\textsc{e}+\zeta_{p})&\text{assuming (\ref {assume:EtopX-ubdd}) (b)}\end{cases}\\
&\qquad\qquad\text{$\forall X_{\bullet}\supset X_{{\bf l}_{r}}$};\\
\mathrm{(ii)}&-\zeta^{\prime}_{5}r\leq{\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{{}^{\prime}\mu_{r}}(X_{\bullet})(A_{r},\Psi_{r})={\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{{}^{\prime}\mu_{r},\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}}(X_{\bullet})(A_{r},\Psi_{r})\leq r(\textsc{e}+\zeta_{e}\ln r)\\
&\qquad\qquad\text{\rm$\forall X_{\bullet}\subset X-\mathring{X}_{{\bf l}_{r}}$}.\end{split}

(4.14)

The positive constants

\zeta
,
\zeta^{\prime}
,
\zeta^{\prime}_{5}
,
\zeta_{e}
above depend only on the metric, the
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}

structure,
\nu
,
\varsigma_{w}
,
B_{0}
, and the constants
\textsl{z}_{i}

in Lemma
3.11
.
\zeta_{p}
,
\zeta^{\prime}_{p}
are positive
constants that depend only on the preceding
list of parameters, together with the constant
\textsl{z}_{p}
in
Assumption
3.4
. In particular, these constants as well as
\boldsymbol{\hat{\mathop{\mathrm{l}}}}
and
r_{0}
above
are independent of
X_{\bullet}
and
r
.

A proof of the preceding lemma will be given in Section

4.5
. The undesirable factors of
\ln r
in
(
4.14
) appear due to the previously-mentioned trouble
with non-translation invariant
\nu
at the ends
(cf. (
4.38
) and remarks that follow). They
will eventually be removed in Section
6.2
. (See Proposition

6.4
.)

Looking ahead, the definitions and lemmas above are relevant to the first group of
results mentioned in the beginning of this section in the following
manner: The typical first step towards such results is to use the
relation between analytical and topological energies to bound
L^{2}(X_{\bullet})
-norms of
gauge invariant terms such as
F_{A}
and
\nabla_{A}\Psi
in terms
of the topological energy over
X_{\bullet}
. (In our context, this appears as Lemmas
4.7
and

4.8
below.) Upper bounds on the topology energy over
X_{\bullet}
, such
as those given in Lemma
4.6
and Proposition

6.4
in our context, then give rise to upper
bounds on the aforementioned squares of
L^{2}(X_{\bullet})
-norms of
gauge invariant terms. The latter are used to obtain
L^{2}_{1,A}(X_{\bullet})
bounds on Seiberg-Witten solutions. (This is done in
Section
4.6
in our context.)

In existing literature,
(X,\nu)
is cylindrical on the ends, and upper
bounds on the topological energy over
X_{\bullet}
follows directly
from a corresponding bound over
X
. (The analog of Lemma

4.3
in our context.) This is due to the fact that the
\operatorname{CSD}

functional is decreasing on a Floer trajectory, which can be
interpreted as the gradient flow line of the
\operatorname{CSD}

functional. In the more general
setting of ours, local upper bounds for
{\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{\mu}
likewise follows from
a lower bound on
{\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{\mu}
for
X_{\bullet}
that are
contained in the cylindrical ends of
X
; see Section

4.4
. This lower bound makes use of an
interpretation of the Floer trajectory as a gradient flow line of a

time-dependent

\operatorname{CSD}
-functional; see Section

4.3
below.

4.2
L^{2}_{local}
bounds on gauge invariant terms in terms of
(modified) topological energies

Let
(A_{r},\Psi_{r})=(A,\Psi)
be an admissible solution to the Seiberg-Witten
equation
{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mu_{t},\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}}(A_{r},\Psi_{r})=0
with

Y_{i}
-end limit
(B_{i},\Phi_{i})
,
and let
A_{0}

\{B_{0,i}\}_{i}
be the reference
connections fixed previously.
Recall the definition of
|X_{\bullet}|
from Section

1.4
and let

|X_{\bullet}|_{1}:=\min\{1,|X_{\bullet}|\}.

Recall also the definitions of
X^{{}^{\prime}a}
,
X^{\prime\prime}
from Definition

1.6
, and note that
\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}(A,\Psi)
is supported on the vanishing ends; in fact, on

X-X^{\prime\prime}\supset X-X^{{}^{\prime}a}
. Let

X_{\bullet,v}:=X_{\bullet}\cap(X-X^{\prime\prime})\qquad\text{and}\qquad X_{\bullet,m}:=X_{\bullet}\cap X^{\prime\prime}.

In this subsection, we show that:

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
X_{\bullet}\subset X
be compact and let
r\geq 1
. Then there exist
positive constants
\zeta_{0}
,
\zeta^{\prime}
,
\zeta^{\prime}_{p}
,
\zeta^{\prime\prime}
,
\zeta^{\prime\prime}_{p}
that depend only on

\hss\left\{\mkern-20.0mu\parbox{310.4979pt}{\begin{itemize}\par\itemize@item@the metric,
\par\itemize@item@the $\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}$-structure on $X$,
\par\itemize@item@the cohomology class of $\nu$,
\par\itemize@item@the constant $\varsigma_{w}$ in Assumption \ref {assume},
\par\itemize@item@the constant $\textsl{z}_{\mathfrak{p}}$ in Assumption \ref {assume}, \par\end{itemize}}\right.

(4.15)

such that the following hold:

\begin{split}\text{(a)}&\quad\frac{1}{8}\int_{X_{\bullet}}|F_{A}|^{2}+\int_{X_{\bullet}}|\nabla_{A}\Psi|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{X_{\bullet}}\big|\frac{i}{4}\rho(\mu_{r}^{+})-(\Psi\Psi^{*})_{0}\big|^{2}\\
&\qquad\quad\leq{\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{{}^{\prime}\mu_{r}}(X_{\bullet})(A,\Psi)+r\,(\zeta_{0}|X^{\prime}_{\bullet,m}|+\zeta^{\prime}|X_{\bullet}|_{1})+\zeta^{\prime\prime}|X_{\bullet}|;\\
\text{(b)}&\quad\frac{1}{16}\int_{X_{\bullet}}|F_{A}|^{2}+\int_{X_{\bullet}}|\nabla_{A}\Psi|^{2}+\frac{1}{4}\int_{X_{\bullet}}\big|\frac{i}{4}\rho(\mu_{r}^{+})-(\Psi\Psi^{*})_{0}\big|^{2}\\
&\qquad\quad\leq{\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{{}^{\prime}\mu_{r},\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}}(X_{\bullet})(A,\Psi)+r\,(\zeta_{0}|X^{\prime}_{\bullet,m}|+\zeta^{\prime}_{p}|X_{\bullet}|_{1})+\zeta^{\prime\prime}_{p}|X_{\bullet}|.\end{split}

(4.16)

Proof.
To begin, combine
(
4.4
) with the Seiberg-Witten equation

{\mathfrak{G}}_{\mu_{r},\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}}(A_{r},\Psi_{r})=0
to get:

\begin{split}&\frac{1}{4}\int_{X_{\bullet}}|F_{A}|^{2}+\int_{X_{\bullet}}|\nabla_{A}\Psi|^{2}+\int_{X_{\bullet}}\big|\frac{i}{4}\rho(\mu_{r}^{+})-(\Psi\Psi^{*})_{0}\big|^{2}\\
&\quad=-\int_{X_{\bullet}}\frac{R_{g}}{4}|\Psi|^{2}+\frac{i}{4}\int_{X_{\bullet}}F_{A}\wedge*_{4}\mu_{r}+{\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{\mu_{r}}(X_{\bullet})(A,\Psi)\\
&\qquad\qquad+\|\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}(A,\Psi)\|_{L^{2}(X_{\bullet})}^{2}\\
&\quad=-\int_{X_{\bullet}}\frac{R_{g}}{4}|\Psi|^{2}+\|\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}(A,\Psi)\|_{L^{2}(X_{\bullet})}^{2}+\frac{i}{4}\int_{X_{\bullet}}F_{A}\wedge*_{4}w_{r}\\
&\qquad\quad+\frac{ir}{4}\int_{X_{\bullet}}F_{A_{0}}\wedge*_{4}\nu+{\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{{}^{\prime}\mu_{r}}(X_{\bullet})(A,\Psi).\end{split}

(4.17)

The terms in the third and fourth line above are bounded in Steps
(1)-(3) below.

(1) The terms
-\int_{X_{\bullet}}\frac{R_{g}}{4}|\Psi|^{2}
,
\|\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}(A,\Psi)\|_{L^{2}(X_{\bullet})}^{2}
are bounded by the same general trick.
Observe that for any real
valued function
{\mathfrak{f}}
,

\begin{split}0&\leq\Big|\frac{i}{4}\rho(\mu^{+}_{r})-\big(1-\frac{{\mathfrak{f}}}{2|\Psi|^{2}}\big)(\Psi\Psi^{*})_{0}\Big|^{2}\\
&\leq\Big|\frac{i}{4}\rho(\mu^{+}_{r})-(\Psi\Psi^{*})_{0}\Big|^{2}+\frac{{\mathfrak{f}}^{2}}{4}-{\mathfrak{f}}|\Psi|^{2}+\frac{|{\mathfrak{f}}|}{8}|\mu_{r}|\end{split}

where
\Psi\neq 0
.
So, for general
\Psi
,

{\mathfrak{f}}|\Psi|^{2}\leq\Big|\frac{i}{4}\rho(\mu^{+}_{r})-(\Psi\Psi^{*})_{0}\Big|^{2}+\frac{{\mathfrak{f}}^{2}}{4}+\frac{|{\mathfrak{f}}|}{8}|\mu_{r}|.

(4.18)

Taking
{\mathfrak{f}}=\pm R_{g}
in (
4.18
) then gives us

\begin{split}&\Big|-\int_{X_{\bullet}}\frac{R_{g}}{4}|\Psi|^{2}\Big|\\
&\qquad\leq\frac{1}{4}\Big\|\frac{i}{4}\rho(\mu^{+}_{r})-(\Psi\Psi^{*})_{0}\Big\|^{2}_{L^{2}(X_{\bullet})}+\int_{X_{\bullet}}\frac{R_{g}^{2}}{16}+\int_{X_{\bullet}}\frac{|R_{g}|}{32}|\mu_{r}|\\
&\qquad\leq\frac{1}{4}\Big\|\frac{i}{4}\rho(\mu^{+}_{r})-(\Psi\Psi^{*})_{0}\Big\|^{2}_{L^{2}(X_{\bullet})}+\zeta_{g}|X_{\bullet}|+\zeta_{h}\,r(|X_{\bullet,m}|+|X_{\bullet}|_{1}).\end{split}

(4.19)

In the above,
\zeta_{g}
,
\zeta_{h}
are positive constants
depending only on the metric,
\varsigma_{w}
, and
\nu
. To go from the
first line to the second line, we also made use
of the well-known fact that
\nu
exponentially decays to
\nu_{i}
on the

Y_{i}
-end for each
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}
(cf. (
4.29
)).

Meanwhile by (
3.4
),

\begin{split}&\|\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}(A,\Psi)\|_{L^{2}(X_{\bullet})}^{2}\leq z\big(\|\Psi\|_{L^{2}(X_{\bullet,v})}^{2}+|X_{\bullet,v}|\big),\end{split}

where
z
is a positive constant depending only on the constant

\textsl{z}_{\mathfrak{p}}
and the metric on the vanishing ends. Taking

{\mathfrak{f}}=4z
in (
4.18
) then gives us

\begin{split}&\|\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}(A,\Psi)\|_{L^{2}(X_{\bullet})}^{2}\\
&\qquad\leq\frac{1}{4}\Big\|\frac{i}{4}\rho(\mu^{+}_{r})-(\Psi\Psi^{*})_{0}\Big\|^{2}_{L^{2}(X_{\bullet,v})}+z_{1}|X_{\bullet,v}|_{1}+z_{2}r\int_{X_{\bullet,v}}|\nu|\\
&\qquad\leq\frac{1}{4}\Big\|\frac{i}{4}\rho(\mu^{+}_{r})-(\Psi\Psi^{*})_{0}\Big\|^{2}_{L^{2}(X_{\bullet,v})}+z_{1}|X_{\bullet,v}|_{1}+z_{3}r|X_{\bullet,v}|_{1},\end{split}

(4.20)

noting that
|\nu|
decays exponentially to 0 on vanishing
ends. (For a more precise statement, see (
4.29
).) In
the above,
z_{i}
are positive constants that depend only on
\nu
,
the metrics, and the constants
\varsigma_{w}
,
\textsl{z}_{\mathfrak{p}}
.

(2) The term
\frac{i}{4}\int_{X_{\bullet}}F_{A}\wedge*_{4}w_{r}
in
(
4.17
) is bounded as follows.

\frac{i}{4}\int_{X_{\bullet}}F_{A}\wedge*_{4}w_{r}\leq\frac{1}{16}\|F_{A}\|_{L^{2}(X_{\bullet})}^{2}+\|w_{r}\|_{L^{2}(X_{\bullet})}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{16}\|F_{A}\|_{L^{2}(X_{\bullet})}^{2}+z_{4}|X_{\bullet}|,

(4.21)

where
z_{4}
depends only on the constant
\varsigma_{w}
.

(3) The term
\frac{ir}{4}\int_{X_{\bullet}}F_{A_{0}}\wedge*_{4}\nu
in
(
4.17
) is bounded as follows. Recall that by assumption,

F_{A_{0}}
is the pull-back of
F_{B_{0,i}}
on the ends

\hat{Y}_{i}
, and that
\wp_{i}=0
for vanishing ends. Thus,

\begin{split}&\frac{ir}{4}\int_{X_{\bullet}}F_{A_{0}}\wedge*_{4}\nu\\
&\qquad=\frac{ir}{4}\int_{X_{\bullet}\cap X_{c}}F_{A_{0}}\wedge*_{4}\nu+\sum_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}}r\wp_{i}|X_{\bullet}\cap\hat{Y}_{i}|\\
&\qquad\qquad\quad+r\sum_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}}\int_{X_{\bullet}\cap\hat{Y}_{i}}F_{A_{0}}\wedge*_{4}(\nu-\nu_{i})\\
&\qquad=\frac{ir}{4}\int_{X_{\bullet}\cap X_{c}}F_{A_{0}}\wedge*_{4}\nu+\sum_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}}r\wp_{i}|X_{\bullet}\cap\hat{Y}_{i}|+r\,\zeta_{5}\sum_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}}|X_{\bullet}\cap\hat{Y}_{i}|_{1}\\
&\qquad\leq r\,(z_{5}|X_{\bullet,m}|+\zeta^{\prime}_{5}).\end{split}

(4.22)

Here,
\zeta_{5}
and
\zeta_{5}^{\prime}
are positive constants depending only on the choice of

A_{0}
and
\nu
, while
z_{5}
is a positive constant depending only on the choice of

A_{0}
, the
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}
-structure, and
\nu
. In estimating the
last term in the second line above, we also used the exponential decay
of
\nu
on the ends of
X
. (Cf. (
4.29
) for a precise statement.)

Combining the bounds (
4.19
), (
4.20
), (
4.21
),
(
4.22
) with (
4.17
) and re-arranging, we arrive at
item (a) of (
4.16
). The inequality in item (b)
follows from item (a) when
X_{\bullet}\subset X^{{}^{\prime}a}
. By the additivity of

{\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{{}^{\prime}\mu_{r},\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}}
, it remains to verify the
inequality on the vanishing ends. The proof in this case is similar and is deferred
to the next subsection.

\Box

Recall the definition of
\wp_{i}
from Lemma
3.11
. For each
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}
, set

\wp_{i}^{+}:=\begin{cases}\wp_{i}+\frac{\pi}{2}\|*\nu_{i}\|_{T}&\text{when $\hat{Y}_{i}$ is a Morse
end};\\
0&\text{when $\hat{Y}_{i}$ is a vanishing end.}\end{cases}

Note that according to Proposition
3.15
and Remarks

3.16
,

\wp_{i}^{+}\geq\frac{\pi}{2}(\|*\nu_{i}\|_{T}+\zeta_{*\nu_{i}})
in our
context, with the right hand side,
\frac{\pi}{2}(\|*\nu_{i}\|_{T}+\zeta_{*\nu_{i}})
vanishing in many cases, such as in the
context of
[
LT
]
.

When
X_{\bullet}=\hat{Y}_{i,[l,L]}
, the bound
(
4.16
) (a) can be refined as follows.

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
(A,\Psi)=(A_{r},\Psi_{r})
be as in Lemma

4.7
.
When
X_{\bullet}=\hat{Y}_{i,[l,L]}
, we have:

\begin{split}&\frac{1}{8}\int_{X_{\bullet}}|F_{A}|^{2}+\int_{X_{\bullet}}|\nabla_{A}\Psi|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{X_{\bullet}}\Big|\frac{i}{4}\rho(\mu_{r}^{+})-(\Psi\Psi^{*})_{0}\Big|^{2}\\
&\qquad\quad\leq{\mathscr{E}}_{top}^{{}^{\prime}\mu_{r}}(X_{\bullet})(A,\Psi)+r\,(\wp_{i}^{+}+z_{i})|X_{\bullet}|+rz^{\prime}_{i}|X_{\bullet}|_{1}+\zeta^{\prime\prime}|X_{\bullet}|,\end{split}

(4.23)

where
z_{i}
is a non-negative constant depending only on the metric
on
Y_{i}
, and
z^{\prime}_{i}
is a positive constant that only depends on the
metric on
X
, the cohomology class of
\nu
, and the constant
\varsigma_{w}
in Assumption

3.4
. (In particular,
z_{i}
and
z^{\prime}_{i}
are independent of
r
and the
\mathop{\mathrm{Spin}}\nolimits^{c}

structure.) Moreover, the constant
z_{i}
has the following
properties:
z_{i}=0=\wp^{+}+z_{i}
when
\hat{Y}_{i}
is a vanishing
end. When
\hat{Y}_{i}
is a Morse end and
Y_{i}
is irreducible and atoroidal, then for any
\epsilon>0
, there exists a metric
on
Y_{i}
so that its corresponding
z_{i}
is less than
\epsilon
. Meanwhile,
\zeta^{\prime\prime}
is a positive constant that depends only
on the metric and
\varsigma_{w}
.

Proof.
To begin, recall
[
KM1
]
’s
Theorem 2. Reformulated as
[
BD
]
’s Theorem 5.4, it asserts that
when
Y_{i}
is irreducible, and atoroidal,

\|*\nu_{i}\|_{T}=\frac{1}{4\pi}\inf_{h}\{\|R_{h}\|_{h}\,\|\theta_{h}\|_{h}\},

(4.24)

where
h
ranges through all Riemannian metrics on
Y_{i}
. In the above,
\|\cdot\|_{h}

denotes
L^{2}
-norm with respect to
h
, and
\theta_{h}
denotes
the harmonic representative of the cohomology class
[*\nu_{i}]
with
respect to the metric
h
.
R_{h}
denotes the scalar curvature on

Y_{i}
corresponding to
h
. Recall also that
\|*\nu_{i}\|_{T}=\|[*\nu_{i}]\|_{T}
is the Thurston semi-norm of
[*\nu_{i}]\in H^{1}(Y_{i};{\mathbb{R}})
.

By assumption, over
\hat{Y}_{i}\simeq[0,\infty)\times Y_{i}
,
g
is
the product metric of the standard affine metric on
{\mathbb{R}}
and a
metric
g_{i}
on
Y_{i}
. Thus,
R_{g}|_{Y_{i:s}}=R_{g_{i}}
for any

s\geq 0
. Combined with the asymptotic behavior of

\nu
(described explicitly in (
4.27
) below), (
4.24
) implies that for any

s\geq 0
,

\|R_{g}\|_{L^{2}(Y_{i:s})}\|\nu|_{Y_{i:s}}\|_{L^{2}(Y_{i:s})}\leq 4\pi\|*\nu_{i}\|_{T}+z_{i}+{\mathfrak{z}}_{i}(s),

where
z_{i}=z_{Y_{i},\nu_{i}}:=\|R_{g_{i}}\|_{L^{2}(Y_{i})}\|\nu_{i}\|_{L^{2}(Y_{i})}-4\pi\|*\nu_{i}\|_{T}
. According to (
4.24
),
z_{i}
has the properties
asserted in the statement of the lemma.
{\mathfrak{z}}_{i}(s)
is
a positive function on
[0,\infty)
that depends only on
\nu

and exponentially decays to
0
as
s\to\infty
. (Explicitly, it
is a multiple of the right hand side of (
4.29
).)
Use the preceding inequality to replace (
4.19
) in this context by:

\begin{split}&\Big|-\int_{X_{\bullet}}\frac{R_{g}}{4}|\Psi|^{2}\Big|\\
&\quad\leq\frac{1}{4}\Big\|\frac{i}{4}\rho(\mu^{+}_{r})-(\Psi\Psi^{*})_{0}\Big\|^{2}_{L^{2}(X_{\bullet})}+\int_{X_{\bullet}}\frac{R_{g}^{2}}{16}+\int_{X_{\bullet}}\frac{|R_{g}|}{32}|\mu_{r}|\\
&\quad\leq\frac{1}{4}\Big\|\frac{i}{4}\rho(\mu^{+}_{r})-(\Psi\Psi^{*})_{0}\Big\|^{2}_{L^{2}(X_{\bullet})}+\zeta_{1}|X_{\bullet}|\\
&\qquad\qquad+\frac{r}{32}\big(\int_{l}^{L}ds\|R_{g}\|_{L^{2}(Y_{i:s})}\|\nu|_{Y_{i:s}}\|_{L^{2}(Y_{i:s})}\big)\\
&\quad\leq\frac{1}{4}\Big\|\frac{i}{4}\rho(\mu^{+}_{r})-(\Psi\Psi^{*})_{0}\Big\|^{2}_{L^{2}(X_{\bullet})}+\zeta_{1}|X_{\bullet}|\\
&\qquad\qquad+r\,\big(\frac{\pi}{2}\|*\nu_{i}\|_{T}+z_{i}\big)|X_{\bullet}|+r\zeta_{2}|X_{\bullet}|_{1},\end{split}

(4.25)

where
\zeta_{1}
,
\zeta_{2}
are positive constants depending only on
R_{g}

and
\varsigma_{w}
. (In particular, they are independent of
r
and

{\mathfrak{s}}_{i}
.)

Meanwhile, replace (
4.22
) in this context
by

\frac{ir}{4}\int_{X_{\bullet}}F_{A_{0}}\wedge*_{4}\nu\leq r\wp_{i}|X_{\bullet}|+r\zeta_{5}|X_{\bullet}|_{1},

(4.26)

\zeta_{5}
being a positive constant depending only on
\nu
.
Combine the bounds (
4.25
), (
4.21
),
(
4.26
) with (
4.17
) and re-arranging, we arrive at
(
4.23
).

\Box

Remarks 4.9
.

Since both
r
and
|X_{\bullet}|
can be arbitrarily large,
applying (
4.23
) to the case when
X={\mathbb{R}}\times Y
and

(A_{r},\Psi_{r})=(\hat{B}_{r},\hat{\Phi}_{r})
where
(B_{r},\Phi_{r})

is a sequence of solutions to the 3-dimensional Seiberg-Witten
equation
{\mathfrak{F}}_{\mu_{r}}(B_{r},\Phi_{r})=0
from Theorem

1.3
implies that
c_{1}({\mathfrak{s}})\cdot[*\nu]+\|*\nu\|_{T}\geq-\frac{2z_{Y,\nu}}{\pi}
when the
sequence of
(B_{r},\Phi_{r})
in Theorem
1.3
exists.
This bound is comparable to
[
KM
]
’s
Propositions 40.1.1 and 40.1.3, where certain non-existence results of
Seiberg-Witten solutions
are obtained under similar constraints on
c_{1}({\mathfrak{s}})
. In
particular, in the case when
b_{1}(Y)>0
, the
aforementioned propositions in
[
KM
]
were used to prove that for irreducible

Y
, the dual
Thurston polytope is the convex hull of the “Seiberg-Witten
basic classes” (cf.
[
KM1
]
Theorem 1 and
[
KM
]
Theorem
41.5.2).

4.3
Seiberg-Witten solutions as gradient flow lines of
time-dependent CSD functionals

We begin with some preliminary observations on the behavior of the form
\nu

on the ends, and introduce some notations along the way. In
particular, the previously imentioned exponential decay of

\nu
on the ends of
X
is made precise.

Consider an end
\hat{Y}_{i}

of
X
.
Write the harmonic 2-form

\nu
as

\nu=\underline{\nu}+ds\wedge*_{Y}{\mathfrak{v}}\ \quad\text{on
$\hat{Y}_{i}$,}

where
\underline{\nu}
,
{\mathfrak{v}}
are
s
-dependent 2-forms on
Y:=Y_{i}
,

*_{Y}
denotes the 3-dimensional Hodge dual on
Y_{i}
, and
s=\text{{\small s}}_{i}
. The harmonicity of
\nu
implies
that both
\underline{\nu}
and
{\mathfrak{v}}
are closed 2-forms.
Moreover,
{\mathfrak{v}}
and
\underline{\nu}-\nu_{i}
are
s
-dependent
exact 2-forms on
Y_{i:s}=\text{{\small s}}_{i}^{-1}(s)
, and satisfy

\partial_{s}(\underline{\nu}-\nu_{i})=-d_{Y}*_{Y}{\mathfrak{v}}\quad\text{and}\quad\partial_{s}{\mathfrak{v}}=-d_{Y}*_{Y}(\underline{\nu}-\nu_{i}).

(4.27)

Here,
d_{Y}
denotes the
exterior derivative on
Y_{i}
.

Let

\xi_{\nu}:=\underline{\nu}-\nu_{i}+{\mathfrak{v}};\quad\xi_{\nu}^{\prime}:=\underline{\nu}-\nu_{i}-{\mathfrak{v}},

so that in this notation,
\nu_{+}=\nu_{i}+\xi_{\nu}
on

\hat{Y}_{i}
.
\xi_{\nu}
and
\xi_{\nu}^{\prime}
are exact
2-forms satisfying

\partial_{s}\xi_{\nu}=-d_{Y}*_{Y}\xi_{\nu};\quad\partial_{s}\xi_{\nu}^{\prime}=d_{Y}*_{Y}\xi_{\nu}^{\prime}.

(4.28)

As
d_{Y}*_{Y}
is a self-dual operator on
the space of
L^{2}
exact 2-forms and
Y_{i}
is closed, it has
discrete real eigenvalues
\{\kappa_{j}^{(i)}\}_{j}
with

\min_{j}|\kappa_{j}^{(i)}|=:\kappa_{i}>0
. Thus, there exists a constant
\zeta_{i}>0

such that

\begin{split}&\|\xi_{\nu}\|_{C^{k}(Y_{i:s})}+\|\underline{\nu}-\nu_{i}\|_{C^{k}(Y_{i:s})}+\|{\mathfrak{v}}\|_{C^{k}(Y_{i:s})}\leq\zeta_{i}e^{-\kappa_{i}s};\quad\text{and via (\ref {DE:xi}), }\\
&\|\nu-\pi_{2}^{*}\nu_{i}\|_{C^{k}(Y_{i,s})}\leq\,\zeta_{i}e^{-\kappa_{i}s}.\end{split}

(4.29)

The (
s
-dependent) form
\nu_{+}

on
Y_{i}
, introduced previously in Section
4.1
, is
expressed in this part’s notation as:

\begin{split}\nu_{+}&=\underline{\nu}+{\mathfrak{v}}.\\
&=\nu_{i}+\xi_{\nu}.\end{split}

Returning now to the task of interpreting of Seiberg-Witten solutions
on the ends of
X
, we choose to work in a gauge such that
(A,\Psi)|_{\hat{Y}_{i}}
is in the temporal gauge over each end
\hat{Y}_{i}

of
X
. Namely, over the ends
X-X_{c}
we can write

(A,\Psi)=(\partial_{s}+B,\Phi),

(4.30)

where
(B(s),\Phi(s))
,
s\geq 0
, is a path in
\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits(Y_{i})\times\Gamma({\mathbb{S}}_{Y_{i}})
. Recall that the reference connection
A_{0}
is
chosen such that
over the ends of
X
,
A_{0}=\partial_{s}+B_{0}
, where

B_{0}=B_{0,i}
on the
Y_{i}
-end.

Restricting to an end
\hat{Y}_{i}
of
X
,
the 4-dimensional
Seiberg-Witten equation
{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mu_{r},\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}}(A,\Psi)=0

may be re-expressed in terms of
(B(s),\Phi(s))
over
\hat{Y}_{i}

as

\big(\frac{1}{2}\partial_{s}B,\partial_{s}\Phi\big)+\mathfrak{F}_{\mu_{+},\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}}(B,\Phi)=0,

(4.31)

where
\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}=\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}(s)=\chi_{i}(s){\mathfrak{q}}_{i}+\lambda_{i}(s){\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}_{i}
is regarded as a path of tame
perturbations as in (
3.6
).

Square both sides of the previous equation and use integration by parts plus
(
4.27
) to get:

\begin{split}&\|\partial_{s}B\|_{L^{2}(\hat{Y}_{[l,L]})}^{2}+4\|\partial_{s}\Phi\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\hat{Y}_{[l,L]})}+4\|\mathfrak{F}_{\mu_{+},\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}}(B,\Phi)\|_{L^{2}(\hat{Y}_{[l,L]})}^{2}\\
&\quad\quad=8\operatorname{CSD}_{\mu_{+}(l),\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}(l)}^{Y}(B,\Phi)|_{Y_{l}}-8\operatorname{CSD}_{\mu_{+}(L),\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}(L)}^{Y}(B,\Phi)|_{Y_{L}}\\
&\qquad\quad-ir\int_{\hat{Y}_{[l,L]}}ds\,(*_{Y}\xi_{\nu})\wedge(F_{B}-F_{B_{0}})-8\int_{\hat{Y}_{[l,L]}}(\partial_{s}f_{\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}(s)})(B,\Phi)\,ds\end{split}

(4.32)

for any interval
[l,L]\subset[0,\infty]
. (Here
Y
stands for
any of the ending 3-manifolds
Y_{i}
.)

In what follows we use the preceding formula in two ways: (i) In the
remainder of this subsection, we use the upper
bound on the right hand side of (
4.32
) to get an upper bound on the square terms
on the left hand side; in particular, this leads to the completion of
the proof of
(
4.16
) (b). (ii) In the subsequent subsection, we use the non-negativity of the left hand
side to obtain a lower bound of the first two terms on the right hand
side, and hence a lower
bound on
{\mathscr{E}}^{{}^{\prime}\mu_{r},\hat{{\mathfrak{p}}}}_{top}(\hat{Y}_{[l,L]})(A,\Psi)
,
generalizing the positivity results in the cylindrical case mentioned
towards the end of Section
4.1
.

Verifying the remaining case of (
4.16
) (b).

As observed in the previous subsection, it remains to verify the claimed inequality for
the case when

X_{\bullet}
lies in vanishing ends.
Nevertheless, assume for the moment that
X_{\bullet}=\hat{Y}_{[l,L]}
,

\hat{Y}=\hat{Y}_{i}
is a general end of
X_{\bullet}
. (Either
vanishing or Morse.)

To begin, recall from Assumption
3.4
(3) and use the
3-dimensional Weitzenböck formula (
3.14
) to get
