i_{r^{\prime},r}
,

i_{\infty}
are given by

\begin{split}&i_{r^{\prime},r}=\\
&\begin{cases}\operatorname{c}_{\{\underline{{\mathfrak{h}}}\,(\tilde{o}_{Y_{i}}([B^{i}_{0,r^{\prime}}],[B^{i}_{0,r}]))\}_{i}}&\text{\footnotesize in 1st CD;}\\
\text{the map $h\mapsto\underline{{\mathfrak{h}}}\,(\tilde{o}_{Y_{i}}([B^{i}_{k-1,r}],[B^{i}_{k-1,r^{\prime}}])*h*\underline{{\mathfrak{h}}}\,(\tilde{o}_{Y_{i}}([B^{i}_{k,r^{\prime}}],[B^{i}_{k,r}]))$}&\text{\footnotesize in 2nd CD;}\end{cases}\end{split}

(7.37)

\begin{split}&i_{\infty}=\\
&\begin{cases}\operatorname{c}_{\{\underline{{\mathfrak{h}}}\,(\tilde{o}_{Y_{i}}([B^{i}_{0,r}],[B^{i}_{0,\infty}]))\}_{i}}&\text{\footnotesize in 1st CD;}\\
\text{the map $h\mapsto\underline{{\mathfrak{h}}}\,(\tilde{o}_{Y_{i}}([B^{i}_{k-1,\infty}],[B^{i}_{k-1,r}])*h*\underline{{\mathfrak{h}}}\,(\tilde{o}_{Y_{i}}([B^{i}_{k,r}],[B^{i}_{k,\infty}]))$}&\text{\footnotesize in 2nd CD.}\end{cases}\end{split}

(7.38)

(In the above, “CD” stands for
“commutative diagram”.)

Recall that the relative homology class of
{\mathfrak{C}}
is defined to be
the composition

[{\mathfrak{C}}]=\operatorname{c}_{\{[{\mathfrak{C}}_{i}]\}_{i}\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}}([{\bf C}_{0}]),

and

[{\mathfrak{C}}_{i}]:=\begin{cases}[\tilde{C}_{i,{\mathfrak{K}}_{i}}]*\cdots*[\tilde{C}_{i,2}]*[\tilde{C}_{i,1}]&\text{when $i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}^{\prime}_{m}$;}\\
0\in H_{2}(Y_{i};{\mathbb{Z}})&\text{when ${\mathfrak{K}}_{i}=0$.}\end{cases}

Denote by
\text{{\scriptsize h}}_{r}
the relative homotopy class of
(A_{r},\Psi_{r})
and recall that by assumption, versions of

\text{{\scriptsize h}}_{r}
corresponding to different, sufficiently large
r

are identified via the canonical isomorphisms in
Lemma
3.19
and collectively
denoted as
h
. To compare
{\mathfrak{h}}(\operatorname{\text{{\scriptsize h}}})
with
[{\mathfrak{C}}]
, first fix
r>R_{2}
and
identify
\operatorname{\text{{\scriptsize h}}}=\operatorname{\text{{\scriptsize h}}}_{r}
with the relative homotopy class of
(A_{r},\Psi_{r})
in
\pi_{0}{\mathscr{B}}_{X}(\{{\mathfrak{c}}_{i,r}\}_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}})
. We shall express

\operatorname{\text{{\scriptsize h}}}_{r}
as a composition,

\operatorname{\text{{\scriptsize h}}}_{r}=\operatorname{c}_{\{\operatorname{\text{{\scriptsize h}}}_{i,r}\}_{i}}(\operatorname{\text{{\scriptsize h}}}_{0,r}),\quad

(7.39)

where
\operatorname{\text{{\scriptsize h}}}_{0,r}\in\pi_{0}{\mathscr{B}}_{X}(\{{\mathfrak{c}}^{i}_{0,r}\}_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}})
,
and

\begin{split}&\operatorname{\text{{\scriptsize h}}}_{i,r}=\\
&\begin{cases}\operatorname{\text{{\scriptsize h}}}^{r}_{i,{\mathfrak{K}}_{i}},*\cdots*\operatorname{\text{{\scriptsize h}}}^{r}_{i,2}*\operatorname{\text{{\scriptsize h}}}^{r}_{i,1},\quad\operatorname{\text{{\scriptsize h}}}^{r}_{i,k}\in\pi_{Y_{i}}({\mathfrak{c}}^{i}_{k-1,r},{\mathfrak{c}}^{i}_{k,r})&\text{when
$i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}^{\prime}_{m}$};\\
0\in H_{2}(Y;{\mathbb{Z}})\simeq\pi_{Y_{i}}({\mathfrak{c}}_{i,r},{\mathfrak{c}}_{i,r})&\text{when $i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}-{\mathfrak{Y}}^{\prime}_{m}$},\\
\end{cases}\end{split}

(7.40)

such that

{\mathfrak{h}}(\operatorname{\text{{\scriptsize h}}}_{0,r})=[{\bf C}_{0}]
and
{\mathfrak{h}}(\operatorname{\text{{\scriptsize h}}}^{r}_{i,k})=[{\bf C}_{i,k}]
for all
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}^{\prime}_{m}
and
k\in\{1,\ldots,{\mathfrak{K}}_{i}\}
.

(7.41)

Once (
7.39
), (
7.40
), (
7.41
) are
established, modulo Lemma

3.23
(b), the proof of Item (c) in the statement of the
theorem follows directly from the naturality of
{\mathfrak{h}}
under concatenation/composition maps.

To define
\operatorname{\text{{\scriptsize h}}}_{0,r}
,
\operatorname{\text{{\scriptsize h}}}^{r}_{i,k}
, we begin with a modification of

[(A_{r},\Psi_{r})]\in{\mathscr{B}}_{X}(\{{\mathfrak{c}}_{i,r}\}_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}})
.
Introduce the cutoff function
\lambda(s):=\chi(2s)\chi(-2s)
and let
(\hat{B}^{i}_{k,r},\hat{\Phi}^{i}_{k,r})
denotes the pull back of
(B^{i}_{k,r},\Phi^{i}_{k,r})
to

\hat{Y}_{i}
. Set

\begin{split}\tilde{A}_{r}:&=A^{\prime}_{r}+\sum_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}^{\prime}_{m}}\sum_{k=0}^{{\mathfrak{K}}_{i}-1}\big(\lambda(s-l^{i}_{k,r})(\hat{B}^{i}_{k,r}-A^{\prime}_{r})\big),\\
\tilde{\Psi}_{r}:&=\Psi^{\prime}_{r}+\sum_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}^{\prime}_{m}}\sum_{k=0}^{{\mathfrak{K}}_{i}-1}\big(\lambda(s-l^{i}_{k,r})(\hat{\Phi}^{i}_{k,r}-\Psi^{\prime}_{r})\big).\end{split}

Then
[(\tilde{A}_{r},\tilde{\Psi}_{r})]
represents the same relative homotopy class
as
[(A_{r},\Psi_{r})]
, namely
\operatorname{\text{{\scriptsize h}}}_{r}
. Now, we define
\operatorname{\text{{\scriptsize h}}}_{0,r}
to
be the relative homotopy class of
[(A_{0,r},\Psi_{0,r})]
, which
is in turned given as follows:
Define
(A_{0,r},\Psi_{0,r})\in\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits(X)\times\Gamma({\mathbb{S}}_{X}^{+})

by setting
(A_{0,r},\Psi_{0,r})=(\tilde{A}_{r},\tilde{\Psi}_{r})
over

X^{+}_{\vec{L}_{T}(r)-\vec{3/2}}:=X^{{}^{\prime}a}_{\vec{L}_{T}(r)-\vec{3/2}}\cup(X-X^{{}^{\prime}a})
, and then extending over
X
by the
s
-independent
configurations
(\hat{B}^{i}_{0,r},\hat{\Phi}^{i}_{0,r})
,
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}^{\prime}
, over the complement of

X^{+}_{\vec{L}_{T}(r)-\vec{3/2}}
.

Similarly, when
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}^{\prime}_{m}
,
\operatorname{\text{{\scriptsize h}}}^{r}_{i,k}
is defined to be the relative homotopy
class of
[(A_{k,r}^{i},\Psi_{k,r}^{i})]
, where
(A_{k,r}^{i},\Psi_{k,r}^{i})\in\in\mathop{\mathrm{Conn}}\nolimits({\mathbb{R}}\times Y_{i})\times\Gamma({\mathbb{S}}_{{\mathbb{R}}\times Y_{i}}^{+})
is given by setting

(A_{k,r}^{i},\Psi_{k,r}^{i})=\tau_{-L^{i}_{k,r}}(\tilde{A}_{r},\tilde{\Psi}_{r})
over
J^{i}_{k,r}\times Y_{i}
, and then extending over

{\mathbb{R}}\times Y_{i}
by the
s
-independent
configurations over the complement, namely
\hat{B}^{i}_{k-1,r}
and
\hat{B}^{i}_{k,r}
respectively over the

-\infty
-end and the
+\infty
-end. With
\operatorname{\text{{\scriptsize h}}}^{r}_{i,k}
,

\operatorname{\text{{\scriptsize h}}}_{0,r}
so defined, the identities in
(
7.39
), (
7.40
) hold by construction.

Let
\{\mu_{j}\}_{j}
be a set of closed 2-forms on
X
that
represents a basis of
H^{2}(X;{\mathbb{R}})
. We choose

\mu_{j}
so that for every
j
,

•

\mu_{j}|_{\hat{Y}_{i}}=\pi_{2}^{*}\mu_{i,j}
for a closed
2-forn
\mu_{i,j}
on
Y_{i}
for all
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}
.

•

When
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{v}
,
\mu_{i,j}=0
. This is possible because
of the condition (
1.6
).

•

When
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}
,
\mu_{i,j}
is supported on the
complement of

\bigcup_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in{\mathbb{P}}({\mathfrak{s}}_{i})}\gamma\subset Y_{i}
. This is possible because
{\mathbb{P}}({\mathfrak{s}}_{i})
consists of
finitely many elements, and the closure of each
\boldsymbol{\gamma}
is a compact
1-manifold with (possibly empty) boundary.

To verify the first identity in (
7.41
), it suffices to verify
that
for every
j
, the pairing

\big\langle[\mu_{j}],{\mathfrak{h}}^{\prime}(\operatorname{\text{{\scriptsize h}}}_{0,r})-(i_{\infty})^{-1}[{\bf C}_{0}]\big\rangle=0,\quad r>R_{2}.

(7.42)

Given (
7.38
), the left hand side of the preceding expression equals

\begin{split}&\int_{-1/2}^{1/2}\Big(\int_{X^{{}^{\prime}a}_{\vec{L}_{T}(r)-\vec{3/2}+\vec{t}}}\frac{iF_{A_{0,r}^{E}}}{2\pi}\wedge\mu_{j}-\int_{\tilde{C}_{0}|_{X^{{}^{\prime}a}_{\vec{L}_{T}(r)-\vec{3/2}+\vec{t}}}}\mu_{j}\\
&\,\,-\sum_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}^{\prime}_{m}}\Big(\int_{\tilde{C}_{0}|_{\hat{Y}_{i,L_{T}(r)-3/2+t}}}\pi_{2}^{*}\mu_{i,j}-\lim_{r^{\prime}\to\infty}\int_{Y_{i}}\big(\frac{i}{2\pi}(B_{0,r^{\prime}}^{E}-B_{0,r}^{E})\big)\wedge\mu_{i,j}\Big)\Big)dt\\
&=\int_{-1/2}^{1/2}\Big(\int_{X^{{}^{\prime}a}_{\vec{L}_{T}(r)-\vec{3/2}+\vec{t}}}\frac{iF_{A_{r}^{E}}}{2\pi}\wedge\mu_{j}-\int_{\tilde{C}_{0}|_{X^{{}^{\prime}a}_{\vec{L}_{T}(r)-\vec{3/2}+\vec{t}}}}\mu_{j}\\
&\,\,-\sum_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}^{\prime}_{m}}\Big(\int_{\tilde{C}_{0}|_{\hat{Y}_{i,L_{T}(r)-3/2+t}}}\pi_{2}^{*}\mu_{i,j}-\lim_{r^{\prime}\to\infty}\int_{Y_{i}}\big(\frac{i}{2\pi}(B_{0,r^{\prime}}^{E}-B_{0,r}^{E})\big)\wedge\mu_{i,j}\Big)\Big)dt,\end{split}

and is independent of
r
. However, according to (
7.16
),
the fact that
{\bf C}_{0}
is asymptotic to a t-orbit, and the
convergence of
[B_{0,r}]
in current topology, the preceding
expression converges to 0 as
r\to\infty
. We have thus confirmed
the first identity in (
7.41
).

The second identity in (
7.41
) for each given
i,k
is verified similarly, with the
roles of
A_{0,r}
,
{\bf C}_{0}
,
X^{{}^{\prime}a}_{\vec{L}_{T}(r)-\vec{3/2}}

above respectively replaced by
A_{k,r}^{i}
,
{\bf C}_{i,k}
, and

J^{i}_{k,r}\times Y_{i}
, and with the role of (
7.16
) in
the preceding argument now played by
(
7.31
).
\Box

Finally, we prove Lemma
3.23
(b).

Proof of Lemma
3.23
(b).

Recall that the
currents
\{\tilde{\gamma}_{i}\}_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}}
are integral, and

{\mathscr{H}}\big((X^{{}^{\prime}a},\nu),\{\tilde{\gamma}_{i}\}_{i}\big)
embeds in
{\mathscr{H}}^{\mathbb{R}}\big((X^{{}^{\prime}a},\nu),\{\tilde{\gamma}_{i}\}_{i}\big)
as an orbit of
the
{\mathscr{H}}_{X}
-action. The map

{\mathfrak{h}}=i_{\infty}\circ{\mathfrak{h}}^{\prime}
maps

\pi_{0}{\mathscr{B}}_{X}(\{{\mathfrak{c}}_{i,r}\}_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}})\simeq\pi_{0}{\mathscr{B}}_{X^{{}^{\prime}a}}(\{{\mathfrak{c}}_{i,r}\}_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}})
to another orbit of the

{\mathscr{H}}_{X}
-action in
{\mathscr{H}}^{\mathbb{R}}\big((X^{{}^{\prime}a},\nu),\{\tilde{\gamma}_{i}\}_{i}\big)
. We claim that these two orbits are identical. To see
this, note that by Theorem
1.10
(a), there is a
subsequence, also denoted
\{(A_{r},\Psi_{r})\}_{r}
, which weakly converges to a chain of
t-curves,
{\mathfrak{C}}
. The associated current
\tilde{{\mathfrak{C}}}
is integral,
and has relative homology class in
{\mathscr{H}}\big((X^{{}^{\prime}a},\nu),\{\tilde{\gamma}_{i}\}_{i}\big)\subset{\mathscr{H}}^{\mathbb{R}}\big((X^{{}^{\prime}a},\nu),\{\tilde{\gamma}_{i}\}_{i}\big)
. Meanwhile,
[{\mathfrak{C}}]={\mathfrak{h}}(\operatorname{\text{{\scriptsize h}}})
is in
the image of
{\mathfrak{h}}
. This implies that the
{\mathscr{H}}_{X}
-orbits

{\mathscr{H}}\big((X^{{}^{\prime}a},\nu),\{\tilde{\gamma}_{i}\}_{i}\big)
and
{\mathfrak{h}}\,(\pi_{0}{\mathscr{B}}_{X}(\{{\mathfrak{c}}_{i,r}\}_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}}))
in
{\mathscr{H}}^{\mathbb{R}}\big((X^{{}^{\prime}a},\nu),\{\tilde{\gamma}_{i}\}_{i}\big)
are identical.
\Box

Proof of Proposition
1.13
, the
non-cylindrical case.

Adopt the
notations from the proof of Theorem
1.10
above.
By the assumption (
1.8
),
C_{0}\cap P
,
C_{i,k}\cap({\mathbb{R}}\times\textsl{p}_{i})

\forall i,k
all consist of finitely many points. Thus, there exists an
R_{3}\geq R_{2}
such
that for all
r\geq R_{3}
:

•

C_{0}\cap P
lies in the interior of

X^{{}^{\prime}a}_{\vec{L}_{T}(r)-\vec{3}}
;

•

C_{i,k}\cap({\mathbb{R}}\times\textsl{p}_{i})
lies in the interior of
J^{i}_{k,r}\times Y_{i}
for all

i
,
k
.

•

For all
i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}
,
\operatorname{\text{{\small d}}}_{\hat{Y}_{i,I_{*}}}(P,{\mathbb{R}}\times\textsl{p}_{i})<\epsilon/4
for all

I_{*}\subset[L_{T}(r)-5/2,\infty)
with length
|I_{*}|=2
.

Let
\tilde{P}\subset X^{{}^{\prime}a}
be a subvariety such that:

•

\tilde{P}
agrees
with
P
over
X^{{}^{\prime}a}_{\vec{L}_{T}(r)-\vec{3}}
;

•

it agrees with

{\mathbb{R}}\times\textsl{p}_{i}
over
\hat{Y}_{i,L_{T}(r)-5/2}

\forall i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}
, and

•

\operatorname{\text{{\small d}}}_{\hat{Y}_{Y_{i,[L_{T}(r)-3,L_{T}(r)-5/2]}}}(P,\tilde{P})+\operatorname{\text{{\small d}}}_{\hat{Y}_{Y_{i,[L_{T}(r)-3,L_{T}(r)-5/2]}}}(\tilde{P},{\mathbb{R}}\times\textsl{p}_{i})\leq\epsilon
.

•

P-\tilde{P}
is a boundary.

Write

\begin{split}\int_{P}\frac{i}{2\pi}F_{A_{r}^{E}}&=\int_{\tilde{P}\cap X^{{}^{\prime}a}_{\vec{L}_{T}(r)-\vec{\frac{5}{2}}}}\frac{i}{2\pi}F_{A_{r}^{E}}+\sum_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}^{\prime}_{m}}\sum_{k=1}^{{\mathfrak{K}}_{i}}\int_{\tilde{P}\cap\hat{Y}_{i,J^{{}^{\prime}i}_{k,r}}}\frac{i}{2\pi}F_{A_{r}^{E}}\\
\end{split}

Taking the
r\to\infty
limit, by (
7.16
) and
(
7.31
) we then have

\lim_{r\to\infty}\int_{P}\frac{i}{2\pi}F_{A_{r}^{E}}=\#\,(P\cap{\bf C}_{0})+\sum_{i\in{\mathfrak{Y}}^{\prime}_{m}}\sum_{k=1}^{{\mathfrak{K}}_{i}}\#\,\big(({\mathbb{R}}\times\textsl{p}_{i})\cap{\bf C}_{i,k}\big).

Now observe that each term on the right hand side is a non-negative
integer, since
P
,
{\mathbb{R}}\times\textsl{p}_{i}
,
C_{0}
,
C_{i,k}
are
all pseudo-holomorphic.

\Box

7.4
Proving Theorem
1.10
and Proposition

1.13
, the cylindrical case

The proofs require only minor modifications of the proofs in the
non-cylindrical case.

Proof of Theorem
1.10
, the cylindrical
case.

Begin as with the proof of the noncylindrical case in the previous
subsection: Rename the t-curve

{\bf C}
in the statement of Theorem
1.8
as

{\bf C}_{0}
. That is,
\{(A_{r},\Psi_{r})\}_{r\in\Gamma_{0}}

t-converges to
{\bf C}_{0}
over
X={\mathbb{R}}\times Y
.
As noted in Remark
1.7
, in this case we may regard
X
as an MCE with two ends:
{\mathfrak{Y}}={\mathfrak{Y}}_{m}=\{\pm\}
consists two elements,
with the elements
+/-
labelling respectively the

+\infty
/
-\infty
-ends; their corresponding ending
3-manifolds are
Y_{\pm}=\pm Y
. Rerunning the arguments in the
previous subsection, we get a subsequence
\Gamma^{\prime}
and two chains of
t-curves
{\mathfrak{C}}_{\pm}
on
{\mathbb{R}}\times(\pm Y)
, where
{\mathfrak{C}}_{\pm}
has

-\infty
-limit
\pm\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{0,\pm}
and
+\infty
-limit

\pm\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\pm}
. Let
\iota\colon\thinspace{\mathbb{R}}\times(-Y)\to{\mathbb{R}}\times Y
denote the orientation preserving map
(s,x)\mapsto(-s,x)
. Write

\begin{split}&{\mathfrak{C}}=\\
&\begin{cases}\{\iota({\bf C}_{-,{\mathfrak{K}}_{-}}),\iota({\bf C}_{-,{\mathfrak{K}}_{-}-1}),\ldots,\iota({\bf C}_{-,1}),{\bf C}_{0},{\bf C}_{+,1},\ldots,{\bf C}_{+,{\mathfrak{K}}_{+}}\}&\text{\footnotesize when ${\mathfrak{K}}_{-}>0$;
${\mathfrak{K}}_{+}>0$};\\
\{\iota({\bf C}_{-,{\mathfrak{K}}_{-}}),\iota({\bf C}_{-,{\mathfrak{K}}_{-}-1}),\ldots,\iota({\bf C}_{-,1}),{\bf C}_{0}\}&\text{\footnotesize when ${\mathfrak{K}}_{-}>0$;
${\mathfrak{K}}_{+}=0$};\\
\{{\bf C}_{0},{\bf C}_{+,1},\ldots,{\bf C}_{+,{\mathfrak{K}}_{+}}\}&\text{\footnotesize when ${\mathfrak{K}}_{-}=0$;
${\mathfrak{K}}_{+}>0$};\\
\{{\bf C}_{0}\}&\text{\footnotesize when ${\mathfrak{K}}_{-}=0$; ${\mathfrak{K}}_{+}=0$}\end{cases}\end{split}

if
{\bf C}_{0}
is non-constant; and when
{\bf C}_{0}
is constant, let

\begin{split}&{\mathfrak{C}}=\\
&\begin{cases}\{\iota({\bf C}_{-,{\mathfrak{K}}_{-}}),\iota({\bf C}_{-,{\mathfrak{K}}_{-}-1}),\ldots,\iota({\bf C}_{-,1}){\bf C}_{+,1},\ldots,{\bf C}_{+,{\mathfrak{K}}_{+}}\}&\text{when ${\mathfrak{K}}_{-}>0$;
${\mathfrak{K}}_{+}>0$};\\
\{\iota({\bf C}_{-,{\mathfrak{K}}_{-}}),\iota({\bf C}_{-,{\mathfrak{K}}_{-}-1}),\ldots,\iota({\bf C}_{-,1})\}&\text{when ${\mathfrak{K}}_{-}>0$;
${\mathfrak{K}}_{+}=0$};\\
\{{\bf C}_{+,1},\ldots,{\bf C}_{+,{\mathfrak{K}}_{+}}\}&\text{when ${\mathfrak{K}}_{-}=0$;
${\mathfrak{K}}_{+}>0$}.\\
\end{cases}\end{split}

Lastly, when
{\bf C}_{0}
is constant and
{\mathfrak{K}}_{\pm}=0
, let
{\mathfrak{C}}

be the 0-component chain of t-curves with a single
rest point
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+}
. The arguments from the previous
subsections then shows that
\{(A_{r},\Psi_{r})\}_{r\in\Gamma^{\prime}}
weakly t-converges to

{\mathfrak{C}}
, which have the properties described in Items (b)-(d) in the
statement of the theorem. (Note that when
(Y,\nu)
is
cylindrical, the conditions in Items (c) are (d) are both met.)
The case when
{\bf C}_{0}
is constant and
{\mathfrak{K}}_{\pm}=0
occurs only when
\{(A_{r},\Psi_{r})\}_{r\in\Gamma_{0}}
is such that
there exists
r_{0}>1
, such that
\forall r\geq r_{0}
,
(A_{r},\Psi_{r})
is constant. This means that
(A_{r},\Psi_{r})=(\hat{B}_{r},\hat{\Phi}_{r})
, where
(B_{r},\Phi_{r})
strongly
t-converges to
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-}=\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+}
by assumption. In
this case,
{\mathfrak{C}}
is the 0-component chain of t-curves with a single
rest point
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-}=\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+}
.
\Box

Proof of Proposition

1.13
, the cylindrical case.

Let
{\bf C}_{0}
,

{\mathfrak{C}}
be as in the proof of the cylindrical case of Theorem

1.10
above. Then simply repeat
the arguments in the proof for the noncylindrical case of Proposition

1.13
in the previous subsection.
\Box
